#2805. AMD vs. Intel: Which Is the Better AI Chip Stock to Own for the Next 3 Years Following the Semiconductor Crash?

AMD and Intel stocks have both seen major pullbacks in the past month.

Chip stocks have been absolutely pummeled recently, with Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +11.04%) and Intel (INTC +7.71%) two of the biggest losers over the past month. Meanwhile, the strong Democratic showing in the midterm elections put the artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out on notice.

Despite the political shift, I wouldn't expect there to be a major impact on overall AI infrastructure growth in the coming years, with hyperscalers being able to navigate the current environment and shifting some projects overseas. However, between AMD and Intel, I think the clear winner is likely to be AMD. Here's why.

Advanced Micro Devices has been one of the hardest-hit stocks in the semiconductor pullback. However, the company is riding two powerful waves in inference and agentic AI that are just starting to kick in, and its new Helios rack solution could be a game changer.

Helios is AMD's turnkey AI infrastructure solution that incorporates its graphics processing units (GPUs), central processing units (CPUs), and networking components into a single system. The system contains 72 GPUs and 18 CPUs per rack and is designed to provide the "lowest cost per token" to help reduce overall computing costs. The system has already started being rolled out to key customers, including OpenAI, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, Anthropic, and Oracle, and OEM (original equipment manufacturer) and ODM (original design manufacturer) partners will also soon begin to ship Helios.

AMD has two large deals in place with OpenAI and Meta Platforms for 6 gigawatts of compute capacity each. With each Helios system costing between $5 million and $5.5 million and consuming between 225 kilowatts and 245 kilowatts of power, each deal would be worth approximately $120 billion. Meanwhile, Anthropic's 2-gigawatt deal would be worth around $40 billion, with Microsoft and Oracle as smaller customers.

At the same time, the company is seeing huge demand for its server CPUs, given that these chips are needed to help manage AI agents. Its CPUs are helping run Meta's new Muse AI agents, and the company sees server CPUs becoming a $220 billion market by 2030.

As a result, AMD is projecting growth to soar next year, with it recently forecasting revenue to surge 80% in 2027. The pullback has dropped the stock to a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of around 9 times its 2030 analyst estimates, giving it plenty of room to run the next three years.

While AMD has been the victim of a broader semiconductor stock sell-off, much of Intel's wounds are self-inflicted. This is due to the company's still-outsized exposure to the PC market, which is seeing weak demand as high memory costs have caused computer makers to raise prices, slowing demand.

Intel's client computing and physical AI group (CCPG) remains its largest segment, and revenue for the unit fell 5% last quarter. However, the company is expecting units to drop 10% in 2027 after a 12% decline this year, which will also lead to a large inventory correction that will significantly impact the next two quarters.

Meanwhile, Intel's foundry business continues to produce large losses, including a $1.9 billion operating loss in Q3. The company is struggling to find external customers, in part due to technology and in part due to conflicts of interest, and if the data center build-out shifts overseas, this will likely sting it even more, given its strong domestic footprint.

Intel is the market leader in server CPUs, and like AMD, it's been seeing strong growth in this area. However, the company has been consistently losing shares to AMD and custom CPUs. This is only expected to continue in the years ahead, especially as the company has no turnkey solution like Nvidia and AMD.

As such, I think the stock could continue to underperform over the next three years.

Geoffrey Seiler has positions in Advanced Micro Devices and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Advanced Micro Devices, Intel, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, Nvidia, and Oracle. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2803. Knicks Week Preview: Nov. 8-14, 2026

The Knicks have bobbed over .500 for the first time this season, thanks to yesterday's return to form against the Cleveland Cavaliers. Over the upcoming week, our heroes will get a second whack at the Ohio Players, plus two beatable opponents. Let's take a peek at the schedule, shall we?

The Bucks are in a funky funk. Sitting last in the east, they pull into town having lost five straight and seven of their eight games this season. This has not been an auspicious start for new coach Taylor Jenkins. Over the summer, they lost Giannis Antetokounmpo and got younger, which has resulted in struggles at both ends of the floor. Tuesday's game will be their first meeting with the Knicks this season. The last time they tussled, back on February 27, was a 127-98 New York win in Milwaukee.

Our heroes should have many chances to run against Milwaukee and plenty of mismatches to pick on. The Bucks don't necessarily lack talent—Tyler Herro (acquired in the Giannis trade) leads the team at 22.3 points per game—just hope.

The Cavs just got a good look at what New York does well, and boy did it make their eyes water. Four nights after beating the Cavs 109-97 in Cleveland, the two rivals square up again at the Garden. Brunson scored 31 in the first tilt, Karl-Anthony Towns added 29 points and 13 rebounds, and New York led by 19 in the third. Donovan Mitchell found his way to 37, fighting to the end; James Harden died of old age; and Evan Mobley melted into a sweaty puddle under the rim.

Cleveland might have been fatigued on Saturday (playing a SEGABABA), and coach Kenny Atkinson will surely try to mix things up in their rematch. We're curious to see what adjustments are up his sleeve. Jarrett Allen might return from the injured list, so maybe he can keep up with KAT better than Mopey Mobs?

The Knicks finish the week in Indiana. The poor Pacers can make a mess of things if you give them the ball and a head start. The 3-7 club has had trouble getting stops and closing games, but you can chalk that up to their leader Tyrese Haliburton being injured again. If he returns for Friday's game, this could be a different contest.

The Pacers can still score in a hurry, especially if you help them with turnovers and lost rebounds. Without Haliburton, Pascal Siakam and Andrew Nembhard have carried the load. Rick Carlisle's club can turn into layups at the other end, and for as inept as they've been at times this season, they can still hit the gas. According to my notes, you'll watch this one on Prime Video.

#2800. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#2797. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I wasn’t lying in that sense’

The Cavaliers beat the Knicks on Saturday, adding another win to their analytical spreadsheet.

Cleveland remains undefeated on that front, while New York keeps stacking dubs on the court.

On giving Landry Shamet more leeway than younger reserves and keeping bench minutes competitive:

"Obviously, you looking at a guy like Landry, he may have a little bit more leash than Pac [Dadiet]. Landry's as close to being a starter as you can imagine for us. Everybody else, they have to play and even the starters and Landry, they do, too."

On Landry Shamet's standing in the Knicks' rotation:

"About as close to being a starter as you can imagine for us."

On choosing Tyler Kolek over Jose Alvarado as the backup point guard:

"But you know, Jose had the first opportunity [against the Nets) and then I gave Tyler an opportunity in the second half and Tyler played well. So I go with Tyler and if I feel I need to make a change, Tyler's not getting it done in my eyes, I'll go back to Jose."

On Josh Hart's ability to contribute without having plays called for him:

"Josh is a connector. I don't think we call a single play for him at any time, he just goes and gets it done.

"He rebounds. Our pick and roll defense has been bad, and he's helped clean it up at times by putting him on the ball. So his versatility defensively is a bonus for us. So he's definitely going to be missed."

On facing Cleveland again after defeating the Cavaliers in the Eastern Conference finals:

"I mean, they're a great team. We saw them in the Eastern Conference finals. Obviously, they have another year under their belt with each other, more experience. And we saw what experience does for teams, especially like us."

On acknowledging that he underestimated Taylor Swift's history as a Knicks fan:

"Obviously with everything (going on), people have been going back and sending me all the stuff about her going to the games. And she was actually wearing a 13 jersey (in one picture). Probably Joakim Noah back in the day with Carmelo (Anthony). So, I wasn't up to date on my Taylor Swift Knicks fandom, so that's on me."

On the reaction to his comments about Taylor Swift and admitting he answered the question poorly:

"(The video) was everywhere. Everybody saw it. But it is what it is. For me, it's just a question that was asked to me that maybe I answered in the wrong way. I take that on the chin. But I love her music. We were just playing it five minutes ago and I'm singing every word to every song. Nothing against her at all."

On being a Taylor Swift fan despite questioning her Knicks fandom:

"I love her music. I probably know more songs than anybody else in the locker room. I just misspoke."

On returning to Cleveland and remembering the Knicks' championship run:

"It's human nature, you're gonna revert back and think back to the last time you were in the building. I had a quick little firing of that walking in for shootaround, but I think the general sense was that was last year. That was great. You can have those memories and hang on to that as long as we want, but ultimately this year is a new year and a new endeavor."

On the benefits of roster continuity and familiarity with Mike Brown:

"The biggest thing is just being comfortable with the guys on the floor, second year with [head coach] Mike [Brown]. I think that just breeds confidence. Familiarity breeds confidence. There's no denying that. It allows you to get to the point of conversations quicker. It makes for a good formula to find success."

On comparing his unpredictable Knicks role to that of a baseball relief pitcher:

"Especially with [my] role, I think I take a lot from my buddy Ashby, bullpen guy, you don't know when you're gonna get thrown in, what the situation's gonna be. My job might look a little different from night to night. If I'm changing my approach every night, that's not setting myself up for a good situation.

"I think in the past in my career when I was younger I would try to overcomplicate things and think I have to reinvent the wheel. I try to just keep it as simple as possible, reduce it down to my job, my impact and just play my game."

On Cleveland's Saturday loss to the Knicks:

"No excuse. These are games that we feel like we should win. We got another tough stretch coming up so there's no reason to sit here and feel bad and nothing's won in the first 10 games."

"I think we just gave them too many second-chance opportunities. Then it's too late, you're down 20 trying to fight back, but it's too late."

On the Cavaliers failing to match the Knicks' intensity:

"Got to bring your A game and we just didn't do that tonight."

On Cleveland continuing to compete despite trailing New York by 18 points:

"We could have folded down 18, could have packed it in on a back-to-back, tough week, but we kept at it."

On acknowledging that the Knicks dominated the Cavaliers in the Eastern Conference finals and then won the championship:

"They dominated it, and they did it again against San Antonio [in the Finals]. Analytically … there's an 'expected score,' and we've won two out of the three games.

"I think when you look back on it and take the context of the question, I'm not sure that was taken into account, which I know was a little bit of a lesson for me — like, you can just take a sound bite (and run with it).

"I would just say there was no coach more complimentary of the Knicks and honest about our performance and how they dominated us. So you live and learn a little bit with those things. But like I said, 99 percent of my comments were how they dominated us. They were by far the better team. I answered the question, as it was taken, (which was) 'And you're down 3-0, what hope do you have of even giving to your guys?'

"We get a lot of information … and I wasn't lying in that sense, but in no sense was it like, 'Oh, man, we deserve to be winning.' Like zero — zero — thought of that."

On why analytics could not outweigh the Knicks' physical and mental dominance:

"We get information that checks our process. All that being said, we were still dominated. It kind of doesn't matter (what the analytics say) because there is more than that. There is the mental toughness, the physical toughness, the flow to the game, how aggressive you are. There are all these other intangibles that go with that."

On recognizing that the Knicks earned their championship:

"They deserved it, and they did it again against San Antonio. Just an incredible, incredible run."

#2791. Highlights: Spurs third-quarter barrage blows out Trail Blazers

Coming off an opening NBA Cup win against the Sacramento Kings, the Spurs faced a SEGABABA against the Portland Trail Blazers. After a sluggish first quarter, the Spurs, behind by 42 points in the second quarter, built their two-point lead to 15 at halftime. In the third, the Spurs drove the nail in the coffin, outscoring the Blazers 45-27 and ballooning their lead to 33 heading into the fourth. The Blazers simply could not stop the ball movement and three-point barrage by the silver and black, as the Spurs ultimately won 125-103.

Victor Wembanyama continues his early MVP campaign with another efficient high-scoring game of 29 points (9-13 FG, 3-6 3PT, 8-9 FT), seven rebounds, three assists, a block, and a steal. Despite only playing 24 minutes and not being as big a focal point of the offense as he was against Dallas and Sacramento, Wemby once again demonstrated how unguardable he truly is. After starting the season shooting under 30% from three, Wemby is now shooting 7-12 (58%) from three in his last two games.

Since he's shown he can shoot threes, Donovan Clingan steps out to guard him but immediately gets beat by Wemby on the drive to the basket. All Toumani Camara can do is go straight up, but against a seven-foot-four (he's definitely taller) alien, it's almost pointless.

The bulk of Wemby's scoring comes from inside the paint. He is dunking the ball more than any other player in the league and is even leading other TEAMS in total dunks. Where do most of his dunks come from? Lobs, of course.

The most unguardable lob threat in the league, all his teammates have to do is just throw it up above the rim to where only Wemby can grab it. Sometimes, defenders are in the way. This time, Clingan was unfortunately one of his victims.

Devin Vassell dropped another solid game with 15 points, three assists, two rebounds, and two steals. His defense somehow outshone his shooting from three. His agility and lateral movement allow him to stay in front of multiple guards and forwards. He also deflected multiple passes, and one of them turned into a steal and layup all by himself.

Stephon Castle dropped 14 points, seven rebounds, five assists, and a block. Castle, like Vassell, played like a Swiss Army knife against the Blazers. He crashed the glass, dished out dimes, and had himself some highlight buckets. The hustle play of the game was him somehow tight-roping the sideline and dishing out to an open Vassell for the corner three.

Poor Clingan. De'Aaron Fox and Castle run a beautiful DHO that results in a posterizing alley-oop with Castle dunking on his former college teammate.

Although they didn't shoot 50% from three this game, they still drained 17 compared to Portland's nine. For most Spurs fans, that third-quarter explosion showed that when everyone is bought in and focused within the flow of the offense, this team can put teams away early. The communication also reached the defense, as they held the Blazers to 76 points after 36 minutes (before garbage time). Next week, the Spurs face the Suns, Bulls, Warriors, & Jazz.

Finally, here are the full game highlights.

The Spurs' early road trip continues with a visit to the Mortgage Matchup Center, the home of the Phoenix Suns, this Monday at 8:00 P.M. (CST) on DAZN.

#2785. Pistons vs Lakers preview: Best Defense vs best offense

Does it get any better than the No. 1 offense versus the No. 1 defense? The Detroit Pistons find themselves in that matchup as the 122-point-per-game Los Angeles Lakers visit Motown tonight.

Luka Dončić and the Lakers put up plenty of buckets, but scoring on Detroit is still an impossible task. The Pistons only surrender 101 points a game. Smothering defense every night.

Detroit's defense has its biggest test of the season if Dončić suits up this evening. He's questionable with lower back tightness.

It would be a shame if we're robbed of Cade Cunningham going head-to-head with another MVP candidate. The bigger shame would be if we miss out on Dončić versus Ausar Thompson. That matchup is like Jerry Rice versus Deion Sanders with how both are operating.

Detroit hasn't played since Wednesday's loss to the Philadelphia 76ers. They've had plenty of time to rest up. They'll need it if Dončić suits up.

Dončić is averaging 40 points. He's shooting 42% on nearly 14 3s a game. He gets to the line more than anybody. It's basically no stopping Dončić at the moment. This is a Wilt Chamberlain-like hot streak. Those two are the only players to average 40 nine games into the season. Whew, to say he's balling is an understatement.

But tasks like this are why the Pistons employ Ausar Thompson. If *anybody* can slow Dončić down, it's the best perimeter defender on the planet. Dončić usually has success against quick defenders with wiry strength, but Ausar is bigger and stronger this season. We'll be keeping tabs on how Ausar takes Dončić's bumps tonight. Ausar has the quick hands to disrupt things as well.

This might be a game where we see Paul Reed getting to the level and blitzing a bunch. J.B. Bickerstaff may decide to get the ball out of Dončić's hands and force someone else to beat them. Reed is capable of coming to the level on screens and muddying things up.

I'm sure Dončić will see some Cade tonight too. Cade won't make it easy with his sturdy frame. On the other end, the Lakers only have a few perimeter defenders to throw at Cade.

Matisse Thybulle is firmly in LA's rotation as he's started all 10 games. He's still a defensive playmaking threat. Cade deals with defenders like this nightly, but Thybulle is a plus defender nonetheless. So is Ziaire Williams. The springy 25-year-old treats every possession as if he'll never get in the game again if he doesn't go hard.

Those two are solid defensively, and Jake LaRavia competes. Outside of that, Detroit will have plenty of favorable matchups (Walker Kessler is a great rim protector and is off to a good offensive start. He can make a difference).

Dončić displays effort and is trying to shake the terrible defender narrative on his name, but attacking him is still the best way to slow him down. Make him work and keep him involved in actions.

Austin Reaves falls into the same boat. He's under Dončić on the Lakers' best defenders on the team list. Anybody with that matchup should look to be aggressive.

Reaves spends most of his time covering shooting guards. Will JJ Redick make Reaves chase Duncan Robinson all night? That's probably not ideal. Robinson runs even great defenders into the ground.

Robinson's light-em-up stretch continues as he's fourth in 3s made (35). Only Dončić, Nickeil Alexander-Walker, and Steph Curry have made more.

Detroit is letting it fly from deep as a team. According to Cleaning the Glass, 40 percent of their shots are coming from 3 (15th) compared to 31 percent last year (29th). Their rim rate has flipped the other way, but getting Jalen Duren back should rectify that.

The best offense versus the best defense is a mandatory viewing appointment. Can Ausar slow down the MVP-favorite? Do the Pistons show why they're an elite defense and smother Dončić as a team? Does Cade deliver a masterclass against a guy he'll be compared to for the foreseeable future if he's in the MVP race? We'll find out at 6 PM. Be there!

Detroit Pistons (6-2): Cade Cunningham, Duncan Robinson, Ausar Thompson, John Collins, Paul Reed

Los Angeles Lakers (8-2): Luka Dončić, Austin Reaves, Matisse Thybulle, Jake LaRavia, Walker Kessler

What are your thoughts on Austin Reaves? He reportedly did not want to be drafted by Detroit. Does that still bother you?

#2783. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#2778. What we learned from the Spurs’ win over the Trail Blazers

If you've been looking for a signature San Antonio Spurs win early in the 2026-27 season, look no further. On the second game of a back-to-back, the Spurs waltzed into enemy territory and blew out a team motivated to get revenge. It was one of the first times we've seen this year's Spurs put it all together against a playoff-caliber team.

Defense was the name of the game in San Antonio's 125-103 win over the Portland Trail Blazers. Both teams played incredibly physical (or just flopped a lot). But the Spurs were the enforcers overall. Stephon Castle set the tone on the perimeter with some suffocating defense on two of the Blazers' best players, Deni Advija and Jrue Holiday. Advija was particularly frustrated by Castle's defense, scoring just 15 points on 5-13 shooting.

The rest of the team forced turnovers. Portland coughed it up 18 times, leading to 23 easy points for the Spurs. The tape will show that the Spurs dominated this game with force and athleticism. The stat sheet shows they won a lot of the hustle stats, too. San Antonio won the rebounding battle by 13 and grabbed 14 offensive boards. The force they showed against Portland is the same as what they showed last season in their NBA Finals run, but more refined.

The Blazers just feel like a team the Spurs will have to beat in the Victor Wembanyama era. They are young (for the most part), tough, and skilled. Beating them down in such a dominant fashion after the playoff victory last season is a clear sign that San Antonio has not taken a step back, despite some bumps to start the season. It also showed a lot of grit and edge from the Spurs. It was exactly the type of victory this team needed early in the season.

I gave the second unit a tough time in the last WWL. They were MUCH better against Portland. Luke Kornet in particular had one of his best games of the season with 6 points, 4 rebounds, 2 assists, and a block. There were fewer moments when Kornet needed to go one-on-one with a driver at the rim, as the perimeter defense made it harder for the Blazers to get clear drives to the basket. What was even more impressive about Kornet's game is that he actually took a few shots when he was open. Typically, the backup center isn't even thinking about shooting, but he hit a pop-a-shot early in the game and took a jumper from the free-throw line that didn't fall. Just getting Kornet to take those kinds of shots when they are available will help the second-unit offense tremendously.

It's been a minute since we got a sub-25-minute elite performance from Wembanyama. Not because the performances haven't been elite, but because he's usually playing more than 25 minutes. He had 29 points on 9-for-13 shooting, including three triples. Portland had absolutely no answer for the early-season MVP candidate. In the last two games, he's become more selective with his jump shots, and the results have been better looks that are going in more often.

Carter Bryant got an opportunity to play early in the game and made the most of it. He hit a pair of three-pointers, got a deflection and a block, providing energy off the bench. Bryant still struggles with overaggressiveness on defense. He picked up 5 fouls in just 17 minutes on Saturday. Bryant has a lot of talent; it just needs to be harnessed with improved awareness and experience.

The Blazers continued their strategy of guarding Castle with Donovan Clingan and putting Toumani Camara on Wembanyama. It's safe to say the Spurs have figured out how to defeat that coverage. It helps that Castle made two of his five three-pointers, and Wembanyama is a game-breaker. Portland still needed to send doubles at Wembanyama, and when they did, the Spurs were able to get easy looks when the ball was passed out.

#2777. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2774. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

#2765. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2763. The Myles Garrett and Aaron Donald combo was as-advertised

It took two months longer than we thought it would, but we finally saw Myles Garrett and Aaron Donald wreck a game.

Garrett sacked Jayden Daniels three times including a forced fumble. Donald did not record a sack, but he was productive in both the run and pass games despite what the box score indicates. As a result, the Los Angeles Rams held the Washington Commanders to just 13 points as the team moves to 5-4.

According to SumerSports, Donald tied with Puka Nacua for a team-leading nine impact plays. Garrett came in third place with four.

Average time to pressure: 2.15 seconds (0.45 faster than NFL average)

12.9% pressure rate (0.95% above NFL average)

Average time to pressure: 2.38 seconds (0.05 faster than NFL average)

21.4% pressure rate (7.18% above NFL average)

The Rams were creative in how they deployed Garrett and Donald. Donald stunted on 28.6% of his pass rushes, which ranks roughly 15% above his counterparts at the position. Garrett often lined up over the offensive interior in order to take advantage of a lineup change after the Commanders since they were without their starting center.

While these numbers are unofficial and pending review, Pro Football Focus (PFF) also confirms stellar performances by both players. Garrett finished with an unofficial, team-leading defensive grade of 94.5 and Donald was third at 81.6. PFF had Garrett with nine pressures and Donald with five. LA finished with 21 pressures overall, which suggests the duo was also creating opportunities and favorable matchups for other defenders.

Daniels was under pressure on 35% of his drop backs. It's extremely difficult to play quarterback effectively in that environment, even if the Rams were without Trent McDuffie on the backend.

But another way to read that is to say the defense should get even better once McDuffie returns from the back injury he suffered last week. The Rams will be tough to face on defense. With Garrett back in the fold, it's not a bold take to say they are now a defensive team.

Opposing offenses should be shaking in their boots. Myles Garrett and Aaron Donald were exactly as advertised. They just put the rest of the NFL on notice.

#2757. Brandon Aubrey concern levels heightened after Colts win

The Dallas Cowboys won on Sunday. They did so by the skin of their teeth. It took almost all four quarters for the Cowboys to declare victory against the Indianapolis Colts, and for a little while near the end it looked like they were going to lose.

Dallas was trailing 23-20 but wound up scoring a touchdown to give them their final lead, but it is important to note that they missed a field goal prior to their last drive that would have made matters simpler.

It was a 50-yard attempt from Brandon Aubrey, his 24th from 50+ since the beginning of last season. The miss officially means that he now has double-digit misses of that variety.

Aubrey's miss gave the Colts the ball at the Dallas 40-yard line which meant that they did not have to travel all too far for the field goal that successfully gave them their 23-20 lead. That is the inherent risk with attempts from that far, and while it was once Aubrey's specialty, it is now a higher-proposition risk than it used to be.

It should be noted that eight of these field goals have been from 60+ which certainly changes the calculus and more importantly the evaluation. Half of the 60+ attempts are a part of the overall misses from Aubrey so there is still a legitimate value involved of misses between 50 and 59 yards which is pretty standard (relatively speaking) for today's NFL kickers.

The Cowboys gave Aubrey an extension this past offseason that officially made him the highest-paid kicker in the NFL. Without getting too interested in another person's finances, the reality of that deal is that it means Aubrey has to be more reliable, especially at something that was regarded to be his forte.

#2755. Will this week's blue wave election usher in a Democratic president in 2028?

In surfer slang, a wave that lasts a long time is a "runner." A key question for investors is whether the blue wave that washed over the country Tuesday was that kind of wave, and could it ferry Democrats to victory in 2028.

Statistically, the answer is it should have little effect.

CNBC analyzed elections since 1938 and looked at the biggest midterm wave elections where the party in the White House lost seats to determine whether they portend anything about the presidential election two years later.

Tuesday's election was the biggest House seat loss for the incumbent party in the White House since 2018, but it only barely makes the historical Top 10. It ranks 10th in CNBC's analysis using an estimate of 41 House seats gained by the opposition party as close races are still being tallied. The largest loss came in 1938 when Democrats lost 72 House seats amid a renewed recession, opposition to court-packing by President Franklin Delano Roosevelt and the president's battle with conservatives in his own party. Roosevelt went on to win reelection two years later in 1940.

In more modern times, Republicans swept to power in the House in 2010 two years into Barack Obama's first presidential term, with Democrats losing 64 seats, though retaining control of the Senate. Obama went on to reelection two years later. Of the top four wave elections, which all occurred before a president's final term, the incumbent went on to win reelection each time.

But for the next five waves, the incumbent party lost, including John F. Kennedy beating Richard Nixon in 1960 after Republicans lost seats under President Dwight Eisenhower in 1958; and Joe Biden beating Donald Trump in 2020 after Republicans lost seats in 2018.

Jay Campbell, partner at Hart Associates, the Democratic pollster for the CNBC All-America Economic Survey, said the reason midterm waves may not carry forward is the importance of individual characters in presidential elections.

"A lot can happen in two years. And while presidential elections are certainly subject to the overall political environment, they are also very much about each party's nominee," Campbell said. "If candidate quality matters in congressional elections, it matters 1,000 times more in presidential ones."

It's a long way to 2028 and this wave, gnarly as it may have been, could statistically peter out just as easily as it could wash Democrats back into the White House.

#2753. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#2748. Trump says U.S. carried out ‘minor strikes’ on Iran as missiles hit Qeshm Island

President Donald Trump told reporters Friday that the U.S. carried out "minor strikes" on Iran, amid reports that two missiles struck Qeshm Island near the Strait of Hormuz.

The missiles struck the island on Friday, Iranian news agency Tasnim reported, according to Reuters. Tasnim said there was no official information on damage, Reuters added.

The island lies off Iran's southern coast, near the Strait of Hormuz, the vital artery for oil and gas that has become a flashpoint in the war.

Speaking to reporters Friday evening after landing in West Palm Beach, Florida, Trump didn't comment specifically on Qeshm Island, saying: "I don't want to talk about it."

But he added: "We do minor strikes, but they knock out. They built a little bit of an anti-aircraft device, and we knocked it out in about 15 minutes, maybe five." He did not explicitly confirm that he was referring to the reported strikes on Qeshm.

The strikes are the first since Tuesday's midterm elections, after analysts and regional officials had debated whether the vote would lead Trump toward further escalation or renewed diplomacy.

The U.S. struck the same island Monday, in what a U.S. official told MS Now was a "defensive measure."

Trump also told reporters Friday that Iran wants "to make a deal very badly. They have 300% inflation. Their navy's gone. Their airport, everything's gone, and they want to make a deal."

The International Monetary Fund forecast that Iran's consumer prices would rise by an average of 78.6% in 2026, in a report published Oct. 13.

When asked if he wanted to make a deal "really soon," Trump said: "Well, we have nobody getting hurt, nobody getting killed.

"You know, this is a war that we may have been there for seven months, but we probably fought for three or four weeks, really fought. And again, you look at World War One; that's a war. World War Two; that's a war."

Iran has said it wants more than "paper commitments" from the U.S. before entering a new phase of diplomatic talks.

"We must see practical measures by the Americans," Ali Bagheri Kani, deputy secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, told state broadcaster IRIB on Friday, according to Press TV.

#2745. Berkshire shares surge as Wall Street swoons amid AI fears

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway shares roared past the S&P 500 index this week as investors moved money away from slumping tech stocks and moved into what appears to be the relative safety of a traditional conglomerate with relatively little exposure to artificial intelligence amid fears massive spending on data centers won't pay off.

One month ago, Berkshire's widely held B shares were underperforming the S&P by 13.6 percentage points for the year.

With help from three more record-high closes this week, Berkshire is now 5.8 percentage points ahead of the benchmark.

Berkshire more than doubled its share repurchases last quarter to $9.6 billion, while operating earnings declined slightly, the company said on Saturday.

Berkshire was up more than 5% this week while the S&P fell almost 2.7%.

The contrast is even sharper with the Nasdaq Composite, which fell 3.4% on the week.

Thursday's especially heavy tech selling pushed the index down more than 10% below its most recent high in what is commonly referred to as a "correction," but it did make up some of its lost ground Friday.

Two weeks ago, Berkshire's share prices completely erased all of the roughly 15% drop they suffered in the three months following Warren Buffett's surprise announcement at the 2025 shareholders meeting that he would step down as CEO at the end of that year.

Now they are up 6% from the record high closes they hit the Friday before Buffett's revelation on May 3, 2025.

Berkshire is not completely removed from the convulsions around AI.

Abel told us in early September he sees a "significant opportunity" ahead for Berkshire Hathaway Energy to provide electricity to data centers.

He also expected Berkshire would benefit from its big bet on Google parent Alphabet, saying he and Warren Buffett knew AI was "going to have a significant impact on America and businesses," and "we saw Google as a significant player."

Now that investment is getting negative reviews this week as the stock slumps along with other companies related to AI.

As of the end of June, Berkshire reported it owned almost 106 million shares of Alphabet, including 28.6 million shares it bought directly from the company in June: $5 billion of its Class A shares (GOOGL) for $351.81 each and another $5 billion of Class C shares (GOOG) for $348.20 each.

It bought another $7 billion of Alphabet shares on the open market during the second quarter, adding to the position it started building in last year's third quarter, an investment initiated by Warren Buffett.

Bloomberg suggests that "with cracks in the AI trade starting to show, Berkshire's sudden push [into AI] may appear ill-timed."

On June 30, the position had a market value of $37.8 billion and it was the third largest equity holding in Berkshire's portfolio.

Since then both the Class A and Class C shares have dropped by around 20% and the stake is now valued at $30.2 billion, falling behind Coca-Cola's $38.3 billion to the portfolio's #4 position.

(In just over a week, we'll find out whether Berkshire made any changes to its Alphabet position during the third quarter ending September 30.)

Bloomberg quotes CFRA Research analyst Cathy Seifert as saying, "I do think that part of this may be a conscious decision to increase their exposure. One has to ask the question though, is it a little late?"

But the article notes that "for dedicated value investors and longtime Berkshire observers, such jitters may not really matter," with Keefe Bruyette & Woods analyst Meyer Shields saying Abel probably sees bets on AI as a long-term wager on the technology's potential, in keeping with the "conservatism that you would expect at Berkshire Hathaway."

At Barron's, however, Andrew Bary sees what he estimates is a $3 billion loss on Alphabet as part of a "disappointing" long-term trend in Berkshire's stock choices

"The company hasn't created much value from stock picking during the current decade" even though the "S&P 500 has returned 150% since the end of 2019 and tech stocks have quadrupled."

While Berkshire's investments in five Japanese "trading houses," Chevron, and Chubb have done well, "there have been plenty of losers or simply mediocre investments that have been closed out."

He cites Verizon, HP, Paramount (now Skydance), General Motors, Constellation Brands, and Pool.

While he estimates Berkshire's Occidental Petroleum investment is up about 20% from what it paid, OXY is "far behind most energy stocks" amid this year's big increase in crude prices.

Bary also lists stocks that have continued to rise in price after Berkshire cut its stakes, with Apple being the worst offender.

That stake has been reduced by 75% since 2023 at what Bary calculates is an average selling price around $200 per share, well below AAPL's current $342.

Despite those sales, Apple is Berkshire's largest equity holding with a market value of $78 billion. The stock is up 1200% since Berkshire started buying it at the beginning of 2016, at a time when many thought the company had already seen its best days.

Bary thinks Abel will probably keep Berkshire's core equity investments but may concentrate more on his strengths going forward: improving Berkshire's operating companies and making new acquisitions.

His conclusion: "Barring a market rout, equity investing may not be central to Berkshire anymore.

"And if any new Berkshire holdings are revealed soon, it may not be a good idea to buy them. The record hasn't been great."

Berkshire Hathaway's disclosure that it had made a relatively small $27 million addition to its $4.1 billion Sirius XM position helped fuel an ongoing rally that lifted the satellite radio company's shares to a 52-week high this week.

The stock ended 4.9% higher that day at $28.67.

On Friday, Monday, and Tuesday, Berkshire bought more than 924 thousand shares at an average price of $29.10 as the stock continued to gain ground, ending Tuesday at $30.80.

Berkshire disclosed the buys late Tuesday evening, apparently helping to fuel an additional 8.4% gain as the stock closed at $33.40 on Wednesday.

It hit a 52-week intraday high of $33.76 on Thursday but finished lower Friday at $32.40.

Overall, Sirius XM is up 18.6% since the earnings release and has gained 62.0% so far this year.

It's the first addition to Berkshire's stake since late-July and early-August last year.

In 2025, it bought a total of 7.3 million shares at an average price of $21.86.

The position is likely one of portfolio manager Ted Weschler's stocks.

Pacific Power, a unit of Berkshire Hathaway's PacifiCorp, is asking California regulators for a 57% rate increase that would raise the typical residential electric bill by $120 a month.

The Wall Street Journal calls it a "stunning request even in an era of rising electricity costs."

The utility says the hike would be partially offset by a 14% decrease taking effect on January 1.

A lot of the $103 million that would be generated by the increase is already being spent on efforts to make power equipment in the sparsely populated and heavily wooded area more resistant to wildfires.

The utility is facing around $637 million in liability claims for fires in 2020 and 2022.

This week, Reuters reports, Oregon's Supreme Court heard arguments on whether to revive a jury verdict that could expose PacifiCorp "to tens of billions of dollars of potential damages over its role in four Oregon wildfires in 2020."

Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger share their opinions about how artificial intelligence could affect the economy, the government, and how much time people spend on leisure activities.

ANDREW ROSS SORKIN: Thomas Kimay (PH) is here. He's a 27-year-old shareholder from Kentfield, California.

And I should preface this question by saying that he was here 17 years ago at 10 years old, asked you a question from the audience asking you if the internet might hurt some of Berkshire's investments.

At the time, you said you wanted to see how things would play out. He's now updated the question. (Buffett laughs)

"What do you think about the implications of artificial intelligence on Berkshire's businesses, beyond autonomous driving and GEICO, which you've talked about already? ...

WARREN BUFFETT: I certainly have no special insights on artificial intelligence, but I will bet a lot of things happen in that field in the next couple of decades, and probably a shorter timeframe.

They should lead, I would certainly think — but again, I don't bring much to this party. But I would certainly think they would result in significantly less employment in certain areas.

And it may not be good for a given business, but let's take it to the extreme.

Let's assume one person could push a button and, essentially, through various machines and robotics, all kinds of things, turn out all of the output we have in this country.

So, everybody's — there's just as much output as we have. It's all being done by, you know, instead of 150-some million people being employed, one person.

You know, is the world better off or not?

Well, certainly we'd work a lot less hours a week — of work per week and so on.

I mean, it would be a good thing, but it would require enormous transformation in how people relate to each other, what they expect of government, you know, all kinds of things. And, of course, as a practical matter, more than one person would keep working.

But pushing the idea that way is one of the — you'd certainly think that's one of the consequences of making great progress in artificial intelligence.

And that's enormously prosocial, eventually. It's enormously disruptive in other ways. And it can have huge problems, in terms of a democracy and how it reacts to that...

CHARLIE MUNGER: Well, you're painting a very funny world where everybody's engaged in trade. And the trade is, I give you golf lessons and you dye my hair. And that would be a world kind of like the royal family of Kuwait or something.

And I don't think it would be good for America to have everything produced by one person and the rest of us just engaged in leisure.

#2744. Nets vs. Mavericks Preview: Sunday night basketball in Dallas

Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Issac Baldizon/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

That shouldn't have happened. On a night the Brooklyn Nets out-rebounded the Atlanta Hawks by 13, it was their inability to grab rebounds in the final two minutes that lost them the game. Brooklyn wound up losing 124-121 Saturday night, letting a game slip away that was there for the taking.

The opponent tonight is in a pretty interesting spot.

The Mavericks are looking to get back into the playoffs for the first time in a few years, and they hope the talent on the roster is good enough to get it done. They picked up an NBA Cup tournament victory, 112-100, against the Utah Jazz at home on Friday night 112-100 NBA Cup win over the Utah Jazz at home Friday night.

WHERE: American Airlines Center (Dallas, TX)

WATCH: YES Network, DAZN, NBA League Pass

OUT – Tarik Biberovic (right heel bursitis)

OUT – Dereck Lively II (right foot surgery)

OUT – Sergio de Larrea (right knee sprain)

Back-to-backs are tough, and going from Atlanta to Dallas is especially challenging. We'll see how the Nets do in this one.

If they want to win, they'll need to slow down an old friend. Kyrie Irving is back after missing last year with an ACL tear. He's averaging 21 points a night while his shooting numbers remain strong, the way he's getting his buckets looks a little different than what we're used to.

His field goal attempts share of field-goal attempts inside of three feet is at the lowest mark of his career, while his three-point rate is at a career high. Chances are that'll change the further he gets away from the injury, but it's certainly something to keep an eye on. Either way, Irving remains a magnetic scorer and a blast to watch when his game is on.

Speaking of being on, Nolan Traoré continues to shine. The sophomore guard had 19 points and a career-high five steals in just 22 minutes last night against Atlanta on Saturday. He didn't play in the fourth quarter, and with Brown Jr. out tonight Sunday, Traoré should get all the minutes he can handle. When Brown Jr. returns, we'll see if the coaching staff finally starts to give the young guards more playing time alongside each other. The Nets have little to lose by giving that pairing a longer look. Why not give it a shot?

We'll see if the Nets can be great on the glass for all 48 minutes tonight. Brooklyn did great for most of the night, but gave away possessions late with their inability to secure the rebound. Day'ron Sharpe and the team will look to keep Daniel Gafford, Morez Johnson, and the rest of the Mavs off the glass.

Speaking of Sharpe, the big guy collected the first triple-double of his career with 12 points, ten rebounds, and a career high 13 assists.

I try not to fall for hyperbole, but I think I'm gonna make an exception here. Cooper Flagg is so damn good at such a young age and even in his second season as a professional, I think he's comfortably one of the ten best players in the NBA. He's so good and at just 19 years old, has a chance to lead the next generation of men's basketball in the USA in the next decade and beyond.

Something that will come with time and experience is the ability to make an impact at the end of games. In a recent game against the San Antonio Spurs, Flagg was relatively uninvolved in the Mavs' crunch time offense. It was a bit of a surprise considering how excellent Flagg is, and David Trink of Mavs Moneyball has more:

"This is not to say that Flagg is incapable. We have seen him take over fourth quarters multiple times this year. If Dallas wants to be a serious playoff contender, however, they can't have weird nights from him like this. Even if his shots are not going in, he has to demand the ball, and he has to take them. It is his job to take the pressure off Irving late in games. He's young, so it is entirely possible he was in his own head after a poor shooting game. But learning to be the guy on a nightly basis and flushing the bad performances will be the next step in his evolution."

And that next step is gonna be a doozy. That said, he's been terrific already and it'll be exciting to watch him ball out tonight.

It's a homecoming for Julius Randle. The Dallas native is back home and is hoping to put on a show in front of his friends and family. He's had his good moments so far, but he's got to cut down on the turnovers. Julius has turned it over at least four times in five out of the last six games and a season high six turnovers last night. The Nets aren't good enough to withstand that and will need their All-Star to make the most out of possessions every time he has the ball in his hands.

It's probably been a minute since you watched some Dirk Nowitzki footage. Let me help you with that

#2743. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.
