#2742. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2741. America doesn't like socialism. So why are socialists winning?

Democratic socialists just had their biggest election night yet.

There's just one complication: Most American voters don't actually like socialism.

Six candidates backed by the Democratic Socialists of America won House seats Tuesday, giving the movement its largest foothold yet in Congress and greater clout in a Democratic Party already divided over how far left to go.

The victories, coming on the heels of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's rise to power, mark another milestone for a movement that has spent years challenging the party establishment. Now, with Democrats sweeping control of Congress, the question is whether those electoral gains will translate into actual political power.

The results of the new CNBC Voter Poll show the potential hazard of handing too much power to the party's left flank. The survey of roughly 80,000 midterm voters found that 53% view socialism unfavorably, compared with 33% who viewed it favorably. Capitalism fared considerably better, with 56% expressing a positive view. Just 18% of voters identified themselves as democratic socialists.

That disconnect underscores both the opportunity and the challenge facing Democrats and their growing left flank. How the party reconciles the divide between reforming capitalism and fundamentally overhauling it could shape its legislative agenda in Congress, its economic message to voters and, ultimately, its direction heading into the 2028 presidential election.

The DSA's presence in Congress will be quadrupled when Tuesday's victors, all of whom won in safely Democratic districts, are sworn in.

But the projected Democratic landslide of more than 250 House seats could dilute their influence. Party leaders could afford to lose all eight DSA votes on most measures, leaving the group with far less bargaining power than it would wield in a narrowly controlled chamber.

Still, the movement is building financial muscle. While the DSA relies on membership dues, wealthy donors are now backing its candidates through outside groups. The 12 largest donors to American Priorities, a super PAC supporting some DSA-backed candidates, gave nearly $5.9 million this cycle, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings through Oct. 31.

"We've had success with more donors but also we have an even stronger ground game to spread our message," said Megan Romer, co-chair of the DSA. "Mailers and ads alone just don't do it anymore. You have to knock on more doors and make more phone calls and talk to more people at work and in your union."

The more revealing tests on Tuesday came outside of liberal enclaves.

In Florida, DSA member Angie Nixon came within three percentage points of an upset in a state where Democrats haven't won a Senate race since 2012 and President Donald Trump won by 13 percentage points in 2024. But she lost by a wider margin than Democratic gubernatorial candidate David Jolly, who took a more moderate approach.

Michigan told a similar story. Progressive Abdul El-Sayed – backed by self-described democratic socialist Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., – won his Senate seat by seven percentage points. Yet he lagged nearly four percentage points behind Democratic Gov.-elect Jocelyn Benson's winning margin.

El-Sayed shares many DSA priorities but neither belongs to the group nor received its endorsement. He says he favors reforming capitalism over replacing it.

State ballot initiatives offered an equally mixed ideological verdict: Nearly two-thirds of voters in ruby-red Montana backed limits on corporate political spending, while deep-blue California rejected a billionaire wealth tax by roughly 10 percentage points, even as voters approved higher income taxes on the wealthy.

The results sharpen the dilemma over Democrats' identity heading into 2028, as Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., the DSA's most prominent standard-bearer, seemingly weighs a White House bid: Can left-wing populism expand the party's coalition without alienating the swing-state moderates it needs to win a presidential election – especially in a less favorable political environment?

"I think a lot of people did vote for Democrats who would not ordinarily and who probably did it ... with their teeth gritted," Romer told CNBC.

But it's clear that even voters who reject socialism aren't satisfied with capitalism as it stands.

Three out of every four participants in the CNBC Voter Poll said the condition of the U.S. economy was poor or not so good, compared with just 2% who described it as excellent. And 70% said the economic system favors the wealthy rather than treating most Americans fairly.

That frustration helped fuel a populist message far beyond the socialist left, with victorious Democrats from Adam Hamilton in Kansas to James Talarico in Texas tapping into anger over rising costs, economic inequality and the political influence of wealthy elites. Eighteen Sanders-endorsed candidates also won, including in traditionally right-leaning states like Ohio and Montana.

"The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom," Talarico, who flipped a Senate seat for Democrats in the Lone Star state, said in his victory speech. "The people at the top work so hard to keep us angry and divided because our unity is a threat to their wealth and their power."

Even politicians who weren't on the ballot took note. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a video posted to X Friday that "Democrats won by being populist economic fighters."

Economists have pointed to measures showing increasing wealth concentration and a shrinking share of the U.S. GDP heading to workers in the form of pay as signs that the divide between the country's rich and poor is widening. Coming out of the pandemic, the "K"-shaped economy has become a common descriptor for the unequal recovery seen by income classes.

Americans displayed their economic dissatisfaction well before they headed to the polls this week. The University of Michigan's closely followed consumer sentiment index hit all-time lows this year.

"We have a rising tide that is not equally lifting all boats," said Joe Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM, a tax firm. "That's not what we used to tell ourselves when we were young."

Rather than signaling a distaste for capitalism as a whole, Brusuelas said this week's results show voters want a system that also includes populist policies like Medicare for all. Or, as he calls it, "capitalism with a human face."

"These things are not mutually exclusive," said Brusuelas, who regularly briefs members of Congress on policy issues. "We can walk and chew gum at the same time."

Tuesday's Democratic sweep left some on Wall Street racing to prepare their clients' portfolios for potential policy shifts. Bank of America analysts detailed what heightened scrutiny of social media platforms or artificial intelligence data centers could mean for the shares of companies in those industries. CIBC built a basket of stocks that should benefit from a Democratically controlled Congress.

Still, the reaction of Wall Street veterans and New York City business moguls to this week's election was relatively subdued compared with Mamdani's victory in the mayoral race last year.

After Mamdani clinched his party's nomination, Pershing Square CEO Bill Ackman said he was "a bit depressed." Ahead of Mamdani's victory, Kathryn Wylde, who leads the Partnership for New York City, a nonprofit group of business executives, told CNBC that "terror is the feeling" as he rose in the polls.

Wall Street magnates wondered how a city known as the epicenter of financial markets and global capitalism could survive under a self-described socialist. Some questioned if billionaires would flee the city or whether corporations would move their headquarters — fears that largely haven't materialized since Mamdani took office.

With roughly 10 months of Mamdani's term in the rearview mirror, Anthony Chan, the former chief economist at JPMorgan, said finance moguls have realized that politics don't necessarily result in material policy changes. Chan said it's a message they've carried with them exiting this week, with many understanding that any incoming lawmakers in Washington aligned with democratic socialist policies would be a small minority.

"Many of the people that I meet that were very adamantly against and opposed to his candidacy now are sending their kids to his free daycare centers," Chan said, referencing Mamdani's partnership with the state to expand no-cost programs for 2-year-olds in New York City.

"I thought you felt that the world was going to end if this mayor came in," said Chan, who's also an alumnus of Barclays and the New York Federal Reserve. "Now, you're sending your kids to his free daycare centers."

But the DSA has even bigger ambitions for 2028. With growing political clout, infrastructure and financial backing, the movement is looking to expand its influence nationwide, while one of its most prominent allies, Ocasio-Cortez, weighs a White House run.

"That infrastructure is there, and that will make a big difference for a potential left presidential candidate," Romer said. "It's really kind of do or die time. We really have to deliver."

#2739. Raiders-49ers: 16 things we learned

After ending a four-game losing streak with an impressive second half comeback win last week, the Las Vegas Raiders faced another quality opponent in Week 9 and, well, the results were familiar.

The Raiders were blown out in their first game in Bay Area since 2019 as the San Francisco 49ers clubbed the 38-17 on Sunday.

The Raiders are now 4-5 and fell to under .500 for the second time this season after starting 3-0. There has been a clear theme under first-year head coach Klint Kubiak so far this season. They beat bad teams and lose to good teams.

The four teams the Raiders had a collective 5-24 record going into this week's slate. The Raiders are 0-5 against teams with a .500 record or better. Games against the Seattle Seahawks and Denver Broncos loom in the next two weeks. They both have winning records. However, The Seahawks may be down to third-string quarterback Jalen Milroe next with both Sam Darnold and Drew Lock dealing with injuries.

This was their third blowout loss. With the trade deadline looming Tuesday, the Raiders have a chance to become sellers if they wish.

The Raiders' problems Sunday were easy to spot. They were sloppy on offense and leaky as usual on defense. The Raiders committed turnovers on five of their six possessions in the second half with two turnovers in the end end and a Pick Six. Kirk Cousins had his worst game as a Raider with three interceptions.

The Raiders allowed 30 points on defense for the sixth straight game. It tied an NFL record.

Here is a look at the key aspects of this game:

The Raiders' defense is getting gutted every week and first-year defensive coordinator Rob Leonard has to be feeling the pressure. He is likely not in jeopardy of losing his job. But he has to find a way to get improved results. The same thing is happening every week.

Cousins made some awful decision Sunday. The Pick Six was a disaster than he should have thrown away and his interception in the end zone was rough as well. The team will probably stick with Cousins until they re out of the playoff race. but games like this will not quiet the Fernando Mendoza chatter.

The Raiders fell behind early, so that hurt the run attack. But when the did run the ball, like as usual, the Raiders didn't get much going. The Raiders had just 24 yards rushing. Ashton Jeanty and Mike Washington each ran the ball six times. Jeanty had 25 yards and Washington had minus-3. Last week, Washington ran the ball 25 times and Jeanty ran it twice.

The Raiders' trouble stopping the run continued as the 49ers had 190 yards on the ground. Quarterback Brock Purdy had 32 yards rushing on one drive.

Kubiak didn't say the Raiders won't trade any payaers by Tuesday's deadline, at 1 p.m. PT. It would be a shock if the Raiders made a big trade such as dealing Maxx Crosby (who said again after the game he wants to stay in Las Vegas) or Cousins. But a smaller deal could happen. Maybe they can get something for wide receiver Tre Tucker or safety Jeremy Chinn, who are a free agents after the season or the benched Jackson Powers-Johnson. Las Vegas general manager John Spytek has some decisions to make.

Wild, just wild. The quarter started with Kirk Cousins throwing a pick six to give the 49ers a three-possession lead. Then, the Raiders answered with a touchdown. Then, Treydan Stukes picked off Brock Purdy and returned it deep into 49ers' territory with the Raiders down 11. Then, Brock Bowers fumbled it brought back to the 49ers. Then, they were stopped on their own 40 on fourth and inches. Then, Michael Mayer fumbled into the end while trying to score and make it a one-score game. Wow, just wow.

Bowers had three receptions of 20 plus yards Sunday and e leads all NFL tight ends with 11. Remember, he missed two games this season. Bowers had seven catches for 111 yards and a score. Yes, he's a monster playmaker.

Crosby sacked Purdy in the second quarter. It was the second straight game Crosby had a sack and he has four on the season.

Raiders' free-agent pickup, linebacker Nakobe Dean had his fifth straight game with, at least, 12 tackles. He joins Denzel Perryman (2021) as a Raider to do that since 1994.

Dylan Laube is a weapon. The kick returner had a 54-yard return to 49ers' territory in the second quarter. This came a week after he had a 95-yard kick return for a score in a five-point Raiders' win. It was the first kick return for a touchdown in the NFL this season and the Raiders' first in 15 years. Laube enter Week 4, leading the NFL with a 34.1 kick return average, nearly three yards more than any other returner. He's fun.

Rookie, third-round pick, Trey Zuhn, started at right guard for the seconds tight week. He is the Raiders' third starter at the position this season as fellow recent high-draft picks, Powers-Johnson and Caleb Rogers, have both been benched. But Zuhn left the game in the third quarter with a concussion and was replaced by Rogers.

CB rotation continues:Darien Porter was back in the starting lineup, but rookie Jermod McCoy did play some in the second quarter. Rookie Zeke Masses played in the third quarter. This will likely continue.

Keenan starts:Rookie defensive tackle Tim Keenan started as the Raiders began the game with three defensive tackles, Keenan, Adam Butler and Foley Fatukasi. Keenan has been elevated from the practice squad the past three games. Thus, the next time he plays this season. He must be on the 53-man roster. So, he could be promoted soon.

Las Vegas wide receivers Dont'e Thornton and Malik Benson are both healthy scratches for the second straight week. They both may need an injury to occur to see the field in the foreseeable future. The fact they cut Cody White last week before bringing him back the next day, shows they feel like Thornton and Benson could get claimed on waivers if they cut them, so their roster spots may be relatively safe even though they're not playing.

The Raiders won the coin toss and deferred to get the ball in the second half. That is their normal practice when they win the coin toss. But two weeks ago. they won and they took the ball first.

Many Raiders' fans were in the stands at Santa Clara on Sunday. This was the Raiders' first game in the Bay Area since their last game in Oakland in 2019. Bay Area fans missed the Silver and Black.

#2737. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2734. Trump targets Slotkin in call for DOJ probe of alleged Democratic threats to Republicans

President Donald Trump said Saturday that he was ordering the Justice Department to investigate Democratic leaders over what he described as threats against Republican officials, donors and supporters, without identifying the alleged communications or providing evidence of criminal conduct.

"The Democrats are going around threatening a lot of people, including the Attorney General of the United States, and others, with Impeachment and many other forms of punishment, even though they admit they have no reason to do so," Trump said in a Truth Social post.

The alleged threats, the president said, are "extortion at the highest level," he wrote. "I am ordering the Department of Justice to look into this criminal act, immediately!"

In his post, Trump claimed that Democratic Sen. Elissa Slotkin of Michigan is planning to "go after all young people that believe in Republican Values."

Trump was apparently referencing a speech Slotkin made at a University of Chicago event on Oct. 13. At the time, Slotkin said: "I want young people coming out of school to say, 'Should I go work for the Trump administration?'...I want them to be like, 'Lord, that might be buying myself a ton of problems that I don't want. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Slotkin alleges that she, her family and her staff faced physical and legal intimidation after the posting of a November 2025 video in which Slotkin and other Democratic leaders called on the U.S. military to "refuse illegal orders" following the airstrikes targeting boats in the Caribbean.

On Nov. 20, Trump called the video treason and said "an example must be set."

The FBI contacted lawmakers, including Slotkin, days later. In February, Slotkin declined to sit for an interview the Justice Department requested about the video.

"The intimidation is the point, and I'm not gonna go along with that," the senator said in February. A federal grand jury later declined to indict the video participants.

Now, Trump alleges Democrats are threatening legal intimidation against Republican leaders and their supporters.

"Republicans should get smart, fast! Threatening letters have been sent to many people, all innocent, from Democrat Political Leaders," the president wrote.

The White House and Slotkin didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

#2729. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2727. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after U.S. Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether the administration still has support to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline-driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Late Saturday, U.S. Central Command said it disabled a St. Kitts-flagged ship after the tanker ignored multiple warnings and attempted to leave the region after visiting an Iranian port, according to media reports. Iran state media also reported on explosions on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, as well as several blasts occurring in the port of Jask. CNBC couldn't independently confirm the reports.

Several oil tankers have also come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, traded above $97 on Friday, while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump no longer faces future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution anyway.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11, and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

#2725. Meta chief strategy officer Dave Wehner leaves to join AI infrastructure startup Fireworks as CFO

Dave Wehner, Meta's chief strategy officer, is leaving the company for four-year-old AI startup Fireworks.

At Fireworks — an AI inference and model-training platform focused on open models that was valued at $17.5 billion during a Series D fundraising round in July — Wehner will assume the CFO role, he announced on X on Friday.

Wehner will be tasked with scaling the company's finances and operations, Fireworks CEO Lin Qiao said in a statement to CNBC.

"Fireworks has the right mission for the moment," Wehner wrote on X, adding: "I've become increasingly bullish on what they are building. There's a remarkable depth of technical talent here, coupled with a very practical focus on what customers need."

The AI startup, founded in 2022 by former Meta and Google employees, is a platform that lets developers customize and run open models for their businesses. Its latest investment round was led by Atreides Management, Index Ventures and TCV, but Fireworks is also backed by Nvidia and Sequoia Capital. Business Insider reported that it is in early talks to raise funds again at a possible $30 billion to $40 billion valuation.

Wehner joined Meta, then called Facebook, in 2012 and was the company's chief financial officer from 2014 to 2022.

"Dave helped guide Meta through tremendous growth and complexity over 14 years, and brought disciplined thinking to some of our hardest company-level decisions," Meta COO Javier Olivan said in a statement to CNBC.

Fireworks is notably smaller than Meta — the tech giant has about a $1.56 trillion market capitalization as of Friday's close — and its competitors, which include OpenAI and Anthropic.

"Dave told me he wanted to get back to a smaller company where he could build toward a big impact again," Fireworks' Lin wrote in a LinkedIn post on Friday. "He sees AI as the defining technology shift of his lifetime and wants to help shape what comes next."

The company's size has not made it immune to the risks and complications often associated with AI technology. In October, the company found "a security incident involving unauthorized use of internal Fireworks credentials," in which "an unauthorized third party accessed environment variables used in evaluation jobs in a limited number of customer accounts," it said in a press release.

"Based on our investigation at this time, we have not detected evidence of unauthorized access to customer models, datasets, training data, personal information, or inference traffic within the Fireworks security perimeter or control," the release said.

#2724. Packers Snap Counts, Week 9: Green Bay shakes up the offensive line

The Green Bay Packers broke the 100-yard rushing mark on Sunday against the New England Patriots, in part because they changed up both their backfield and their offensive line. Will those changes stick moving forward? That hasn't been determined yet.

Let's get into how this Packers team looked different than in the past in our week's snap count breakdown. As a reminder, these only include offensive and defensive snaps, not special teams.

Backup quarterback Tyrod Taylor got in for the second time this season, his first since he kneeled down twice against the Chicago Bears. Taylor operated the offense on the last few drives of the game, when the Packers had already put away the game. No quarterback controversy here.

The leading rusher on the night was Kaleb Johnson, who has been a healthy scratch as of lately. Johnson was pushed into action because of MarShawn Lloyd's knee injury, and he split time on the field about 50-50 once starter Josh Jacobs left the game with a fractured finger.

Between Lloyd, Jacobs and Chris Brooks, who only has two weeks left in his practice window, the Packers will need to make some tough decisions soon at the running back position. They're dealing with three injuries, have five roster-caliber players, can only carry four on the 53-man roster (at most) and can only carry three on the game-day 48-man roster. At some point, something is going to have to give here.

Former practice squadder and current punt returner Coleman Owen got his first looks on offense in garbage time. He had not played on offense up to this point in the season. Owen was also pulled off the kickoff return unit, allowing Bo Melton to be the top returner with J. Michael Sturdivant as the "off-returner."

Belton had a 48-yard return that highlighted the night in the kicking game, while Owen had a 13-yard punt return.

Oh, yeah. Christian Watson and Matthew Golden went off.

There's nothing special about the tight end rotation this week, other than Josh Whyle being out with an injury. Last week, the Packers decided to roster only three tight ends on game day for the first time all season, and they don't seem to want to do that again. Instead, they brought up Drake Dabney from the practice squad to help replace Whyle's snaps on special teams. While Dabney didn't play on offense, he did play six snaps in the kicking game.

The Packers played all eight of their offensive linemen today, with their last two — Mekhi Becton and Jager Burton — only getting in when Tyrod Taylor hit the field. For what it's worth, Becton played left tackle in his reps. In total, 13 offensive linemen have now played for Green Bay's offense this season.

The big story here, though, is that the Packers are back to a guard rotation, which they have been famous for in previous years. This time, though, they're moving around three guards for two spots, not just splitting reps at one spot between two guards.

Before garbage time, Green Bay played two drives where Laken Tomlinson played left guard and Kevin Zeitler played right guard. Aaron Banks was on the bench for those looks. Otherwise, the team played Banks at left guard, pushed Tomlinson at right guard and Zeitler was on the bench. So the Banks vs. Zeitler rotation also comes with Tomlinson flip-flopping sides to get them on the field, too.

For a staff that tried to sell us on continuity on the line early in the year when the guards were at their worst under Jacob Monk and Donovan Jennings, they seem to have thrown that playbook out entirely. A guard musical chairs is going on, and we've already seen six players suit up at those two spots this season.

Who even knows which eight players are going to be up on game days when Sean Rhyan and Travis Glover are finally back healthy? I'm done guessing about who the line will be. We'll find out on Sundays.

Again, nothing too shocking here. Campbell and Riley are going to be the lower defensive linemen on the team's depth chart until the likes of Warren Brinson and/or Jonathan Ford can return from the injured reserve.

It remains close to an even split for all four of the Packers' top edge defenders, something we've seen throughout the year. Can this keep up whenever Micah Parsons returns to the lineup? I doubt it.

After just two defensive snaps, injury replacement starter Ty'Ron Hopper left the game with a chest injury, which head coach Matt LaFleur was not asked about in the post-game press conference. Officially, the Packers ruled him out of action, and he never returned to the game.

At the end of the game, Isaiah McDuffie, the team's replacement starter at the Mike position, left with an undisclosed injury, too. Forcing Kristian Welch, the team's sixth linebacker option, into the game.

Keep an eye on the injury report this week, as the Packers cannot afford to be out Zaire Franklin, Edgerrin Cooper, McDuffie and Hopper. If those four can't go, they might need to call up practice squad linebacker Jack Dingle and sign a player off the street.

Carrington Valentine got one rep as a dime defender (a second slot) in the first half, but otherwise his snaps and Benjamin St-Juste's snaps came in garbage time, when Keisean Nixon and Brandon Cisse were pulled out of the game. Kamal Hadden's snaps all game as a slot defender, when safety Javon Bullard was getting worked on.

Kitan Oladapo played some safety in garbage time and when Evan Williams was out. Williams had the air knocked out of him at one point, but he returned to action.

At the end of the game, it was Hadden in the slot with Williams and Oladapo playing the high safety looks, due to their lack of bodies at the position. Ultimately, the team allowed Xavier McKinney and Javon Bullard, who was in and out of the game twice, to rest.

#2722. Charles Bassey’s play is turning heads in Golden State

Every season I activate this trope to try and inspire a wary Dub Nation who wonders why we don't replace (insert suspect player) with someone else in the league. You're in the back seat of your mom's car, those sweet sweet Golden Arches come into view, and you make your pitch: "Mom, can we PLEAAASEEE get McDonald's?"

And without missing a beat, back comes the dreamcrushing sentence masquerading as a better suggestion: "We got McDonald's at home."

What's at home is never McDonald's, but it's ground beef, sandwich bread, ketchup, mustard, and relish. You eat it and more often than not depending on who your mama is, it hits. Your tastebuds are awash with flavor and gratitude. Maybe mom was right, maybe you do have McDonalds at home.

That's Charles Bassey for the Warriors right now. When Golden State asked for a real center, the front office looked at the cap sheet and injury report, and said, "we got a center at home."

And maybe the Warriors front office is right, maybe we DO have a big man at home!

And that is quite the development considering the hope of Kristaps Porziņģis dominating as the big man for the Warriors has effectively died as it looks like he's retiring from Warriors basketball. The team re-signed him this summer even though he'd missed 90 games over the past two seasons with three different teams.

Then there's Al Horford, who is 40 years old and dealing with a body banged up from years of playoff warfare. Horford is a professional basketball genius. But asking him to play 30 minutes a night in November is not gonna have him available for any kinda playoff run in the spring.

And of course the Warriors can always counter with the grizzled, undersized legend Draymond Green at center. That works until Draymond does Draymond things and gets ejected two minutes into the third quarter, which brings us to the night the center at home showed up.

On Nov. 2 against the Suns, Golden State trailed by 17. Draymond had been thrown out for body slamming one Phoenix player and quasi strangling Dillon Brooks (rightfully so).

That's when Bassey had a career night, scoring 19 points on 7-of-10 shooting, grabbed 8 rebounds and finished +10. After Draymond's ejection, the Warriors outscored Phoenix 59-45. He also built a connection with rookie Yaxel Lendeborg. Four of Lendeborg's five assists that night went to Bassey. Every time the rookie lofted the ball near the rim, Bassey caught it and finished.

After the game, Bassey called out the Suns for "fake toughness." The center at home has a little seasoning on him, don't he?

This wasn't a one-night fluke. The Warriors started 0-3, then won seven straight to get to 7-3, and Bassey was in the middle of it:

He has a +34 plus-minus over the seven-game streak, and +33 in the five games where he played real minutes

His 15.7 rebounds per 36 minutes ranks second in the NBA

He's shooting 63.6% from the field and 88.2% from the line, with a 25.8 PER.

Over the streak he's averaging roughly 21 points, 15 rebounds and nearly 3 blocks per 36 minutes.

Ummm ladies and gentlemen, he's really balling in his role right now. Here's the case for more Bassey minutes: in the Orlando game, he played only 11. The Magic won the offensive rebounding battle 24-3 and outscored Golden State 33-2 on second-chance points. The Warriors still won in overtime, because Steph Curry is a supernatural force of nature on the basketball court.

Bassey isn't Porziņģis, a guy who stretches the floor to 28 feet or make anyone's All-Star ballot. He's 6-foot-9 and focused completely on rebounding, rim-running and blocked shots. He's also available to play, and we all know the best ability is availability.

Warriors GM Mike Dunleavy may still trade for a center; I absolutely think he should. Using Porziņģis' contract to match salaries for a real big makes sense, and they have a real chance to pull it off before the trade deadline.

Until then, Dub Nation asked for McDonald's and got told they had a center at home, and so far the one at home is providing a lot of sustenance.

#2720. Brands are sponsoring IRL communities. Some experts say that could spell their end

On a brisk Saturday in October, more than 80 people try to shuffle into the small Corner Bookstore in New York City's Upper East Side. They're part of local literary community Lit Club NYC's quarterly book crawl. Today's event, where some attendees are "slightly over sci-fi" and others are in their "rom-com era," is sponsored by Tinder.

The Lit Club-Tinder partnership was facilitated by Offline, a platform for connecting hosts of gatherings across the U.S. with advertisers keen to sponsor their events. The goal is to help advertisers "deliver outsized media performance and drive growth," according to Offline's website. Offline founder Andrew Roth, 28, is also keen to help hosts ensure their gatherings are sustainable long term.

Kelly Chervin is just such a host. Chervin, 35, founded Lit Club in March 2025 as a way to meet other Latina readers and connect over literature. But she has bills to pay. "If we want to have a literary community be around sustainably," says Chervin, "then paid partnerships are part of that."

For the Tinder collab, Chervin makes $20 per attendee, and must throw in deliverables like social media posts highlighting the partnership. On Lit Club's Instagram, a recent post included the line "You can now discover events like these on the @tinder app!"

Whether community members will stay connected when a sponsor gets attached remains to be seen. Some experts are optimistic about the opportunity this kind of marriage presents, while others are certain it will eventually bring about communities' demise.

Either way, many intimate gatherings around the country are quickly adding a sales pitch.

Roth began his career by founding Gen Z-focused research and consulting company DCDX while studying at Vanderbilt University in 2020. The company has worked with major brands including Chipotle, Hinge and Spotify and was recently acquired by Visible Ventures for an undisclosed sum. Roth will stay on as a Board Advisor.

In 2024, while still at DCDX, Roth had the opportunity to work on an advertising campaign for fragrance company Le Monde Gourmand at in-person events with communities like those on which Offline now focuses.

He says it made him wonder: "Can we bring those [advertising] dollars into the real world and help these communities be preserved?" In September 2025, he and fellow entrepreneur Chris Banks officially launched Offline.

The company's platform enables hosts of gatherings across the country, from mom groups to run clubs to literary circles, to create a profile including details about how often events happen and how many people tend to show up. Brands can then use the site to match with profiles and events that fit their target audience and create a brief for a campaign that hosts can submit ideas to.

Offline now features more than 900 communities altogether, says Roth, with hosts making an average payout of $2,500 to $3,000 per event.

"The language we always use is 'intimacy at scale,'" he says.

For hosts like Chervin, the opportunity to generate revenue is critical. Lit Club's current income streams include ticketed events, a monetized newsletter and brand partnerships.

Chervin is choosy about the partnerships she takes on, she says. They have to feel organic. The Tinder tie-in, for instance, checks a couple of boxes: "Tinder really wants to move to in-person connection," she says as an example, which is at the core of her mission as well.

Michael Lombardo, 43, discovered bird watching in 2019 and was immediately struck by how fun and present the activity was. It made him feel "like the eight-year-old version of myself," he says, "where I was actually inhabiting the world."

Lombardo founded the McGolrick Bird Club in April 2023. Named for Brooklyn, New York's McGolrick Park, the free, weekly meetup convenes every Saturday morning, when he and others go bird watching. The event now has about 100 weekly attendees, and many of them are repeat guests.

Lombardo recently started dabbling with brand partnerships and connected with Offline earlier this year. For him, any partnership would have to benefit the community in some way, he says. But as a freelance graphic designer and writer, it's something he'll consider given the bigger picture of his life and priorities.

"I do need to support my family," he says. "I have two children."

Experts differ on what they think introducing the element of advertising to the groups could mean for the human connection they provide.

Amit Kumar, an assistant professor of marketing, psychological and brain sciences at the University of Delaware, is hopeful about the potential of these events, regardless of a brand's involvement. For him, the point is to create as many opportunities as possible for people to meet.

"As long as they actually are allowing people to engage with one another and connect with one another," he says, "there's a potential benefit to that."

Professor of sociology at Cornell University Benjamin Cornwell is not so sure. A community that got together organically through a genuine, shared interest is no longer organic once fueled and harnessed by a profit-motivated company, he says.

When that happens, community organizers risk introducing unwelcome messaging. Members may "lose faith in the original purpose," he says, adding that some will even "drop out." That won't necessarily happen immediately, he says. But, "it's inevitable."

Back at the book crawl, attendees' opinions differ about Tinder's involvement.

Some think it's cool Tinder is branching out into IRL events. Others don't like the implication that the event is somehow a ruse to meet a significant other, rather than a way to just meet people in general. Some people don't mind as long as the company doesn't feel too pushy in its agenda.

In September, Chervin partnered with an AI company at one of her events and appeared in an ad for Meta's AI tool Muse.

But among her larger community, she says, "a few people did tell me they don't think they can come back."

Roth stands by the financial ecosystem Offline is trying to build.

"If 10 years from now people look at us and say, 'Oh, these are the evil guys that bastardized IRL,' so be it," he says. "But I'll look at that and say, you know, we paid out a billion dollars to organizers, and we've created I don't know how many more events."

#2716. Jordan Love, Packers offense give Patriots' $135 million cornerback nightmares

The passes arc with a sense of predetermination these days when Jordan Love uncorks another deep ball.

The intended target is certainly going to have a chance to catch the ball, and odds are he has some degree of separation against the doomed coverage. What's less of a guarantee is whether the pass catcher will perform his duty and complete the grab, but that was not an issue during the Packers' 45-27 victory over the New England Patriots on Sunday, Nov. 8.

The numbers are cartoonish, as Green Bay put up a season high in points and total yards (478) – the worst rushing team in the NFL entering the week scored three times on the ground and ran for 129 yards with running back Josh Jacobs sidelined for most of the game after suffering a broken finger.

Love finished with 372 yards passing and three scores through the air and added a rushing touchdown to kick off the scoring 101 seconds into the game to create a well-balanced stat sheet. The one-two punch in the wide-receiver room of Christian Watson and Matthew Golden showed out with a combined 17 receptions on 18 targets for 335 yards and two touchdowns.

"It's bombs away," NBC's Jason Garrett said during "Football Night in America."

Fellow NBC analyst Mike Tomlin said it was time to consider the tandem among the elite duos in the league "at the tip-top of the food chain."

The performance put Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel in position to criticize one of his best players in cornerback Christian Gonzalez. Signed to a four-year, $135 million extension that made him one of the richest corners in the NFL, Gonzalez was targeted by Love 11 times. He was successful on 10 of those occasions, for 187 yards and a touchdown.

"When your best players don't play well, it's hard to win in this league," Vrabel said.

The 2025 Coach of the Year continued: "We expect a lot more. He expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him. Again, we got to be able to coach and be prepared, and then we have to execute. Just like it is every single week when we don't win, it's all of us, and it starts with me."

By the end of the first quarter, Golden had 126 receiving yards. Love completed seven passes that went for 24 yards or more and averaged 12.8 yards per attempt. He was 6-for-8 on deep passes (20+ air yards) for 235 yards and a touchdown, according to Next Gen Stats.

It marked the second time Love completed six deep passes this season (Week 5 against the Chicago Bears was the other) – no other quarterback in the NFL has done it once in 2026. Love said it felt like he was "flowing" while dropping back and throwing.

"It feels like you're in that flow state and just finding ways to get guys the ball ... it's a great feeling, and you just want to keep chasing every play and keep making good plays," Love told reporters. "I think I made some good throws, but I think guys are making pretty good catches as well. We've just got to keep building on that. Christian and 'MG,' I looked at their stat line after the game, it was just crazy. It's crazy to see that, and I think they're balling right now. So we've just got to keep finding ways to get them the rock. "

More: Tom Brady 'shocked' to learn mom of Green Bay Packer was his babysitter

Golden tagged Gonzalez for seven catches and 105 yards, while Watson handed him 82 yards over three catches, per Next Gen Stats. A single defender surrendering more than 80 yards to a pair of teammates in one game has not happened since at least 2018, also per Next Gen Stats.

LaFleur said any appearance of going after Gonzalez was more related to attacking specific coverages.

"When you got a quarterback playing the way Jordan Love's playing," said LaFleur, who appeared eager to widen the margin of victory against Vrabel, his former boss with the Tennessee Titans. "(I'm) just dialing up the plays.

"Just calling it. I can't say enough about it. Those are low-percentage throws in theory, but he's making them look high-percentage. I mean, they're right on the money."

Love left Gillette Stadium, where Tom Brady called his first Patriots game as a broadcaster, to chants of "MVP!" from visiting and well-traveled Packers fans.

"It's definitely cool," Love said of the recognition, "but just got to keep going and really just block everything else out and keep focusing on what I can do as an individual and to be able to help our team just keep getting better."

#2714. Is Luka Dončić playing tonight? Injury update on Lakers star vs. Pistons

The Los Angeles Lakers are tasked with facing one of the NBA's best defenses in the Detroit Pistons in the first of their four-game road trip on Sunday, Nov. 8. They'll have the added challenge of doing so without the driving force of their offense.

Luka Dončić has been ruled out with lower back tightness, the team announced. Dončić was listed as questionable on Saturday, one day after the leading MVP candidate dropped a season-high 53 points at home against the Portland Trail Blazers.

It's a blow to the Lakers (8-2), who will be without Dončić's 40.1 points, 7.8 rebounds and 7.7 assists per night in nine games played this season. He's missed one other game with back tightness.

Detroit (6-2), meanwhile, has given up an average of 101.8 points per game, fewest in the league.

Amid Dončić's historic stretch of seven consecutive 35-plus point games, the last four of which he's scored at least 40, head coach JJ Redick was asked if there was any danger in relying too much on the two-time scoring champion.

"Part of planning and team building and playing an 82-game season is that you're constantly assessing and trying to be better," Redick said on Friday. "... Let's say you're running a nine-man rotation or 10-man rotation. It's rare that you have each guy playing great. The goal is for each guy to play great, but that just doesn't happen so you're constantly tending to things. It's just the NBA.

" ... We have to rely on Luka because he's our best player, just like every other team relies on their best player. We still have to coach everyone else and coach the team."

The Lakers could be choosing to be proactive with getting rest for Dončić, as they travel to Atlanta for the second night of a back-to-back on Monday.

Dončić does have a history of back injuries during his career. Before this season, he was most recently listed with a back injury in March 2025. He was also listed with back-related injuries during the 2019, 2021 and 2024 seasons.

Dončić entered his third season with the Lakers as the unquestioned leader. He was ranked No. 4 on USA TODAY Sports' top 100 player rankings ahead of the 2026-27 season and is currently No. 1 on Lorenzo Reyes' MVP power rankings.

#2713. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2711. Berkshire Hathaway more than doubles buybacks in third quarter as earnings decline

Berkshire Hathaway said Saturday it accelerated its share repurchases during the third quarter, as operating and overall profits took a hit.

The Greg Abel-led conglomerate bought back $9.6 billion worth of Berkshire stock. That's more than double the $4.5 billion repurchased in the second quarter and brings the company's total buybacks for 2026 to about $14.4 billion.

The sharp increase came as Berkshire's operating earnings — those from wholly-owned businesses — fell year over year, as profits from its insurance business pulled back.

Berkshire operating profits totaled $12.705 billion, down 5.8% from $13.485 billion in the year-earlier period. Insurance underwriting tumbled 43% year on year to $1.352 billion, while insurance investment income declined 14% to $2.734 billion. Berkshire's railroad, energy and manufacturing businesses all saw earnings grow year over year.

The company's overall earnings, which include Berkshire's sprawling stock market portfolio, also dropped 28% in the third quarter to $22.112 billion from $30.796 billion a year prior. Investment gains for the company fell nearly 50% to $9.407 billion.

Despite the earnings pullback, Berkshire stock has outperformed recently amid a massive rotation out of technology stocks. Class A shares are up more than 12% over the past month, while the S&P 500 has dropped 5.5% in that time. Year to date, Berkshire has gained 13%, while the broad market index is up just 7.8%.

Berkshire's five largest equity holdings remained the same in the third quarter: Alphabet, American Express, Apple, Bank of America and Coca-Cola.

Berkshire's cash hoard also grew 3% in the third quarter $377.435 billion from $365.514 billion in the second quarter. However, excluding rail and utility cash along with T-bill payables, the conglomerate's cash holdings totaled $352.3 billion, down 1.9% quarter over quarter.

— CNBC's Alex Crippen contributed reporting.

#2708. Another QB injury: Drew Lock’s status ‘up in the air’ for Week 10 vs. Raiders

It might be Jalen Milroe time for the Seattle Seahawks.

While Drew Lock didn't miss any game action during Seattle's 17-3 win over the Arizona Cardinals, he has a new injury on top of the severe tendinitis he reportedly had been battling over the past couple of weeks. Lock was seen grabbing his right hip area during the third quarter of Sunday's game, grimacing and walking gingerly as he got off the field prior to Seattle's final scoring drive. Jalen Milroe warmed up on the sidelines while Lock went into the blue tent, but Lock stayed in for the rest of the afternoon and didn't miss a play.

Seahawks coach Mike Macdonald told reporters at the post-game press conference that Lock's status is "up in the air" after getting his oblique looked at.

"We're looking at him for an oblique, so we'll assess it and go from there," Macdonald said.

"He made it out [of the game], but you know, like I've sat here before and told you a guy would be ready and then he wasn't. So, leave my options open."

For what it's worth, Lock said "we'll get it looked at [Sunday night]" and that it "was not too bad." When asked if the injury affected him, he said, "I wouldn't say it did."

Lock also said he felt "good" playing through the elbow injury.

Odds are not sounding favorable for Sam Darnold to return against the Las Vegas Raiders, with ESPN's Adam Schefter reporting what felt like a pretty obvious situation that he is in "jeopardy" of missing Week 10 with quad and rib injuries. If Darnold was listed as "week-to-week" by Mike Macdonald, this isn't earth-shattering news as much as it affirms what's already been said.

If the news is negative on Lock, then we could see Jalen Milroe's first career NFL start. But at the minimum, this is a very banged up Seahawks quarterback room and the bye week might be one of the most welcome byes in recent Seahawks history.

#2706. Anthropic IPO meets AI backlash after midterms show stinging rebuke of data centers

Americans were unequivocal at the ballot box this week as they expressed disdain for tech billionaires and mistrust of artificial intelligence. With that backdrop, Anthropic is about to try to go public.

The AI company is gearing up for a potentially historic IPO, likely targeting mid-November for its Nasdaq debut. CNBC reported on Friday that Anthropic investors see the company aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

From Wall Street to Main Street, it's becoming a tougher environment for Anthropic. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, and it's down to start November after October marked its worst month since March 2025. Chipmakers and neoclouds, the companies most closely tied to the AI buildout, are getting hit the hardest.

Voters rebuked AI in Tuesday's midterm elections, while sending Democrats to sweeping congressional victories across the country. Candidates who advocated for more stringent AI regulation won handily in states like Michigan, Ohio, Texas and Georgia, which will all now have Democratic governors, and local communities opposed data center-related ballot measures in 40 out of 41 cases.

More than half of voters said AI data centers were a "crucial" or "major factor" in who they supported for Congress, according to an exit poll from NBC News.

"People don't want a data center going near them if it's just going to house AI that's going to help a big tech company profit," Maura Austin, a behavioral scientist at the University of Virginia, told CNBC. "What Anthropic is doing when they're going public with such a high valuation is they're really leaning into that big tech, billionaire kind of caricature. I expect that's not going to go well with the public."

Anthropic has long tried to position itself as a safety-conscious company that takes AI's potential risks and benefits seriously. CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company alongside a handful of researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

The company's enterprise focus has helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI, leaving that distinction to OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman. But with Anthropic poised to become the first pure-play AI company to hit the public market, minting a number of new billionaires and many more millionaires in the process, a heightened level of scrutiny is coming.

"Being a public company, just that by itself pushes you in the limelight," Reena Aggarwal, a professor of finance at Georgetown University, said in an interview. "And then AI is such a hot-button issue that Anthropic will be in the limelight even more."

Matt Kennedy, a senior IPO market strategist at Renaissance Capital, said that's not necessarily a bad thing for Anthropic.

Public markets force greater transparency, he said, and the company could attract new users through its debut. And since AI has become such a mainstream conversation topic, several private companies are already household names.

"I would say that a public company does not deserve more criticisms than a comparable private company," Kennedy said. "So Anthropic should not be put in a harsher public spotlight than OpenAI just because it's public."

AI backlash accelerated over the summer after a series of high-profile safety and security incidents rattled the public. As concerns mounted, the debate over data centers intensified, while tech's hyperscalers simultaneously boosted their forecasts for capital expenditures, each reaching into the hundreds of billions of dollars.

Anthropic and OpenAI depend on state-of-the-art data centers to train models and serve customers, and they're trying to build out capacity as quickly as possible to meet demand. Anthropic said in an amended IPO prospectus this week that it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Americans grappling with inflation and stubbornly high gas prices have grown worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, a proposed data center in their backyard became the physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy toward AI.

In the risk factors section of its prospectus, Anthropic said opposition to data center development has become more organized across jurisdictions, and that the company's public profile could draw particular scrutiny to projects associated with it.

Nicole Greene, an analyst at Gartner, said she expects that Anthropic's executives have long been prepared to contend with AI backlash, especially given the disruptive nature of the technology.

"There's going to be big emotions," Greene told CNBC. "With big emotions and feelings — fear and excitement — comes heightened scrutiny."

Amodei acknowledged the souring sentiment around AI in a post on X in August. He said it's the result of a "crisis of trust," and that people believe companies, the government and the tech industry are always "cooking up some new way to screw them over."

Rebuilding public confidence is going to be an uphill battle as the company begins providing quarterly financial updates.

The University of Southern California published a survey on Monday that found that 71% of respondents don't trust big tech companies to act in society's best interest "at all" around data center development.

"Once you've lost trust, it's hard to gain it back," Wändi Bruine de Bruin, a professor at the University of Southern California's Schaeffer Institute, said in an interview. "The numbers are not good."

#2703. Why the prognosis for healthcare stocks is uncertain ahead of Congress’ new makeup

Healthcare stocks may struggle to gain momentum as Democrats prepare to take over Congress.

Jared Holz, who covers healthcare for Mizuho, says the outlook for the sector is uncertain because of likely gridlock between lawmakers.

"The next couple of years I really can't see too much happening," the firm's sector specialist told CNBC's "Fast Money" on Wednesday. "I'm not sure you want to own everything, but you can pick and choose the names… Pharma is trading more expensive than tech for the first time in 20 years. So, I backed away from pharma. I would look at medtech."

The State Street Health Care Select ETF (XLV) jumped 3% a day after Election Day.

"You had this very weird situation where typically you wouldn't have all of these categories work in tandem, and they did. So, I think some things just a little bit off," said Holz, a day before healthcare got swept up in Thursday's Wall Street sell-off.

According to Holz, healthcare's real booster shot may come if a Democrat wins the White House in 2028.

"Then I think you see some of the Trump strategies rolled back. That's where I think healthcare could actually perform well," said Holz, who added it hinges on Democrats retaining the Senate and House.

For investors who are long on healthcare, Holz said each subsector should be treated differently.

"Can we get some of the regulation that has taken place over the past couple of years and overturn that over the near term? I don't necessarily believe so," Holz said. "But if you do, then you probably want to be long some of these medical device stocks that are at or near multi-year lows."

The State Street Health Care Select ETF ended virtually flat this week. It's up around 32% since President Trump took office in January 2025 — beating the S&P 500 by almost 9 percentage points.

#2702. Patriots’ best players fail to deliver in blowout loss to Packers

NFL teams are only as good as their best players.

For the New England Patriots, that is quarterback Drake Maye and cornerback Christian Gonzalez. But during Sunday's 45-27 loss to the Green Bay Packers, neither played up to expectations.

"When your best players don't play good, you don't have much of a chance in this league. So, we expect a lot more," head coach Mike Vrabel said.

Gonzalez struggled against Packers wide receivers Matthew Golden and Christian Watson. After never allowing more than 71 yards in a game to a single receiver, both Packers wideouts went for 80-plus yards with Gonzalez in coverage.

In total, Gonzalez surrendered a career-high 10 receptions for 187 receiving yards on Sunday after he allowed just 196 yards in coverage through his first six games this season.

"Obviously, it is frustrating. I want to be who I am for the team, and everybody that knows what I am. But, it's football. I'm going to have a bad game," Gonzalez said. "So, it's the same as I come up here week in, week out. If it's a good game, I say, 'All right. We move on.' It's just like that."

Vrabel added: "We expect a lot more. He expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him."

On the offensive side of the ball, Maye was not his usual self under center.

His first pass of the afternoon was intercepted by Packers safety Xavier McKinney, setting up a Green Bay touchdown just two plays later. The turnover put New England in an early 7-0 hole less than two minutes into the game.

"I'm disappointed in myself for, you can't put the team in the hole straight off the bat, first possession, first half," Maye said.

That was a lead the Packers never surrendered, as Maye threw two additional interceptions in the loss, one that he finished on the bench.

"I feel like in the times we had chances to get back into the game, I messed them up, and we had chances to put us back within three points multiple times, and just frustrating," Maye said. "I got to do a better job from a whole perspective, communication, doing the little things right, and I just, I wasn't good. I wasn't good today. And we got to bounce back."

#2699. In pictures: France protests draw 146,000 as clashes erupt for fourth straight weekend

Protesters clashed with police in France on Saturday as demonstrations over living costs and public services entered a fourth consecutive weekend and drew students, trade unions and members of the revived "Yellow Vest" movement onto the streets.The country's Interior Ministry counted 146,000 demonstrators nationwide, including 46,000 in Paris. A youth union alliance put attendance at 250,000 nationally and 75,000 in the capital. At least 360 people were arrested across the country, AFP reported, citing a ministry tally.

Police used tear gas against people throwing projectiles as the main Paris march ended at Place de la Nation, per AFP. Garbage cans were set alight as protesters and police clashed in several cities, according to Le Monde.

Police blocked a separate, banned Yellow Vest gathering at Place de la Concorde and arrested two movement leaders, Le Monde reported.

The Yellow Vests emerged in 2018 in opposition to fuel-tax increases, before broadening into a movement protesting high living costs and the policies of President Emmanuel Macron.

The turnout was lower than on the previous three weekends. It was also below union-led demonstrations on Nov. 5, when the Interior Ministry counted 575,000 people nationwide.

The protests began in late September with high-school students demanding better conditions, before university students and unions joined.

This weekend's protests come after French lawmakers rejected the revenue section of the 2027 budget, as the government wrestles with addressing public anger while trying to rein in its finances.

The government's original proposal now goes to the Senate, which is scheduled to begin debating it Nov. 20.

Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu is due to meet labor and employer representatives Monday to discuss wages and purchasing power.
