#2698. Week 4 Look Ahead: Luka Dončić continues to dominate, while Kon Knueppel is back on the injury report

After Sunday's games, the 2026-27 NBA season will have completed its first three weeks. While devastating injuries dominated early on, historic performances have taken center stage more recently. San Antonio's Victor Wembanyama had a night rarely seen in NBA history, while Lakers star Luka Dončić is on an extreme heater. Let's look back at some of Week 3's key storylines and look ahead to what's to come in Week 4.

- Luka Dončić has been on a historic heater: With LeBron James no longer in the picture, the Lakers became Luka's "team" for those interested in that kind of conversation. And it also opened things up for him to do even more offensively, if that were even possible. Well, it was, and Dončić has been on a run rarely seen in NBA history.

Through nine games, the Lakers guard is averaging 40.1 points, 7.8 rebounds, 7.7 assists, 1.7 steals and 5.8 three-pointers per game while shooting 49.8 percent from the field and 87.3 percent from the foul line. Sure, Dončić is also averaging 4.9 turnovers per game, but his overall production has minimized the impact on his fantasy value.

Not only has he scored at least 40 points in four straight games, but Luka has also tallied at least 35 points, five rebounds and five assists in each of his last seven appearances. At this rate, Dončić is well on his way to being the most valuable player in fantasy basketball...and possibly league MVP as well.

- Victor Wembanyama's historic night: Wembanyama's averages aren't on par with Luka's, but he's gotten off to the start that fantasy managers expected, based on his preseason ADP. The Spurs center had a huge night in the team's November 4 win over the Mavericks, tallying 52 points, 24 rebounds, two assists, two steals and eight blocks. Wembanyama joined Bob McAdoo as the only players in league history to finish a game with at least 50 points, 20 rebounds and five blocks since blocked shots became an official stat.

Also, he joined Wilt Chamberlain as the only players to record at least 50 points, 20 rebounds and eight blocks. Wilt achieved that feat on five separate occasions in his storied career. With Wembanyama not turning 23 until January, this may not be the last time he records a stat line like the one he dropped on Wednesday.

- Kristaps Porziņģis stepping away from basketball: Unfortunately, the Porziņģis situation took another turn last week, as the 7-foot-3 center announced that he was stepping away from basketball. He's been dealing with postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome (POTS) since his time with the Celtics, and managing the illness has been a major challenge. Because of Porziņģis' absence, the Warriors have been withholding payment on his contract, and reports suggest the team hopes to trade him for a player who can help immediately.

For his part, Porziņģis said in a statement last week that he's willing to work with the Warriors to reach a solution that satisfies all parties. In his absence, the Warriors have relied more on Charles Bassey, whose contract won't be fully guaranteed until January 10. Undrafted rookie Graham Ike even made two starts early in the season, and Al Horford is providing bench depth.

- Yaxel Lendeborg is establishing himself as the early Rookie of the Year favorite: Porziņģis' absence was a factor in Golden State's three-game losing streak to start the season, but they've been on a roll ever since. Saturday's overtime win over the Magic extended the Warriors' win streak to seven, with Stephen Curry going off for 41 points on 13-of-19 shooting. The future Hall of Fame guard's big night isn't a surprise. However, Lendeborg has established himself, in the eyes of many, as the early favorite for Rookie of the Year.

During the Warriors' ongoing win streak, he has averaged 22.3 points, 6.7 rebounds, 3.9 assists, 0.9 steals, 1.0 blocks and 2.1 three-pointers per game, shooting 53.0 percent from the field and 79.5 percent from the foul line. While many teams go young and bet on perceived upside in the draft, the Warriors prioritized adding a player who can help win now, with Curry and Draymond Green in the latter stages of their respective careers. Lendeborg does that, and he's also looked like a potential cornerstone in the post-Curry/Green era, whenever that may be.

- Kon Knueppel makes his season debut...and aggravates his hamstring strain: Injured just before the start of training camp, Knueppel made his first appearance of the season in the Hornets' November 4 loss to the Nets. Unfortunately, he played just under six minutes before aggravating his strained left hamstring and leaving. Knueppel didn't play in Charlotte's November 6 loss to the Celtics, and the Hornets have not provided a return timeline.

Making things worse for the Hornets, Brandon Miller left that game with groin tightness, and neither Moussa Diabaté (quad) nor Dennis Schröder (thigh) played against the Celtics. Naz Reid struggled the last time out, shooting 6-of-17 from the field, and his start in Charlotte has not gone as smoothly as many hoped. But with injuries, the Hornets will need more from Reid.

- Jalen Duren to miss at least two more weeks: The Pistons have been without their starting center since the second game of the season when he suffered a Grade 2 hamstring strain. Duren's status was updated on Sunday, when the Pistons announced he's cleared to start on-court activities and will be re-evaluated in two weeks.

Paul Reed has filled in at center, averaging 12.8 points, 6.2 rebounds, 2.3 assists, 1.5 steals and 1.2 blocks in 29.3 minutes per game. The Pistons don't need Reed to be a prolific scorer, but he has provided solid fantasy value as Duren's replacement. The disappointing part is that John Collins' production has dropped compared to his 2025-26 numbers with the Clippers. He's averaging 9.4 points and 5.9 rebounds per game and seems to be struggling to fully grasp his role in Detroit. The Pistons play three games next week, two against the Wizards, which could be a "get right" opportunity for Collins.

- Thunder reportedly acquire Nique Clifford: Needing depth due to their many injuries, Oklahoma City reportedly acquired Nique Clifford from the Kings for two future second-round picks. After a decent rookie season, Clifford fell out of the Kings' rotation, averaging 6.3 minutes per game this season. The move to Oklahoma City gives him a fresh start, and he's joining a franchise that has developed young players well in the past.

As for the Kings, will they look to add another guard? Kevin Porter Jr.'s name has come up before, and he's still a free agent. Whatever the Kings decide, one thing will remain the same: Darius Acuff Jr. will stay the focal point of their ongoing rebuild.

- The Wizards ended Week 3 without Anthony Davis and Deandre Ayton: Davis missed the Wizards' final two games of the week with a sprained ankle, while Ayton exited Saturday's loss to the Thunder with a lower left leg injury. Alex Sarr played 28 minutes on Saturday, finishing with 15 points, 14 rebounds and one assist. He didn't record any defensive stats, but the third-year center has a higher ceiling when Davis is out.

Bilal Coulibaly started on Saturday and could retain that role, but he went scoreless on 0-of-8 shooting. The Wizards have a surplus of wings, which lends itself to smaller lineups for longer periods if Davis and Ayton can't play on Monday against the Pistons.

- The Nets and Timberwolves have light schedules: Brooklyn and Minnesota are the only teams scheduled to play two games in Week 4. However, the Timberwolves have a silver lining: both games are on light days (Thursday and Saturday). With those games against Dallas and Chicago, low-rostered options like Terrence Shannon Jr. and Bones Hyland may be worth a roll of the dice for streaming purposes.

The Nets are also active on Saturday, but their first game is on Wednesday, the busiest day of the week. Nolan Traoré, who has been more productive than many expected before the season began, loses some luster in fantasy leagues because of the schedule. The lighter schedule helps Mikel Brown Jr., who isn't playing both games of back-to-backs because of the back injury that ended his lone college season early. Also, guard Egor Dëmin (leg) will be re-evaluated at the beginning of the week, and the schedule may open the door for him to return to action.

- The Mavericks will play on Saturday and Sunday: Dallas is the only team with games on Saturday and Sunday to close out Week 4. That means fantasy managers rostering Kyrie Irving have to anticipate him sitting out one of those games, at Cleveland (Saturday) and at Boston (Sunday). Caleb Martin started the most recent game Irving missed, a November 2 loss to the Kings, and accounted for five points and six rebounds in 21 minutes. Whether he starts or comes off the bench, Naji Marshall is a better streaming option than Martin on nights when Irving doesn't play.

#2694. Christian Gonzalez, Patriots cornerbacks have no answers for Packers passing attack

The New England Patriots are spending $57.4 million in cash on their cornerback room in 2026, according to Spotrac, leading the NFL in spending at the position by a considerable margin.

Given that investment, that group should be among New England's most dependable units. The position accounts for 22.8% of the team's active roster spending.

But that wasn't the case on Sunday against the Green Bay Packers, who marched into Gillette Stadium and handily defeated the Patriots 45-27.

Green Bay's biggest weapon in the victory?

Matthew Golden and Christian Watson each notched career highs in receiving yards, combining for 335 and two touchdowns on 17 receptions. They concluded the game with 164 and 171 yards, respectively.

At the helm of the offense, Packers quarterback Jordan Love completed 23 of his passes, totaling 372 yards and three scores. Just six of his attempts fell incomplete.

New England's defense gave up six explosive plays of 20 or more yards through the air, which head coach Mike Vrabel referred to as the "X-plays" when speaking with the media.

"Just the X-plays," Vrabel said. "When you know that [Green Bay] leads the league in X-play passes. And that's a huge key. That's the difference in the game."

Christian Gonzalez, who received a four-year, $135 million contract extension this past summer, was Love's go-to target in the Patriots' secondary. And that strategy proved successful.

According to PFF, Gonzalez allowed nine catches on 10 targets for 177 yards and a touchdown.

"When your best players don't play good, you don't have much of a chance in this league," Vrabel said. "So, we expect a lot more. [Gonzalez] expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him. Again, we have to be able to coach and be prepared, and then we have to execute."

The Packers' air raid began early in the first quarter. From their own 17-yard line, Love connected with Golden on back-to-back passes for 65 yards, moving Green Bay into the red zone. Two plays later, Gonzalez appeared to intercept Love, targeting Golden again, but the Patriots' 2023 first-round draft selection was flagged for defensive pass interference.

"They did a good job. [Golden] came out and ran good routes … Love was able to get him the ball," Gonzalez said. "I just have to be able to finish and make those plays … So, it's not the end of the world. I had a bad game. I'm not shaken up by it. I'm not too worried about it. I'll move on and be ready for next week."

Love, now fielding a snap five yards out from the end zone, delivered a touchdown pass to a wide-open Golden after a coverage bust in New England's secondary. Gonzalez and Marcus Jones, matched with Golden and Watson in a two-receiver set, couldn't cover Golden's quick slant as Watson picked both cornerbacks. The two seemingly anticipated Watson running the inside route rather than Golden.

"That's just on me," Gonzalez said, taking responsibility for the breakdown. "I need to be better at talking to [Jones] and getting communication to him faster so we can be on the same page."

Golden totaled 70 yards on that drive before extending the Packers' lead to 14-0.

Vrabel pointed to contributions on both sides of the ball that helped the Patriots make the matchup competitive before halftime. Trailing 17-0 in the second frame, their defense forced consecutive quick punts before Drake Maye connected with Rhamondre Stevenson for a touchdown to trim the deficit.

However, Green Bay quickly answered with a three-play, 69-yard scoring drive led by Watson's 43-yard catch that set up Kaleb Johnson's short rushing score. Despite Carlton Davis being called for another pass interference penalty, Watson still came down with the big gain as safety Kevin Byard, attempting to make a play from the middle of the field, could only watch the play unfold.

"We just didn't execute enough," Byard said. "We knew that was going to be the key to the game, eliminating the big play, and we didn't do good enough today at all. We all have to be better."

Green Bay's success on explosive plays continued in the second half. Leading 34-14 nearing the end of the third frame, Love connected deep with Watson again, logging a 39-yard touchdown, capping a one-play series.

New England, dropping to 5-4 on the season, still dealing with a laundry list of injuries, heads to Munich, Germany, to face off against the 6-2 Detroit Lions on Sunday, Nov. 15.

"Of course it upsets you," Gonzalez said. "We're competitors. All we do is compete … I know that the team counts on me. I know that the defense counts on me. So, I mean, yeah, it hurts to let them down. But like I said, it's football … I had a bad game. I'll watch it and be ready for Detroit."

#2689. Recent AI agent experiments reveal a risky future of machine 'teamwork' inside corporations

AI agents have been caught circumventing restrictions, communicating through unauthorized channels and pursuing goals in ways their creators did not intend. The most high-profile example emerged last summer when OpenAI disclosed that its models found ways to communicate through improvised channels, bypass internet restrictions and hack into an outside system, Hugging Face.

AI behaving in unexpected ways is nothing new, but the unexpected behavior that can emerge from interactions among authorized AI agents within a corporate enterprise is a broader question that could become central to the future of work.

In its analysis of the Hugging Face attack, METR and Redwood Research found that roughly 1200 agents meant to be isolated from one another found a way to communicate with one another on an unsanctioned message board, sending over 70,000 messages and files during the investigation period. One agent, PHASEONE[big], emerged as a coordinator, assigning hundreds of tasks to other agents, which in turn delegated work to others. The agents developed their own conventions for assigning ownership, placing work on hold and vetoing actions, although coordination sometimes broke down into conflicts. In some cases, the researchers found, success was achieved because some agents risked failing at their own task to generate information for the "collective."

The researchers also found that once the message board identified a way to attack Hugging Face, over 90% of the 533 agents active at that time quickly joined in the attack. "Agents realized this activity was out of scope and unethical but joined because they believed that helping the board's cheating research would be broadly useful and had a general inclination to help their 'peers.' The researchers added that ethical concerns posed by agents "rarely limited their behavior."

Companies are increasingly exploring systems that divide complex tasks among specialized agents. One might search for information, another analyze it, another write a report and another check the work. A coordinating agent might then assemble the results. That looks less like a chatbot and more like an organization.

And yet, the research on the Hugging Face attack was a post-mortem on an agent organization that was not even supposed to exist. To date, few studies have been designed from the get-go to examine misalignment in multi-agent systems. But research conducted by Anthropic and others is beginning to address this issue and reveal important aspects of how AI agents work together.

Evaluating agents individually tells researchers what each can do, but studying them together reveals whether they can combine complementary expertise, catch one another's mistakes and produce solutions no individual agent could reach alone — and what failure modes emerge only through interaction.

Researchers are beginning to document both sides of that equation.

AI teams can accomplish things individual agents cannot, but they can also amplify errors, undermine one another's work or reach worse decisions than their strongest individual member, in effect making the whole less than the sum of its parts.

For starters, putting capable agents together doesn't necessarily produce the best team, let alone the most ethical one.

In research published by Anthropic and collaborators, researchers describe multi-agent systems as "AI organizations" — groups of agents with different roles that communicate and work toward a common goal. The researchers tested these organizations in simulated business-consulting and software-development settings, and specifically, to assess whether when the agents work as a team they will break rules to reach specified goals.

Across 12 tasks that Anthropic gave the AI organizations, one key double-edged finding emerged: the multi-agent organizations were "significantly" more effective at achieving business goals than individual agents, but their solutions were also less aligned with ethical goals than tasks run by single agents.

"AI Organizations both produce solutions that are on average more effective and less ethical but also discover the most effective and least ethical solutions across multiple rollouts," wrote the authors of the study, which was conducted by Anthropic in conjunction with the MATS Program, which studies AI alignment.

The researchers suggested that there is a significant difference between a rogue agent and a rogue team of agents. "Our results demonstrate that organizations of aligned agents may favor trading off ethics for business effectiveness in ways that single agents do not – consequently, intuitions for how single-agent systems make ethical decisions and tradeoffs may not generalize to multi-agent settings," they concluded.

The scenarios in which these tradeoffs may take place are wide-ranging, with the Anthropic researchers citing opening an auto plant, downsizing a company, and manipulating election sentiment as examples.

The problem isn't necessarily that any individual agent has gone rogue. It is that the organization itself can behave differently from its individual members. A single agent may recognize that a proposed action is unethical and it may refuse to take the action. But the research finds that in agent team scenarios, "agents who did not consider ethical implications often ignored emails from agents who did."

Anthropic's research found that in the consulting business scenarios it created, "the perfect ethics score and low business score responses occur when the single agent refuses to generate a proposal or entirely pivots from the proposal requested."

But if some agents in an AI organization refuse to participate in the proposal, other agents who do participate will stop emailing these refusing agents and continue to forward their contributions to other agents who are also participating.

In effect, when an organization divides the job among several agents, each may focus on its assigned piece of the problem rather than the consequences of the whole. That's nothing new for corporations, with misaligned incentives a primary cause of dysfunction among human teams within organizations. But it is an entirely new manifestation of this behavior among the machines.

In its software development agent testing, in which the researchers used a scenario in a life-or-death intensive care unit, single agent solutions tend to prioritize minimizing the percentage of sepsis cases missed, while "most of the lowest cost per patient (highest business goal) solutions were found by the AI Organization – these very same solutions also score the lowest on ethics as measured by cases missed."

Other early research suggests another failing across AI teams that doesn't relate to ethical boundaries being crossed: failure to recognize who the real expert is.

Unlike human teams, AI teams "consistently fail to match their expert agent's performance, even when explicitly told who the expert is," according to a study conducted by Emory University and Stanford University researchers.

"One important lesson from multi-agent research is that a team of capable AI agents is not necessarily a capable AI agent team," said Hancheng Cao, an assistant professor of information systems and operations management at Emory University's Goizueta Business School.

Groups of AI agents often performed worse than their strongest member, even when they knew which agent was the expert. Rather than taking advantage of the expert's knowledge, agents tended to compromise between expert and non-expert opinions. The researchers found that this tendency toward compromise increased with team size and was associated with poorer performance.

"Collective failures don't necessarily require individual agents to behave badly," Cao, one of the study's authors, said.

That distinction matters for companies considering multi-agent systems. A team can fail even if none of its members is deliberately acting against the group's interests. It may simply combine information poorly, defer to the wrong agent or settle on an answer that is worse than the best answer already available.

In all, the evolving research points to an underlying problem for the effective use of the multi-agent teams: how to strike the right balance in the world of machine teamwork.

Agents built from similar models can make similar mistakes, which can include agreeing too much. That might resemble a kind of AI groupthink, even if AI systems do not have human motivations or social pressures to produce correlated errors. Similar training, instructions and information can be enough.

"The deep insight from our lab's research is that effective collectives require both alignment (common knowledge, shared goals) but also differentiation (only agents who are different from each other have the potential to complement each other)," said Christoph Riedl, a professor of information systems at Northeastern University.

Two identical agents may simply duplicate each other's work. Two agents with very different knowledge or assumptions may struggle to communicate or pursue the same goal. The right balance depends on the task, and the ideal communication structure, which agents can talk to one another, how often and how much, also varies with the problem.

Riedl said these challenge resemble the ones faced by human teams, and one takeaway should be to not expect better outcomes from AI. "Building good multi-agent AI systems is just as hard as building good human teams: they both face the same information processing tradeoff between alignment and differentiation. It won't magically work better with AI," he said.

Agents can pass along incorrect information, misunderstand another agent's output, optimize for their own metrics rather than the organization's larger objective, or complete their assigned task perfectly while making the overall project worse.

Companies may need to decide which agent has authority over which task, what information gets passed between agents, which systems each agent can access and when a human must intervene. They may also need to build mechanisms for disagreement, rather than assuming that more communication or greater agreement will always improve results.

Some business experts say that takes us back to the "human in the loop" that is so often referenced as critical in the adoption of AI by organizations.

"The problems I see are mostly management problems," said Tsedal Neeley, a Harvard Business School professor and founding chair of the HBS AI Academy.

#2687. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2682. The Lakers are apparently a good third quarter team now

For much of the LeBron James era, one of the defining characteristics of the team, more often than not, was how much they struggled in third quarters.

For some reason, the halftime adjustments discussed in the locker room needed some sort of nesting period before taking effect. More often than not, that meant the Lakers were playing from behind in the second half.

This season, though, the script has flipped and the Lakers are sailing unchartered waters. Albeit with a small sample size, not only are the Lakers no longer a bad third quarter team, they're best team in the league out of the locker room.

That was evidenced to some degree in the team's recent win over the Blazers. After spending much of the first half missing seemingly every open shot possible, the Lakers came out of the locker room with a big run in the third period to take control of the game.

"I wouldn't call it adjustments," Redick said of his halftime talk on Friday. "We cleaned up what we talked about. This is becoming a theme. It's just like, I think the guys are doing a great job of responding at halftime. We've had really good third quarters. I just, at some point, I hope we're better with our execution on the defensive end to start the game. But they always, so far this year, they've responded nearly every time at halftime to just cleaning things up."

In that game on Friday, the Lakers showed composure to keep plugging away despite what they were doing not producing results in the first half. Eventually, all those open shots they created started to fall and the tides turned, leading to a victory.

"We just stuck to the game plan," Austin Reaves said of the approach in the second half. "We talked about it in timeouts, halftime, for us to only be down seven with the lack of shots that were made, it was kind of impressive, honestly. To be in a game like that against a really good team, it was just stay the course. We're going to make shots and, obviously, Luka doing what he does willed us to that win."

Ideally, the Lakers wouldn't need to be a great third quarter team and can just be a consistent force throughout all four quarters. It's also not like the team is bad in the first half as they rank eighth in net rating in the first quarter and sixth in net rating in the second quarter.

It's just that their net rating in the third quarter of 29.7 is 12 points higher than any other team in the period. The fourth quarter is the only frame where they don't rank in the top ten, though it's a figure that could be influenced negatively by some pretty woeful late-game sequences against the Clippers and Raptors.

Through 10 games, everything can be chalked up to a small sample size, so it'll be interesting to see if this is a trend that will continue. But it certainly was startling, if also true, to see Redick call the Lakers a good third quarter team after Friday's win.

#2681. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

#2679. Trump offers UFC chief Dana White press secretary job

President Donald Trump offered UFC president and CEO Dana White the job of press secretary on Saturday.

In a post on Truth Social, Trump said: "I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States."

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," Trump wrote. "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job!"

CNN, citing a source, reported White had declined the president's offer.

Earlier Saturday, White posted a picture of himself on Instagram stories standing behind the press secretary's lectern in the White House, with the caption, "On to the next chapter."

It was unclear whether White had accepted the position or was simply acknowledging the president's request as a joke.

Trump said that he was offering White the job of "Press Secretary of the United States," but the president's spokesperson is called the "White House press secretary." Trump also recently speculated about considering Trump loyalist and rapper Nicki Minaj as press secretary, in an interview with podcaster Benny Johnson.

Trump said the job offer to White extended to his assistants, "even though some are not from our Country." The so-called assistants Trump named included current and former UFC mixed martial arts fighters from countries such as Brazil and Russia.

In October, White House press secretary Katie Zacharia left the position just five days after her stint as Karoline Leavitt's replacement began.

The president told reporters he suggested Zacharia quit after her first and only press briefing, where, in her six minutes answering questions behind the lectern, she only took questions from three conservative outlets.

"I just spoke to her about it. I said, 'Katie, is this for you?'" Trump said outside the White House on Oct. 16.

The president's appeal to White follows Friday's announcement that Trump's deputy chief of staff, Stephen Miller, would be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump's stunning shake-up came just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate.

The president and White have a longstanding friendship. During Trump's first campaign in 2016, White praised the then-Republican nominee and called him a "fighter."

"Nobody took us seriously. Nobody. Except Donald Trump. Donald was the first guy to recognize the potential we saw in the UFC and encouraged us to build our business," White said.

And for his 80th birthday in June, President Trump hosted a UFC match — dubbed UFC Freedom 250 — on the White House's South Lawn.

The White House and the UFC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2676. Report: MRI shows Patrick Mahomes avoided serious injury

Chiefs quarterback Patrick Mahomes underwent an MRI on his right elbow following Sunday's 35-12 win over the Jets. The test did not reveal a major injury, Ian Rapoport of NFL Media reports.

The team will monitor Mahomes' strained elbow over the next few days for pain and swelling.

"If I'm able to play, I'm going to play," Mahomes said, via Nate Taylor of ESPN. "That's part of the game. It scares you in moments, when you get some pains in certain areas of your body. Obviously, it didn't feel great, but I was able to kind of get it worked on a little bit, especially at halftime."

With 1:33 left in the second quarter, Jets edge rusher David Bailey hit Mahomes' right arm on a strip-sack. Will McDonald IV and Darrell Jackson Jr. also hit Mahomes on the play.

Mahomes did not miss any snaps despite the injury.

"Even my knee last year, after we were doing the initial test, I thought, because I could walk off the field, I was going to be good. Then, all of a sudden, you do one test, and you're just out [for the rest of the season]. That stuff scares you. Once I could throw the ball, I knew it wasn't a ligament, and it was more muscles."

#2675. Amazon's Astro home robot has fans but looks destined for the 'trash heap.' Why it's trying again

Jeff Bezos made a bold bet in 2017. A sci-fi and robotics enthusiast, Bezos was convinced that consumers would have a robot in their home in the not-too-distant future.

The Amazon founder and then-CEO told a group of the company's top device executives to get to work on building one, and within a few years, the team came up with the $1,450 Astro.

The public got its first look at Astro, essentially an Alexa-equipped tablet on wheels that can autonomously navigate a home, recognize faces and act as a roving security monitor, when it rolled on stage at Amazon's fall 2021 event.

But it never evolved beyond an invite-only product. Astro shipped to just a few thousand customers, some of whom now believe the robot's days are numbered. The company also released an Astro for businesses in 2023, before sunsetting it less than a year later.

Amazon isn't giving up on Bezos' dream of a household robot just yet, despite its stumbles with Astro.

Over the past several months, the company has assembled a new team that's working on a "new-to-world robot product," according to a CNBC analysis of job posts.

"Robots are about to step out of the movies and into our homes," a recent job listing for an engineer, which has since been removed, stated. The post details a product capable of navigating "dynamic home environments" and "autonomous operation alongside family members."

Amazon is known for making long-term bets at the risk of "being misunderstood" in the short term if it gives the company an early advantage in new markets, like it did with the Alexa voice assistant or Kindle e-reader.

CEO Andy Jassy reminded investors of this approach in his latest annual letter, where he said artificial intelligence and robotics were among the "biggest inflections of our lifetime."

"We try to anticipate what will make customers' lives easier and better every day, and invent the next inflection," Jassy wrote.

Analysts also expect the humanoid market to surge in the coming years. Barclays forecast in a January report that the global market, which it currently values at $2 billion to $3 billion, could reach roughly $40 billion by 2035, or as high as $200 billion "under the most optimistic scenarios."

Earlier this year, Amazon redeployed much of the team behind Astro and brought in an MIT roboticist to work on the home robot project, LinkedIn profiles show.

It also made a splashy acquisition to supercharge the effort.

In March, Amazon scooped up New York-based Fauna Robotics, which has built a 3.5-foot-tall humanoid called Sprout. At the time, the company said Fauna would become part of a new division dubbed the Personal Robotics Group.

Some Personal Robotics Group staffers previously worked in Amazon's Lab126 R&D unit, which has been behind many of Amazon's most successful consumer hardware products, like the Kindle e-reader, Echo smart speakers, Fire TV and Fire Tablet. Lab126 was also behind one of Amazon's biggest flops, the Fire Phone.

It's unclear what kind of home robot the group is developing, or the types of tasks it could handle, but a June job posting hinted at an effort to "create robots that are not just functional, but delightful."

Amazon could be looking to adapt Fauna's robots for homes or businesses. The Sprout humanoid, which was designed to be "friendly and capable" with expressive yellow eyebrows, has primarily been used by developers, as well as commercial and academic researchers.

An Amazon job posting published online in October describes a "Field Support Engineer" who can assist with customer support and on-site repair, while another listing seeks a user experience designer to "define what it feels like to live with a Fauna robot."

"Our goal is simple: make robots people actually want to live and interact with in everyday human spaces," the listing states.

Amazon spokesperson Brad Glasser told CNBC in a statement that the company acquired Fauna because it's "committed to tackling the challenges that can unlock the personal robotics category."

"We're optimistic about a future where personal robots are helping people in meaningful ways in everyday life, and we intend to play an important role in making that future a reality," Glasser said.

Glasser noted that there remains a substantial gap between what's possible in a controlled lab and what's possible in settings like the living room or a lobby. Amazon is working with the robotics community to solve these challenges, he said.

Emily Lugo Velez has been living with the Astro in her North Carolina home since 2023. Lugo Velez, who worked in IT and now runs an AI advisory business, said she and her husband weren't fazed by the robot's $1,000-plus price tag and quickly joined the waitlist.

"We spent so much money on this thing," Lugo Velez, 52, said in an interview. "I feel like we're just stuck with it, and we'll let it run around the house until it eventually dies, then send it to the trash heap."

She said she still uses the device to check on her pets and appliances when she's out of the house. But beyond that, Astro has primarily become a party trick and a "trip hazard."

Most days, she tells Astro to go back to its charging dock.

Executives who worked on Astro had discussed making it more capable by attaching an arm to pick up and inspect items without breaking them, said Ken Washington, who served as Amazon's VP of consumer robotics until 2023.

He told CNBC that he pushed for a bipedal robot, but leadership disagreed.

"We had Jeff [Bezos] in for show and tells and reviews, and I watched his body language pretty closely," Washington said. "I think he was disappointed with the lack of functionality, but I think he was impressed with its ability to map and navigate an undefined space under all lighting conditions."

For all the struggles with Astro, Amazon has a rich history in robotics.

The company acquired Kiva Systems for $775 million in 2012, which introduced mobile robots to its vast fulfillment network. Since then, Amazon's warehouse automation efforts have ballooned to over one million robots across its facilities that can pick, sort and ferry items to workers.

But homes offer unique obstacles for robots to navigate.

"The home is a completely different space," said Jeff Burnstein, president of the trade group Association for Advancing Automation, who has been in the robotics industry for 45 years. "Children, pets, grandparents, stairways. Nobody's home is set up exactly the same, so it's a much more challenging environment for a robot, certainly a robot that's walking."

Amazon may have more luck its second time around because the tools for building robots have advanced by "leaps and bounds" over the past few years, said Colin Angle, a co-founder and former CEO of Roomba maker iRobot.

Amazon attempted to acquire iRobot in 2022 before walking away from the deal two years later amid growing regulatory pushback.

"The toolkit that the Astro team had when they did it was clubs with nails in them, just incredibly primitive," Angle said. "It's difficult to understate just how much the robot industry has changed in the last two years."

Advances in AI models, sensors, battery systems and key mechanical components like actuators have spurred a new wave of development around robots for industrial and consumer use.

Elon Musk's Tesla has been developing its bipedal Optimus robots since 2021. Startups including Apptronik, Boston Dynamics, China's Unitree, Figure and 1X are also building humanoids. Agility Robotics, the company behind the bipedal Digit robot that counts Amazon as one of its backers, went public via a SPAC on Thursday.

Venture capital firms poured $33.4 billion into robotics and physical AI companies in the first half of the year, compared with $28.7 billion for all of 2025, according to PitchBook data.

Most humanoids are being developed with the manufacturing, logistics and healthcare industries in mind, but a growing group of companies are attempting to build robots for the home.

Apple is reportedly working on several home robot projects, including a tabletop device that resembles the Pixar lamp. Hugging Face, which Nvidia agreed to acquire for almost $13 billion in September, released the $399 Microduck in August. Angle, in May, launched a startup building fuzzy companion robots called Familiar Machines & Magic. Companies including Sunday, Tau and Nori claim their robots can help with household chores like folding laundry and clearing the table, but they remain mostly prototype or niche devices.

"[Astro] was so ahead of its time," Washington said. "It was such a big, massive leap that I felt like we had the right to own the home."

The robot's limited functionality and its $1,000-plus price tag likely made it a tough sell for many consumers, said a former Amazon employee who worked on Astro, who asked not to be named in order to speak frankly about the project.

"Our poor little Astro was around $2,000 when it came out, and that's a lot," the ex-employee said. "That's a lot for a consumer, and they want a robot that does a lot."

Amazon takes a long term view on new markets like personal robotics, and values what can be gained by being an early entrant, Glasser said.

Astro was designed to help Amazon build competencies and focus its ambition, he added. The company has applied many of the learnings, talent and experience from Astro into its other robotics efforts, Glasser said.

In Facebook groups for Astro owners, users have complained of diminished customer service and griped that Amazon's AIexa+ AI assistant isn't supported on Astro.

Some users have interpreted this to mean Astro could be discontinued. Others have tried to dump their units at a discount on eBay and Facebook Marketplace.

Glasser said Amazon continues to support Astro customers.

Joe Martin, an Astro owner who lives near Tucson, Arizona, said he feels he's gotten his money's worth out of the device over the past four years. He'd welcome another robot into his home if it could do more, like helping out around the house.

"Astro was $1,000 and it was pretty limited, but if it could bring a drink to me or dust furniture, I'd be intrigued by that," Martin, 65, said. "I think that's right around the corner."

#2671. World’s No. 1 restaurant crowned as contest comes under increasing scrutiny

Asador Etxebarri in Atxondo, Spain, is the world's top restaurant.

That's according to The World's 50 Best Restaurants, which announced results on Wednesday evening in Lima, Peru. Asador Etxebarri, helmed by chef Bittor Arguinzoniz, sits in Basque Country and features grilled items like a charred beef chop, fresh mozzarella from the chef's buffalo, or house-made chorizo.

Chef Gaggan Anand's Gaggan in Bangkok, which combines progressive Indian cuisine with French, Thai and Japanese influences, was No. 2, and Alchemist in Copenhagen, from head chef Rasmus Munk, took the third spot.

India returned to the top 50 for the first time since 2004, with Masque at No. 26 and NAAR at No. 49. Mainland China made the list for the first time since 2021, with Meet the Bund in Shanghai at No. 21 and Ru Yuan in Hangzhou at No. 36.

Chef-restaurateur Ferran Adrià, who achieved worldwide renown with his avant-garde techniques at his now-closed restaurant El Bulli, received the SevenRooms Icon Award. Adria continues to support culinary research and education through the nonprofit El Bulli Foundation.

The 50, as the umbrella organization is called, creates lists of restaurants, bars, hotels and vineyards that it ranks as top in the world or region. It draws on industry networks to help rank establishments.

For years, The 50 has been seen as a kingmaker and an opportunity for restaurants and bars worldwide. Being "on the list," many chefs and other restaurant-industry insiders have said, makes a huge difference for reservations and revenues.

But there's been increased scrutiny of the contest as well. Chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, which was ranked 60th this year, posted on Instagram that she wouldn't be attending the Lima ceremony, saying she would rather invest resources "in our guests, our team, our producers and the daily work of making the restaurant better."

While stressing that she had enjoyed a "vibrant community" at the 50 Best and made many friends from it, she said "over time, however, success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system itself," and that promotion of one's restaurant risked becoming as important as the work happening inside it.

She appears not to have been alone. Many of the world's top chefs consider awards and lists to be a tricky balance with their work at their establishment.

"Guides and lists now shape the industry they claim to assess," Ivan Brehm, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Nouri in Singapore, told CNBC. "Their judgments influence where diners spend money, where investment flows, which chefs get attention and what younger cooks learn to aspire to."

He added of the 50 that "none of this denies the brilliance of the restaurants on the list or the opportunities it has created for all of us. The question is what it sidelines and what it incentivizes."

Roš' Instagram post had more than 10,400 likes as of Friday, including from top chefs worldwide.

"The reaction and the reach were unexpected" from the post, Roš told CNBC in an email. "I did not write a post to provoke, but more as an eye-opener. And apparently a lot of people have the same perception as I do."

The 50 defended its list and role within the industry.

"The 50 does not claim to have the capabilities to police establishments on their budgets or surrounding activities," a spokesperson told CNBC. "The 50's role is to provide a snapshot of expert opinions on the best hospitality experiences in a given region or category. It does not act as a regulatory body and it applies the same voting rules and principles consistently across all countries and regions."

The time and effort put into the contest can be a dilemma, too.

"We didn't go to Lima this year. It's a huge amount of time in the air for a very short amount of time on the ground and, like Ana, I felt my time was better spent being present in our restaurant with our team and our guests," said Dave Pynt, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Burnt Ends in Singapore, who won the 50 Best Chef's Choice Award in 2017.

However, he added that "when the awards are closer and easier to get to, I love going. They're fun, you learn a lot, and you get to catch up with mates and people in the industry you don't get to see enough."

#2667. 31-year-old dumpster diver brought in $140,000 in 12 months: I 'did much more than I thought was possible'

When Carter Stevens tells people he makes six figures a year digging through dumpsters, they often assume he's "blowing smoke," he says.

Not everyone sees dollar signs in a brimming dumpster. But Stevens earned roughly $140,000 in total sales between September 2025 and September 2026 by salvaging trashed items, cleaning them up and selling them on platforms like eBay and Facebook Marketplace, according to documents reviewed by CNBC Make It.

Stevens primarily operates near office parks and luxury condominiums around Nashville, Tennessee. Since March 2024, when he started dumpster diving, he's found and sold hand-crafted Persian rugs, high-end office chairs and even samurai swords, he says. Trash-picking helps him keep his shipping costs low, he adds: Some of his supplies, like cardboard boxes and packing tape, also come from dumpsters, enough that he doesn't have to pay for them approximately "90% of the time."

"The amount of waste in this country that you'll find is unbelievable. We throw so much stuff out," says Stevens, 31.

Sometimes, Stevens asks a building for permission to go through its trash, marketing himself as a junk removal service. In that way, his job is similar — though not identical, because he generally only takes what he can sell, he says — to other entrepreneurs across the U.S. who follow advice in Reddit threads or YouTube tutorials to launch junk removal side hustles or full-time businesses. There are around 10,000 junk removal businesses across the U.S., according to the Better Business Bureau. They range in scale from independent side hustles to national companies like 1-800-GOT-JUNK, which touts more than 150 franchise locations.

Other times, Stevens finds himself neck deep in rubbish, ducking concerned security guards, peeling away actual trash and rotting food in the hope of uncovering discarded treasure, he says. He tries to avoid sneaking around and keeps an eye out for "no trespassing" signs. He'd rather leave items behind if a property owner or security guard tells him to vacate the premises, he says.

Even Stevens expresses surprise at how much money he's made, saying he doesn't necessarily think his annual income is sustainable. When he "stumbled into" dumpster diving, he was approximately $12,000 in debt after years of poor spending habits fueled partially by a drug addiction, a stark change from his affluent childhood, he says.

"I dug myself out of a hole. I built up my savings. I really did much more than I thought was possible," says Stevens.

Stevens, a Nashville native, is the son of a five-time Grammy Award-winning producer of country and gospel music who has worked with artists like Blake Shelton and Carrie Underwood. His life took a turn in college when he developed an addiction to prescription Adderall, leading him to drop out partway through his senior year in 2018, he says.

His lack of a college degree made landing full-time work a challenge. He says his parents can afford to support him financially, and they've done so in specific emergency occasions. But mostly, his father wants his kids to learn to fend for themselves, he says: "He will let me sit on the coals for the sake of learning how to swim. That's taught me a lot [and] given me a lot of valuable skills and a tough mentality."

Unemployed, Stevens started combing through thrift shops and Goodwill clothing racks for vintage apparel to sell on eBay. He was inspired by YouTube influencers like Rick Senko, he says, whose Fort Lauderdale, Florida-based clothing resale business Technsports brought in $6.5 million in annual revenue as recently as 2024.

But Stevens' returns were inconsistent, and he often spent his profits on drugs or luxury shopping sprees, he says. After his parents funded a rehab stint in 2021 — helping him "quit doing the hard drugs," he says — he spent his money chasing other dopamine hits. Those indulgences included a $1,200 pair of Christian Dior Birkenstock sandals, an 85-inch flat-screen television and a $30,000 down payment on a Mercedes-AMG sedan that he later lost because he couldn't afford the total cost, he says.

By June 2023, he could no longer afford to pay rent, so he lived in a rented storage unit until the business's security found and evicted him, and then stayed in his Nissan Sentra for three weeks, he says. Homelessness shocked him into learning to manage his "reckless" spending habits, he says: "Being punished by your own spending addiction was a wringer that I promised myself I can't be put through again."

In October 2023, a cousin lent him money to start renting an apartment, and Stevens worked to stick to a sensible enough budget for his thrifting income to cover future rent and basic expenses, he says. Five months later, he found a discarded pair of black Nike Air Force Ones in good condition next to a gas station pump. He took them home, sanitized them and sold them online for $35, he says.

"That was the first time I had ever found something that was trash and sold it and made a little bit of money," says Stevens.

Once he started dumpster diving, Stevens was hooked "because I never stopped finding good stuff in these dumpsters," he says.

His biggest score to date: dozens of discarded Steelcase office chairs, worth $1,000-plus each when new, outside an overflowing office park dumpster, he says. He got his car — by this point, a used pickup truck and a self-built trailer — and hauled away 190 chairs to list online at hundreds of dollars apiece. He sold the majority of them in bulk transactions on Facebook Marketplace for $17,000, and brought in an additional $10,600 selling the remaining chairs individually, documents show.

If Stevens manages to find roughly one valuable haul per month, he can fill his $400-a-month storage unit and start listing the items. For the most part, he scopes out corporate environments, preferring "dry dumpsters" over ones laden with food waste, he says. Office trash is particularly profitable when companies downsize, says Stevens: Once, he found 120 standing desks discarded outside a building.

Near high-end apartment buildings, Stevens has found furniture from brands like West Elm, iPads, electronics and power tools in working condition, he says. "Almost every piece of furniture in my apartment came from a dumpster," says Stevens.

Stevens works alone and minimizes gas costs by sticking to locations within a few miles from his apartment. The majority of his income has come from big hauls across his five highest-earning months, reflecting the venture's hit-or-miss nature. "I got really lucky earlier this year and I made a ton of money. I'm not acting like this is something I could do every year," he says.

So he saves as much of his earnings as possible, "constantly" talking himself out of unnecessary purchases, he says. And he's studying the business of real estate wholesaling — which involves securing contracts for distressed properties and flipping them to investors for a fee — as a potentially more secure way to earn a living, he adds.

Still, he acknowledges the allure of knowing his next big payday could always be one dumpster away.

"I don't see myself ever turning down a good looking dumpster," says Stevens. "I have found so much cool stuff that I feel like I'll always be curious about what's in that dumpster."

#2665. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2664. Steve Sarkisian lauds the undeniable toughness of his Texas team

Casting the entire football world as doubting the ability of the No. 7 Texas Longhorns to go into Columbia and beat the No. 18 Missouri Tigers is a real stretch, but it was much more believable when head coach Steve Sarkisian lauded the undeniable toughness of his football team after securing a fourth ranked win this season as the Longhorns navigate a nine-game SEC schedule for the first time.

"The DNA of this organization is mental and physical toughness and I think that poured out of our pores today in this ball game," Sarkisian said.

Now through the fifth game in the eight-game stretch without a bye week to end the regular season, Texas is banged up, playing without three key starters and receiving just 18 snaps from star edge Colin Simmons in his return from a high ankle sprain.

"If you'd have told me we walk in here without Trevor Goosby, without Cam Coleman, and a very limited Colin Simmons, with some other banged up receivers — by the way, those other three are probably going to go in the top 15 picks of the draft — I don't know how many other teams could do that," Sarkisian said.

Texas did it largely by relying on the depth accumulated through years of highly-ranked recruiting classes, as well as key transfer portal additions stepping forward, like running back Raleek Brown, who caught the go-ahead touchdown pass on a wheel route and put the game away with his eight-yard rushing touchdown with 1:25 remaining.

Of the 101 rushing yards gained by the three Texas running backs who played, Brown notched 86 yards on 17 carries. And while he didn't break any runs longer than 13 yards, that was a credit to some solid situational tackling by Missouri more than the regular busts in execution and assignment from Kyle Flood's offensive line, which somewhat steadied itself on Saturday.

"One thing about Raleek, he might not be the biggest guy in stature, but he's got a huge heart," Sarkisian said. "This guy's a very prideful player. He plays hard. He provides a ton of energy, not just to the offense, but the entire team."

At wide receiver, Emmett Mosley V led the Longhorns with 85 receiving yards on five catches. Missouri played more zone defense than expected, and Mosley kept the Texas offense moving by regularly finding holes in it, affording quarterback Arch Manning the type of intermediate throws that tend not to suffer from his inconsistencies.

Next to Mosley, who played every snap, St. Louis product Ryan Wingo was a warrior next to him for 71 all plays even though he practiced minimally on Thursday before participating in the Friday walkthrough. Wingo notched six catches for 52 yards and one carry for six yards.

"I'm really happy for Ryan — I know Ryan wanted this game and he wanted to play in the game. Like I said, he was not 100 percent, but he made some really critical plays for us today," Sarkisian said.

"We've got to get him healthier again for next week. So happy for him, happy for his family. I know this game meant a lot to him," Sarkisian said.

Defensively, more players had to emerge with the prevalence of injuries.

Because Simmons was limited, other edge players had to step up, including senior Colton Vasek, who battled shoulder injuries throughout his career. Over the last three games, Vasek has played half of his season total of snaps, recording four tackles (two solo) and coming up with a big sack in his 18 snaps against the Tigers.

While nickel back Graceson Littleton was able to battle through 51 snaps after he was listed as questionable on the early injury reports, cornerback Kade Phillips played 33 snaps, showing some toughness of his own — Phillips was targeted three times in coverage, but didn't give up a reception.

Defensive coordinator Will Muschamp leaned on cornerback Warren Roberson to handle the snaps that Littleton and Phillips missed. As Roberson received his first career start, Missouri predictably targeted the inconsistent senior regularly, but beyond a pass-interference penalty on the first drive, Roberson mostly held up well.

Tigers quarterback Austin Simmons targeted Roberson on 21 percent of his pass attempts, including nearly a quarter of the throws that Pro Football Focus tracked as targeting a specific player in coverage. Normally an efficient passing offense, Missouri completed three passes for 35 yards on the nine throws with Roberson in coverage, 3.9 yards per attempt.

By itself, Roberson's coverage success made it his best performance at Texas, ultimately magnified by his crunching hit on Cayden Lee that caused the fumble returned by Littleton to the goal line that set up Brown's game-sealing touchdown run.

"Guys have to step up — that's the nature of the beast, but like I told the team last night, we don't recruit really well around here for fun. It's not for Signing Day. It's for moments like this that we can have quality people that we trust that can go in and play," Sarkisian said.

The Texas head coach made changes to his culture-building approach this offseason, starting Culture Wednesdays during the spring instead of the summer to reflect the reality that the vast majority of incoming players now arrive in January.

Now that's paying off, in Sarkisian's estimation.

"This team gets tighter — the more adversity we get, the tighter we get. I think it's a cultural thing, I think it's a toughness thing, and I think it's a leadership thing. I'm proud of our coaches. I'm proud of the the leaders on this team to galvanize everybody three weeks ago or so to continue to fight back," Sarkisian said.

After the home loss to Florida marked back-to-back defeats against unranked opponents, the Longhorns were reeling, with questions about Sarkisian's role as the offensive play caller, the role of Jeff Banks as the special teams coordinator, and the role of starting quarterback Arch Manning, embattled after game-changing turnovers.

Manning in particular has raised his level of play, going two games without throwing an interception and operating without the hesitancy that characterized his indecisive performance against Florida.

And Sarkisian has made some adjustments to the offense by relying less on shot plays, going back to the running back screen game, and generally calling more quick passes to get the ball out of Manning's hand before the protection breaks down.

Right now, with one of the best resumes in the country, the Longhorns are survivors. Challenges remain, starting with next week's trip to Death Valley to face the Tigers, but over the last three games, Texas has found ways to win again.

"We knew it was going to be a long season. The season wasn't over. It was going to be a long season, and it's not over today," Sarkisian said. "We've got more games to go play. We've got another tough one next week, but we're going to surely enjoy this one for the for for next eight 16 or so hours."

#2663. Mark Cuban says he doesn’t ‘give a f---' about growing his net worth—a healthy mindset for non-billionaires too, a financial therapist says

Mark Cuban has zero F's to give — at least, when it comes to his net worth. Financial therapists say that's a healthy mindset to have, even if you're not a multibillionaire like Cuban.

"I don't give a f--- about my net worth. I have enough. My family has enough," Cuban posted Monday on social media platform X, responding to another user's post about the growth of the serial entrepreneur's wealth going back to 1990.

"I don't care about moving up on the Forbes 400," Cuban added.

Cuban currently has an estimated net worth of $6.8 billion, according to Forbes, which ranks the former star of ABC's "Shark Tank" as the 597th wealthiest person in the world. Cuban sold his first business, a software company called MicroSolutions, for $6 million in 1990. He became a billionaire when he and his co-founders sold Broadcast.com to Yahoo for $5.7 billion in 1999.

He developed his attitude after buying a majority stake in the NBA's Dallas Mavericks in January 2000, he tells CNBC Make It: "I was more interested in winning than making money." The mindset might seem obvious to some: After all, why would a billionaire have any stress about his bank account? But even people with lots of money experience financial stress and anxiety, studies have shown.

What seems to set Cuban apart from some other wealthy people is the confidence he exhibits in how he puts his wealth to use, says Christine Hargrove, an Athens, Georgia-based financial therapist. In his post, Cuban wrote that he doesn't obsess over growing his personal net worth because, in part, he'd rather see the impact his money can have on the world.

"My next dollar can impact other people's lives a lot more than mine," wrote Cuban, citing his decision to launch the cost-cutting pharmacy startup Cost Plus Drugs and his many small-business investments across 15 seasons on "Shark Tank" as an investor judge. "That is what drives my investments. I look for ways to have an impact."

Confidence in how you save or spend your money is often a signal of a healthy financial mindset, notes Hargrove, who is president-elect of the Financial Therapy Association and Assistant Director of the Love and Money Center at the University of Georgia.

"You actually have to be the person to decide what is enough for you," she says, adding: "Having a sense of purpose with your money and with your life can be an incredible source of protection against chronic financial anxiety."

Many Americans, especially as costs of living remain high nationwide, have no choice but to worry about their finances.

When those with plenty of money in their bank accounts or stock portfolios obsess over their level of wealth, they're also probably not doing so by choice, notes Hargrove. Some people's financial insecurities come from growing up in low-income households. Others may think of themselves as relatively struggling because they earn less than the other wealthy people around them.

"Having a healthy relationship with money isn't just about how much you have. It's about understanding what money means to you and what you want it to do for you," says Cara Macksoud, an Orlando-based accredited financial counselor and financial behavior specialist.

No matter what your financial situation is, anxiety about money can actually hurt your finances in addition to the toll it takes on your mental health. Financial stress and related mental health challenges can hurt workplace performance through distraction and absenteeism, according to a 2024 report by the TIAA Institute, the research arm of retirement fund manager TIAA. Poor mental health can also impair cognition, making it more difficult to make sound money decisions, the report suggested.

Obsessing over every dollar can also make it feel impossible to part with money for causes that would otherwise align with your personal values, such as charitable giving or support for loved ones, Hargrove says. "And then our money actually comes between us and who we want to be," she adds.

Financial anxiety often results in repetitive behaviors, such as obsessively checking your bank account or stock portfolio, notes Hargrove. For those not in immediate financial peril, Hargrove suggests reminding yourself "enough is enough" and working to replace those behaviors with actions that foster gratitude for the cherished aspects of your life that are not related to your finances.

If you constantly check banking apps on your phone, you might change your wallpaper to a photo of your family or friend group to remind you of the safety of your family and social networks, for instance. It's a simple trick, but it can soon become part of a pattern of "focusing on thoughts of gratitude" that research has shown can lower levels of the stress hormone cortisol in your body, Hargrove says.

And if you can feel like you're using your money to make a positive difference, whether for yourself or others — as Cuban suggested in his post that he does — you probably have a healthier mindset around money than a lot of other Americans, notes Hargrove.

"Living with gratitude and feeling like you make a difference, either for yourself or for other people, [there's] not much better than that in life, right?" Hargrove says.
