#2661. Cathie Wood 'always feels better when the market is worried' — here's why

Ark Invest CEO Cathie Wood is shrugging off this week's market jitters, telling CNBC on Friday that concerns among investors actually reinforce her optimism that further gains lie ahead.

U.S. equities sold off sharply on Thursday, reversing the initial rally seen in the wake of the midterm elections. AI-related stocks led the rout, sending the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite into correction territory. On Friday, tech stocks staged a tentative recovery.

Much of the sell-off on Thursday was driven by concerns that a Democrat-led Congress would lead to a slowdown in data center construction. But concerns about artificial intelligence – particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing – have sparked multiple bouts of volatility over the course of this year.

Wood, whose firm manages various funds focused on tech and innovation, told CNBC there was a "wall of worry" pervading financial markets right now. But her decades of experience as an investor give her a somewhat unconventional view of the state of play.

"I experienced the tech and telecom bubble, and you know there really wasn't much worry about it," she said. "It was the 80s and the 90s, and it was a bull market, and people were just assuming this would go to the moon, that is not how this [the AI trade] has evolved."

She explained that "from the beginning," many investors have been concerned about how much firms are spending on AI development, how rapidly the spending is taking place, and how the hyperscalers have moved from extreme levels of positive free cash flow to negative free cash flow.

"So it's been unsettling the markets for quite some time," she said. "I always feel better when the market is worried, because that tends to suggest a more durable bull market, and I don't think we've derailed this bull market."

In 2025, her flagship Ark Innovation ETF gained more than 35%, far outperforming the S&P 500's 16.4% rise and the Nasdaq Composite's 20.5% rally.

Asked on Friday whether markets were anywhere near the end of the current bull run, Wood replied: "I don't think so."

"I think we're in the middle of a technology revolution," she said, pointing to the development of technologies like robotaxis and AI agents.

One thing that did risk becoming a "showstopper," Wood noted, was the rising cost of capital.

Interest rates and bond yields have risen sharply this year as the U.S.-Iran war pushes inflation higher across the globe. Yields on corporate bonds have been rising too, with creditors demanding greater compensation for lending to companies.

"This correction is partly caused by what's going on in the bond markets," Wood said on Friday.

"I had been saying for quite some time that the wall of worry has been built by senior leaders in the financial world who were young during the bubble," she said.

"It's the elder statesmen and women of the industry who are keeping this wall of worry in place, and that's not bad. Now you can add the bond investor to that. Bond investors in the old days weren't necessary for technology. It was software. It was low fixed cost. It was viral apps, very high margins. [AI] is high fixed cost, so bond investors are experiencing or taking a journey into the technology world, really that they haven't very much before."

But she noted that AI hyperscalers were somewhat insulated by opportunities for growth.

"If a company thinks that taking on debt is going to accelerate its momentum, meaning its growth rate, it hardly matters what happens to interest rates for those companies," Wood explained.

Wood said her advice to investors in the current volatile environment was to identify their high-conviction positions and double down on them as the market shifts.

"When you see a rally in a volatile environment, take some profits. When you see a sell-off, you'll have the psychological wherewithal if you have taken profits to move back in," she said.

"That's what we do. So what we do during periods like this is concentrate towards our highest conviction names. So that's what we've been doing in the last week or so. Our SpaceX position has gone up. If anything, the controversy around data centers on Earth is giving data centers in space a bigger opportunity."

In 2027, Wood said she expects the equity rally to continue.

"This is two steps forward, one step back," she said of the post-election turbulence. "The revolution will become clearer and clearer next year, and I think the market will do well."

#2658. How Democrats will try to end Trump's tariffs

The battle for Congress is over. The war over President Donald Trump's tariffs is about to heat up.

The knotted web of tariffs that Trump spent the last two years weaving could soon face its biggest threat, as Democrats prepare to unload their new legislative firepower against the Republican president's signature policy.

Democrats have made no secret of their plans to bombard Trump and his associates with investigations as soon as the next Congress is sworn in. They are also preparing a first-100-hours legislative blitz, putting forward a series of bills that would unwind much of Trump's agenda.

Both prongs of attack will take aim at Trump's unprecedented use of tariffs, which Democrats have quickly identified as a main target following their landslide victory in Tuesday's midterm elections.

Ending Trump's tariff regime, and especially his trade war against Canada, is something Democrats "absolutely have to do immediately," Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed, D-Mich., told MS NOW's "Morning Joe" Thursday.

Rep. Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the Democratic minority leader who could be elected House speaker in the next Congress, has made clear that ending Trump's tariffs is a top priority.

"We should restore congressional authority as it relates to tariffs, because the Trump tariffs are making life more expensive for the American people," he said at a press conference in late August.

If picked as speaker, Jeffries would speed legislation to the House floor that reasserts congressional authority over trade.

Democrats are also laying the groundwork for investigations into Trump's tariffs, which the party folded into its winning campaign message linking high costs with alleged Trumpworld corruption.

Rep. Jamie Raskin of Maryland, the top Democrat on the House Judiciary Committee, on Oct. 28 asked Trump to hand over records related to tariff exemptions. Trump, Raskin alleges, has wielded the exemption power to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

The plans are likely to ratchet up the pressure, both legally and politically, that Trump's tariffs were already facing.

The administration is already embroiled in multiple lawsuits challenging different parts of the government's tariff policy.

The most recent suit, filed Oct. 20, targets Trump's use of a Great Depression-era law to impose 50% tariffs on a range of Canadian goods.

Another court case accuses Trump of trying to recreate the near-global "liberation day" tariffs that the Supreme Court struck down as illegal in late February. The administration has since been ordered to refund $166 billion in tariff revenue it had collected under the scrapped policy.

Congress, not the president, holds the power to levy taxes under the U.S. Constitution. Over decades, the legislative branch has ceded some of those powers to the executive. But Trump has asserted virtually unchecked tariff powers – which Democrats say they are intent on clawing back.

"Every day Trump insists on his indiscriminate taxes on so many of the products Americans need, he digs a deeper hole for Republicans enabling his tariff spree," said Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, who is poised to lead the Senate Finance Committee when Democrats take it over.

"If Democrats somehow had a veto-proof majority, or if voters shame Republicans into doing the right thing, there would be no shortage of trade policies to address," Wyden said in a statement to CNBC before Election Day.

"One of the most obvious priorities would be repealing Trump's tariffs on Canada, which are raising prices and harming communities on both sides of the border," he said. "I'll also continue to work to ensure Congress has the final say on tariffs, so future presidents can't reenact Trump's economically disastrous trade taxes."

The Wall Street Journal, citing Wyden, reported Thursday that Democrats are eyeing the Canada trade war as the "first target" in their efforts against his tariff agenda.

As Wyden's statement suggested, the Senate's rules will hamper Democrats' anti-tariff efforts.

Despite their blowout victory Tuesday night, the party will still lack a 60-vote filibuster-proof majority in the Senate, giving Republicans a crucial tool to block legislation even in their diminished state.

Trump is also poised to lean heavily on his veto pen in the next Congress, presenting a major obstacle for Democrats' legislative priorities.

Overriding a presidential veto requires two-thirds majorities in both the House and Senate, meaning that even with their historic electoral gains, Democrats would need significant Republican help to force a bill into law over Trump's objection.

Democrats may consider it worthwhile to pass legislation even if Trump vetoes it, as doing so could help them build political narratives in the 2028 election cycle. But they may have other options available.

"Absent the brute force of a veto-proof coalition, Congress might consider other political solutions, such as adding riders on 'must-pass' bills or negotiation with the administration," Kit Johnson, a senior tax manager for BDO's customs and international trade group, told CNBC in an email.

Trump has said he plans to use the veto power to force Democrats to the negotiating table. But when it comes to tariffs, it's hard to imagine what deal Trump might accept.

Trump has tried to reshape U.S. trade, foreign and economic policy around his asserted power to slap steep duties on imported goods. He describes tariffs as a win-win, praising them as both a powerful negotiating tool and a major revenue generator.

After Tuesday's election, Trump seemed to suggest tariffs were non-negotiable.

"If I took tariffs off — we have 3,000 plants under construction right now. Every single plant would stop building," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a phone call Wednesday.

Since taking office in January 2025, Trump has hiked tariffs on nearly every country on the planet, leading to multiple protracted trade wars, most notably against Canada and China. He insists Americans do not share the burden of the duties, even though tariffs by definition are taxes on foreign goods paid by those importing them. The nonprofit Tax Foundation estimates Trump's tariffs have raised the average U.S. household's tax burden by $820 in 2026.

Trump has also consistently overstated the amount of revenue the government has amassed through his tariffs. He claimed before Election Day that tariffs enabled him to offer every U.S. adult $5,000 "dividend" checks if Republicans were to win both chambers of Congress – a plan that would cost roughly $1.2 trillion. But the duties – which are collected from U.S. importers – have only brought in around $300 billion in total between January 2025 and July 2026, according to the Penn Wharton Budget Model.

What's more, about $138 billion of that total has been refunded since the Supreme Court ruling in February. Nearly $20 billion in tariff collections are still set to be refunded.

Most voters do not share Trump's rosy view of tariffs. Sixty-five percent of respondents to the CNBC Voter Poll on Nov. 3 said the duties have hurt the U.S. economy, while just 22% say they helped.

What remains to be seen is how hard Republicans in Congress will push back on Democrats' efforts.

A number of GOP lawmakers came out against the duties during the campaign, as their opponents gained ground blaming inflation on Trump's tariffs and the Iran war.

As they try to regroup and look forward to the 2028 election cycle, some Republicans – who largely supported free trade prior to Trump's political rise – may be less willing to continue aligning themselves with Trump's aggressive protectionism.

#2656. Cowboys' big stats don't equate to points in close Week 9 win vs Colts

A win is a win in the NFL, and Sunday's victory in Indianapolis keeps the Cowboys in contention for the NFC East, but it's difficult to feel very good about the 27-23 close call.

Once again, Dallas let a weak team hang around, and this time it nearly cost them a second straight gut-punch loss. Despite outgaining the Colts 508-271 and allowing them to convert less than a quarter of their third-downs on the day, the Cowboys watched a double-digit lead evaporate in the final 17 minutes of play and had to engineer a come-from-behind two-minute drive in order to secure the win against a 2-6 Indianapolis team who's currently in the middle of a clearance sale on star players before the trade deadline of what appears to be an already-conceded 2026 season.

The Cowboys offense should have been able to post far more than 27 points. Dak Prescott was productive, going 21-of-34 passing for 335 yards (tying his season high) and a pair of touchdowns, even though he didn't have George Pickens all day and lost Jake Ferguson for a chunk of time.

CeeDee Lamb continued his mind-boggling season. The targets leader through eight weeks got 16 more on Sunday, catching 10 of them for 195 yards (his second-best day as a pro) and a touchdown. According to PFF, it was Lamb's second game of 2026 with 10 receptions/180 yards/1 TD, while the rest of the league combined has just one.

But despite racking up their most yards in three calendar years and averaging 7.2 yards per play, the offense did as much to stop themselves throughout the afternoon as the Colts defense.

Lamb's costly fumble in the fourth quarter wasted a 48-yard pickup and turned the ball over at the Colts' 9-yard line. Though Indy couldn't translate that takeaway to points, the miscue thwarted a probable touchdown drive by the Cowboys and kept the Colts close enough to tie the score a few minutes later. Earlier, Lamb failed to get both feet down in the end zone on a would-be touchdown catch. So his monster stats don't tell the full story.

Prescott also coughed up the ball. Instead of driving toward an early double-score lead, that turnover gave the Colts possession on a short field and gave them a head start on their first go-ahead touchdown, setting the tone for a unexpectedly closely-fought game.

And of the season-high 14 penalties walked off against the Cowboys, nine of those calls went against the offense. Three of them were for offensive pass interference, negating another 56 yards worth of catches. The Cowboys continue to show themselves to be a thoroughly undisciplined bunch, and a higher-quality opponent would have made them pay dearly.

Brian Schottenheimer's play calling will undoubtedly continue to draw criticism. His decision to dial up a jet sweep/push pass to Lamb on a 3rd-and-1 (that failed) early in the second quarter definitely fell into the "too cute" category and drew immediate ire from fans on social media.

While the win is welcome, make no mistake: the Cowboys offense is not playing like an elite unit of late.

Christian Parker's beleaguered unit, however, did some good things Sunday, including ultimately bailing out the offense and icing the win. They held the Colts to 105 total rushing yards and limited Jonathan Taylor to just 3.5 yards per carry. They allowed just three third-down conversions on 13 attempts, a 23.1% clip. They forced five three-and-outs and permitted only one Indianapolis drive to go for more than eight plays or longer than 2:40. All are improvements over what the defense has shown for most of the season.

The defense did give up a pair of explosive plays, the 55-year touchdown run-and-catch by Ashton Dulin and Seth McGowan's 36-yard breakaway run. That's an area that's been particularly troublesome this year, though just two explosives out of 59 plays is a frequency the Cowboys should be able to live with.

But for the first time this season, the Dallas defense held its opponent to under 300 total yards. Were there mistakes? Of course. DeMarvion Overshown getting flagged for an unsportsmanlike conduct penalty while in street clothes and not even on the active gameday roster is simply inexcusable and cannot happen, especially from a four-year veteran. And DaRon Bland got burned badly again, hit with an awful pass interference call that moved the ball 40 yards.

Individually, Quinnen Williams had a big game, leading the defense with seven tackles- three of them for a loss- and forcing a fumble. Rashan Gary had a big load: six total tackles, a sack, and a pair of TFLs. And rookie Caleb Downs snagged the key interception with under a minute to play that ended the Colts' last chance at a rally. He also added a huge sack in the second quarter that got the defense off the field.

Whispers about the Cowboys making a trade for edge rusher help could get louder now, after Donovan Ezeiruaku and Dee Winters were both dinged up during the game. Safety P.J. Locke also sustained a scary-looking injury, even after switching to a Guardian Cap midgame. Overshown and cornerback Joey Porter Jr. were inactive, leaving the defense well below full strength and creating question marks for a massive challenge next week versus the 49ers.

It's time to talk about Brandon Aubrey. The Cowboys kicker was flagged yet again for a kickoff that fell short of the landing zone; his third penalty for that infraction leads the entire league in 2026. And he missed yet another field goal, this one from 50 yards away with under four minutes to go. While replays showed that long snapper Trent Sieg didn't deliver his best effort- skipping the ball on its way to Bryan Anger's hold- Aubrey has now missed 22% of his field goal tries on the season. (And he's only 14-of-25 on attempts from 50 or beyond over the last two seasons.) Sunday's wide-left shot gave Indy the ball on the 40-yard line; they needed just 28 yards to set up a field goal of their own to give them a late lead that the Cowboys were lucky to overcome.

Aubrey may have once been considered automatic from anywhere near the logo. That is no longer the case.

Top Democrats are ramping up oversight of the Trump administration and businesses it has partnered with, while Attorney General Todd Blanche warned he could counterinvestigate lawmakers conducting probes.

Democrats romped to majorities in both the House and the Senate during Tuesday's midterm elections, putting them in position to seize control of committee gavels and subpoena power in January. The electoral rout leaves Democrats with at least 54 Senate seats and 254 seats in the House of Representatives as votes continue to be counted in several states.

Democrats vowed to investigate both President Donald Trump's White House and businesses that have worked with the administration. CNBC reported on Thursday that Democrats are expected to take an aggressive approach to probing businesses, especially those companies with ties to the Trump family. The technology, cryptocurrency and prediction market industries, among others, could immediately be in the crosshairs.

Elizabeth Warren, the incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee, on CBS' "Face the Nation" on Sunday. "The American people are just damn sick of this."

Blanche was referring to comments made by Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., who said during a University of Chicago event that she wants "young people coming out of school to say, 'Should I go work for the Trump administration?'...I want them to be like, 'Lord, that might be buying myself a ton of problems that I don't want. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Trump has ordered the Justice Department to investigate Democrats over the comments.

Blanche also took aim at Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the likely incoming chair of the House Oversight Committee — appearing to caution lawmakers who sit on the panel. Garcia warned the administration and business leaders to "lawyer up" after the election.

#2654. China wholesale inflation hits highest in more than four years as consumer measure slows

Producer prices in China grew at the fastest rate since June 2022, accelerating for the third month in a row.

The PPI for October came in at 5.7%, compared to the 5.3% expected by economists polled by Reuters. The increase was driven by a sharp rise in prices in the mining and raw materials industries.

The increase was driven by very strong external demand, Zhang Zhiwei, president and chief economist at Pinpoint Asset Management, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Consumer inflation in China slowed to 0.7% in October, its first deceleration after two straight months of increases.

The figure missed expectations of a 0.8% rise from economists polled by Reuters, and was lower than the 0.9% seen in September.

Core inflation, which strips out food and energy prices, came in at 0.8%, down from the 1% seen in the month before.

The rise in consumer prices were still held up by prices of gasoline, which was the largest contributor to the increase in prices, but this was offset by a sharper than expected fall in food prices, according to Dong Lijuan, chief statistician of the urban division at China's National Bureau of Statistics.

The Chinese economy has shown some signs of expansion recently. It grew faster than expected in the third quarter, and September saw a sharp beat in retail sales.

The mainland Chinese CSI 300 index gained 0.56% after the data release.

Pinpoint's Zhang said that domestic demand has been rather weak due to the property market, and this is a key reason why inflation is not picking up in China"There's no clear sign of stabilization [in that sector] yet," he said, adding this was weighing on the household consumer confidence and local government revenue.

"So the the overall economic momentum has been weak and hasn't really seen [a] clear sign of recovery," Zhang explained.

#2653. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2652. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2650. Trump says renewed Iran strikes ‘highly unlikely’ if talks progress but warns he could still strike

President Donald Trump said he is "highly unlikely" to restart open war with Iran, but warned he could still strike the country as Washington tries to reach a deal with Tehran to end the conflict.

The now more than eight-month-old war, which stoked inflation and sent oil prices surging, has seen repeated bursts of fighting separated by ceasefires and peace talks for months. It was widely speculated that Trump might restart open conflict after the midterm elections, where his Republican Party lost badly to Democrats.

Trump's comments came during an interview with CNN, where he said restarting the war is unlikely as long as talks to constrain Iran's nuclear program and reopen the Strait of Hormuz make progress.

"It depends on whether or not they sign a very strong agreement," Trump said. "The answer is highly unlikely it's going to be necessary. They're a failing nation."

Trump, however, left the door open for additional strikes if he deems it necessary. The president reiterated his threats to strike bridges and power plants in Iran if the war restarts.

"I've got three aircraft carriers here. The other one just arrived. We have three. They're fully locked and loaded," the president said. "We have 38 bridges in our site that we can knock down in literally one hour."

An end to the war and reopening of the Strait of Hormuz would likely ease oil prices, though finding a truce has proven difficult. All previous attempts to end the war and reopen the strait have been met with disappointment.

Trump is likely to come under increased pressure to end the conflict, however, now that Democrats have won control of both the House and the Senate. Some Republicans are also likely to be eager for an end to the war, given the role high gas prices played in their drubbing at the ballot box.

The president has demanded Iran fully open the Strait of Hormuz and agree to never develop a nuclear weapon as part of any deal.

Later in the day on Truth Social, Trump posted that "Task Force Iron Gate is in full force and effect in the Islamic Republic of Iran."

"Coupled with history's greatest Naval Blockade, very little gets into, or out of, Iran!" he said.

Central Command announced Task Force Iron Gate on Saturday, describing it as an initiative aimed at "preventing the movement of advanced weapons systems, dual-use technologies, sanctioned materials, and illegal revenue streams across Iran's land borders and the Caspian Sea."

The U.S. has maintained a naval blockade of Iranian ports for months.

#2648. From supermarket fridges to passenger flights, a wave of threats is testing Europe's defenses

Cyberattacks, bomb threats against passenger flights, civil unrest and suspected acts of sabotage across Europe have raised questions about the continent's resilience — and how prepared it is to defend itself against modern security threats.

Hackers remotely manipulated refrigeration systems at supermarkets across Europe last week, forcing retailers to discard spoiled food and exposing vulnerabilities in everyday infrastructure.

French blood-bank refrigeration systems were subsequently targeted in what appeared to be a similar incident, while a wave of suspected hoax bomb threats disrupted dozens of flights across the continent, forcing diversions and, in some cases, fighter jet escorts.

The week culminated with bomb threats targeting European news outlets, including the U.K.'s Sky News and Irish public broadcaster RTÉ.

The incidents come against a backdrop of heightened geopolitical tensions and domestic unrest across parts of Europe, with civilian infrastructure potentially becoming an increasingly attractive target for hostile actors.

While authorities have not established who was behind the attacks or whether they were connected, defense and security experts warn that disruption to everyday services can have consequences far beyond the immediate economic damage.

If Russia were responsible, such attacks could be intended to demonstrate the costs of Europe's continued support for Ukraine, said Sidharth Kaushal, a research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute, or RUSI.

"I think it's more about cost imposition," Kaushal told CNBC. "Convincing the European political leadership that the costs of a war can come home to them."

Russia didn't immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

On Monday, supermarkets in Belgium, Luxembourg, Sweden and the Netherlands found that refrigeration systems had been set to as much as 50 degrees Celsius, or 122 degrees Fahrenheit, affecting retailers including Ahold Delhaize, IKEA, Colruyt Group and Carrefour.

It followed an October cyberattack that targeted hundreds of French supermarkets, causing fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil. The incidents highlighted vulnerabilities in food-supply infrastructure.

They also highlight the role of private companies in national resilience, as internet-connected equipment can be exploited to cause physical disruption.

Fredrik Blix, cybersecurity expert at Visente and associate professor at Stockholm University, told CNBC that the supermarket attacks required very little technical sophistication since vulnerable refrigeration control systems were connected directly to the public internet, allowing potential attackers to find and exploit them.

"These attacks, they don't really require any specific advanced knowledge to carry out," he said, adding that some systems were protected only by default passwords or easily guessed credentials.

"This is why this is so cheap to do," Blix said, warning that the publicity surrounding the incidents could encourage copycat attacks. "Anyone can do it, and it does not require any resources from a country or anything like that."

Cyberattacks have become more common over the past decade as societies have become more digital and vulnerable systems have become easier to access, Blix said. However, he noted that mounting geopolitical tensions have also made European countries more alert to potential hybrid warfare threats, leading to scrutiny by both governments and media.

While Blix acknowledged the possibility of a state or state-backed actor being behind the attacks, he said the more likely scenario is someone testing the boundaries and "having some fun."

Kaushal said the incidents should be viewed within the larger context of suspected sabotage and hybrid threats across Europe, including damage to undersea cables in the Baltic Sea.

"If this was an event in isolation, one might say 'okay, we should view it on its own merits,' but when you view it in the backdrop of all of that, it becomes less clear," he said, also citing death threats against European defense executives and a bomb placed on a DHL aircraft in 2024.

European governments are contending with greater uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees after decades of underinvestment in defense, while facing a rise in unconventional but potentially devastating threats.

Hybrid warfare combines tactics such as cyberattacks, sabotage and disinformation to destabilize adversaries without triggering open military conflict.

Countries' abilities to withstand hybrid threats vary substantially across the continent, according to Kaushal. While Finland and Sweden have well-developed civilian defense programs reflecting their Cold War experience, much of Europe's infrastructure was built around economic efficiency rather than defense needs.

"As one goes further west in Europe, the level of preparation, both against hybrid attacks, but also just simple missile attacks of the kind that we've seen in Ukraine, is far less great," he said.

"Every attack generates a bit of resilience," he said, citing NATO's increased focus on threats to underwater cables and gas pipelines after the 2022 Nord Stream sabotage.

Oana Popescu-Zamfir, a senior research fellow specializing in European security at RUSI, said the recent incidents showed Europe had lagged in "preparing for something that we have known would be coming."

"We've been slow in adjusting to a new reality," she said. "We still tend to conceptually divide between peace and wartime, and that is no longer the case because we're not at war, but we're also not in peace time."

She said Europe was likely to face sustained hybrid attacks designed to achieve military objectives without conventional military force.

Popescu-Zamfir said Russia could draw on networks of proxies in Europe, some directly controlled and others more loosely coordinated, making responsibility difficult to trace directly to the Kremlin.

She told CNBC that "the damage that can be done is huge" in targeted attacks on internet-connected systems, citing the potential disruption of blood reserves.

The attacks are not a "piece of cake for Russia to carry out, but they're also not rocket science," she said, adding that they "can do tremendous damage in concrete terms and in terms of the psychological impact on our societies and the feeling that we are unprotected."

Blix said companies can take relatively straightforward steps to reduce their exposure.

"These are basic cyber hygiene things... You should not be allowed to connect industrial control systems and this kind of operational technology directly online without any proper security around it," he said.

Responsibility is split among product manufacturers, maintenance providers and grocery chains, making accountability unclear as each points to the others.

Blix said maintenance companies sometimes prioritize convenience by enabling remote access despite manufacturers' security guidance, while retailers may fail to enforce adequate cybersecurity standards among suppliers.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," Blix said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

Europe has also seen widespread unrest in recent months, adding political pressure as households grapple with high living costs.

Domestic political and economic tensions can also give foreign adversaries opportunities to deepen divisions, said Alexander Anderson, president and director of policy at the Centre for International Security and Economic Strategy.

He told CNBC that Russian and potentially Chinese influence operations may work to amplify those divisions.

"But we should be careful not to dismiss legitimate domestic grievances as the product of foreign interference," he said. "Doing so risks misunderstanding both the causes of political instability and the methods through which hostile actors seek to exploit it."

Weeks of nationwide protests, riots and strikes have increased pressure on the French government, complicating efforts to rein in public finances and deepening existing political divisions.

In Spain, demonstrations and a parliamentary impasse over the housing crisis led Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez to call a snap election. The vote is scheduled for Nov. 29. The far-right Vox party is expected to make gains, putting Sanchez's left-leaning government at risk of losing power.

Germany, Italy, Portugal and Albania have also seen unrest over rising living costs in recent weeks.

On Friday, protesters in Amsterdam tried to enter a Microsoft data center, Reuters reported.

Meanwhile, elevated energy prices, persistent inflation, sluggish growth and large budget deficits are pushing up borrowing costs and leaving governments with less room to support struggling households.

#2647. Smashed Bottle and Fireworks Scare: Lando Norris' Podium Mishap Goes Viral After Sao Paulo Win

Lando Norris has gone viral all over social media after a Brazilian GP podium that went terribly wrong. The British driver looked in control of everything that Interlagos had to throw at him, but only until he got to the podium. Opting for his signature party trick, the British driver went to smash the champagne bottle's bottom on the floor, but the outcome wasn't what he expected.

Hoping to see the contents of the bottle fly out, Norris and the fans of his party trick were in for disappointment when the bottle gave out. As he looked at the neck of the bottle that was still in his hands, the McLaren driver did not realize he was backing in too close to the podium fireworks.

The 2025 F1 champion then got hit in the face with the pyro, as he quickly backed away from it. Ultimately, Norris did not seem to be hurt by the incident as he laughed it off alongside fellow podium finishers, Max Verstappen and Isack Hadjar.

The series of unfortunate events for the three-time Grand Prix winner in 2026 quickly went viral on social media, with fans taking to the comments section to detail their feelings about it all.

Norris' signature celebration is something that he has been doing since his junior racing days, as he once explained how the process works. Instead of shaking the bottle, you smash it against the ground to make the contents fly out. A thick-bottomed bottle works best for this, with one having to remember not to put enough force to smash the bottle itself.

Having won his third race of 2026, the outgoing champion seemed to forget his own advice and smashed the bottle to bits before walking face-first into the fireworks.

Lando Norris forgot to pay heed to his own advice. (Source: MEGA)

"OK, the trophy is made of titanium and I can't break it. What can I break today?" wrote one fan, recalling the 2023 Hungary GP incident when Norris ended up breaking Max Verstappen's porcelain trophy.

Meanwhile, another fan referenced the same incident as they wrote, "smashed a bottle and the race. Didn't smash a trophy this time."

For some fans, Norris' aura loss was a major concern as one wrote, "so much aura was lost during this," while another commented, "aura -1000000000."

Lando Norris indicated that he was okay after the podium incident. (Source: MEGA)

The post-celebration moments saw Norris still holding on to the neck of the bottle and posing with it in one hand while holding the race winner's trophy in the other. For one fan, this was a particularly enjoyable moment as they wrote, "love he's holding on to the remaining of the champagne bottle."

Having bungled the celebrations on the podium, Norris would earn a shot at redemption soon after. As is tradition, the trophy winners have a team photo after all the proceedings have wrapped up, and the spraying of the champagne is as much a tradition outside the garage as it is on the podium.

Taking one bottle for himself to spray during the team celebrations, Norris made sure that he wasn't going to go two-for-two in terms of smashing the bottle to bits in Brazil. In fact, the Briton got so cautious that he ended up shaking the bottle to have it spray, instead of opting for his party trick.

The Briton was seen constantly shaking the bottle to have it spray as he poured its contents onto his teammates outside the McLaren garage. He would later also reveal that his hand was fine from the podium incident, while the champagne bottle was 'not fine.' And while his ear looked visibly red from the fireworks incident, he didn't disclose much about it.

#2646. Nvidia investigates CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with alleged Chinese spy

Nvidia has opened an investigation into co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy.

U.S. intelligence agencies have expressed concerns that the woman, Alice Huang, who is affiliated with a cloud company and an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed, has been passing information to Beijing, according to reports in The New York Times and the Financial Times.

Huang told Nvidia staff in an email on Sunday that he had an affair with the woman and that Nvidia's board of directors had opened an independent investigation into the spying allegations.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," Huang wrote in the message. "I am deeply sorry."

An Nvidia spokesperson said that Huang was committed to continuing as CEO of Nvidia without interruption.

In a statement, the Nvidia board said it would investigate the allegations that Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

"While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," Nvidia's board said in a statement.

Read Huang's email to Nvidia employees and the company board of directors' statement here.

The allegations come as Nvidia has become the center of a trade dispute between China and the U.S. as AI is seen as increasingly critical to national security.

The U.S. restricts exports of Nvidia's most advanced AI chips to China, though it has approved exports of certain older, slower models.

Megaspeed, which Ms. Huang is affiliated with, has been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia AI chips. Nvidia representatives previously said that Megaspeed had already been investigated for evading export controls.

Nvidia's Huang has pushed for broader AI chip exports to China, saying it is better for the U.S. if the world uses and builds on American technology. He has also aligned with President Donald Trump on American artificial intelligence policy and its infrastructure buildout.

In his note to staff, Huang said he was "proud and grateful" to be an American.

Three House Republican committee chairs sent a letter to Huang on Wednesday asking him to sit for a closed-door briefing with lawmakers on Nov. 18.

Nvidia reports third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17 and will hold a major conference in Washington, D.C., at the end of the month.

#2645. 49ers rookie defensive tackle Gracen Halton suffers season-ending broken ankle in win over Raiders

The San Francisco 49ers maintained their place atop the NFL standings with Sunday's 38-17 win over the Las Vegas Raiders, improving their record to 7-1.

However, a team that's already been hit hard by injuries lost one of its best young defensive players in the Week 9 win. Rookie defensive tackle Gracen Halton broke his left ankle and will miss the remainder of the season, head coach Kyle Shanahan announced.

Halton, 22, suffered the injury during the fourth quarter when fellow defensive lineman Keion White was blocked by Raiders tackle DJ Glaze and fell onto his lower leg. Medical personnel applied an air cast to Helton's left leg and he had to be carted off the field.

In eight games (six starts), Halton led the 49ers with five tackles for loss and was second on the team with two sacks. He recorded 15 total tackles with a forced fumble.

"He's played at a very high level as a rookie," Shanahan said after the game. "He's had an awesome seven or eight games here. We're gonna miss him a lot."

Halton was San Francisco's fourth-round pick in the 2026 NFL Draft (No. 107 overall) out of Oklahoma.

"I'm sick for him," linebacker Fred Warner told NBC Sports Bay Area's Bonta Hill. "I know he was emotional getting on that cart, but I let him know, God, He does everything for a reason. So just keep his head up and he'll be all right."

The 49ers acquired defensive tackle DeForest Buckner from the Indianapolis Colts last week, which bumped Halton from the starting lineup. But the depth on the interior defensive line and ability to rotate players in and out is now obviously affected by Halton's season-ending injury.

Cornerbacks Deommodore Lenoir (foot) and Renardo Green (back) also left Sunday's game with injuries and did not return. Shanahan said both players would be re-evaluated on Monday and haven't been ruled out for Week 11's matchup with the Dallas Cowboys.

#2644. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2643. Kings send Nique Clifford to Thunder for draft assets: Trade grades

The Sacramento Kings have agreed to trade guard Nique Clifford to the Oklahoma City Thunder in exchange for an unprotected 2028 second-round draft pick via Utah and an unprotected 2030 second-round draft pick via Minnesota, the team announced Sunday, Nov. 8.

Clifford, 24, is a 6-foot-5, 210-pound guard out of Colorado State that was originally selected by Oklahoma City with the 24th overall pick in the first round of the 2025 NBA Draft. However, Clifford never logged a minute in a Thunder uniform following a trade to the Kings that sent Oklahoma City a protected 2027 first-round pick.

He appeared in 75 games for Sacramento during the 2025-26 season and started 28. Clifford averaged 8.6 points, 3.8 rebounds and 2.4 assists in about 25.1 minutes during his rookie campaign with the Kings, while shooting 41.8% from the field and 33% on 3-pointers.

Clifford was being molded into a perennial "three-and-D" type of player for the Kings. He saw a lot of playing time in his rookie campaign, in part due to the numerous injuries Sacramento sustained to its roster that year.

He scored a career-high 30 points in a 132-126 loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers on Feb. 8. Throughout the 2025-26 season, Clifford also spent some time down in the G-League, playing for the Stockton Kings, the Kings affiliate.

In his collegiate years, Clifford averaged 10.1 points and just under a steal. He shot 46.9% from the field and 36.4% on 3-pointers.

Clifford signed a four-year rookie contract with Kings worth up to $15.7 million with $6.3 million guaranteed, according to Spotrac. Clifford's average annual salary is about $3.9 million.

He'll earn another $3.2 million in base salary.

Clifford appeared to be a contributing piece to the Kings' rebuild. The consummate professional, Clifford came into work, did what he was asked and pushed himself to be the best version of himself.

There was an expectation that Clifford would take a leap into his second-year but instead it came with setbacks. Clifford saw his minutes diminish early in the season. In his last game against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday, Nov. 6, Clifford only logged 4 minutes, 30 seconds and scored five points on 2-of-2 from the field.

He only played more than 10 minutes once this season – against the Memphis Grizzlies on Oct. 31. Clifford appeared in five of their nine games, earning four DNPs by coach's decision.

It appeared that Clifford was on the opposite end of head coach Doug Christie's rotations and a change was warranted. The Kings get to open a roster spot while picking up assets.

The Thunder have had misfortune early in the season, facing season-ending injuries to key rotation players, and additional injuries that will keep guys out long-term. Jalen Williams suffered a fractured left fibula and a dislocated ankle that required surgery. He's out for the year.

Cason Wallace has a torn labrum in his left shoulder and he is also out indefinitely. Chet Holmgren was ruled out 8 to 10 weeks after a biological treatment for a small patellar tendon tear in his right knee.

Oklahoma City needs bodies. Clifford was being buried on the bench early this season, the Thunder believe there's a spark that he can bring to to the team.

The organization has built a reputation of maximizing reserves and role players getting the best out of them, the latest project will be Clifford.

#2642. Devils Won't Start Connor Hellebuyck In His First Game Back In Winnipeg On Nov. 10

The New Jersey Devils announced on Sunday night that Connor Hellebuyck won't start the Nov. 10 game against the Winnipeg Jets. The news comes shortly after a 4-0 Devils victory over the Toronto Maple Leafs, a game Hellebuyck started and earned a 23-save shutout, his first with the team.

Hellebuyck, who won three Vezina Trophies and the 2025 Hart Trophy with the Jets, wanted to start the game and make his much-anticipated return to Winnipeg. "I'm disappointed, but I understand," Hellebuyck stated after the game against the Maple Leafs. "Selfishly, I want to play, but when you look at what's best for the team, I understand where they are coming from," he added.

The decision to start Jake Allen, instead of Hellebuyck, came from the front office and general manager Sunny Mehta, along with the coaching staff. "From our end of it, he's playing his third game in six nights; he's been at this for less than two weeks, and then after we finish this road game against Winnipeg, we go home," head coach Sheldon Keefe added, referencing the divisional matchups on Thursday, Nov. 12, and Saturday, Nov. 14, against the Washington Capitals.

The statement and the decision are questionable. Mehta cited the expected win probability in his statement as the reason for starting Allen against the Jets. The Devils are 5-0 in games Hellebuyck has started while going 6-3 in Allen's starts. Hellebuyck started five of the previous seven games and the Devils were willing to lean on him as their primary starter, especially once he began practicing with the team. His first practice was on Oct. 26 and his first start was Oct. 27 against the Dallas Stars.

The surprise is that the Devils had confidence in Hellebuyck from the moment they acquired him and leaned on him during the hot streak. Yet, for his much-anticipated return to Winnipeg, they prefer Allen, suggesting starting the backup gives them a higher win probability.

The other question is what happens next, not just for the Devils, who have won five games in a row and nine of their last 10, but for the Jets as well. The team was preparing for his return, and while the fans will likely express their frustration with their goaltender demanding a trade, the team was preparing to welcome him back in some capacity. Presumably, the Jets have a video tribute showcasing his highlights and great play on the ice, but more importantly, his impact off the ice and everything he's done for the Winnipeg community over the past decade.

Will the Jets play the video if Hellebuyck isn't on the ice or behind the bench? According to Jets media members, Hellebuyck doesn't sit on the bench when he's backing up. Instead, he stays in the room with the goaltending coach.

In the Devils' statement, Hellebuyck requested to meet with the media before the Tuesday night game. He will meet after the Devils' morning skate at the Canada Life Centre.

#2638. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

Wales wing Louis Rees-Zammit says he has rediscovered his swagger as he aims to fight fire with fire against the All Blacks.

Rees-Zammit will be to the fore when Steve Tandy's side attempt to upset the odds against New Zealand in the Nations Championship on Saturday (14:10 GMT).

Wales go into the game after labouring to a 28-21 bonus-point victory against Japan at Principality Stadium.

It was an unconvincing performance, but Rees-Zammit stood out, scoring a try, making 12 carries for 121 metres and beating 13 defenders to be named man of the match.

He made more line breaks against Japan than he did in the entire Six Nations, when he started every game at full-back.

"I felt really confident out there and that's from the work in the week and at Bristol," said Rees-Zammit.

"I have found some confidence in my game and playing back on the wing helps. I feel I can create chances, help the team go forward and finish tries off."

Wales let themselves down despite win - Lake

He said: "I am just playing with a smile on my face, playing with confidence and always thinking that I can beat defenders, and in defence I feel really good as well."

Louis Rees-Zammit has scored one try in two games against New Zealand [Huw Evans Picture Agency]

"Last year we were in it until 60-odd minutes. It's about hard work and putting your body on the line against those boys," said the wing, who scored an acrobatic try against New Zealand last year.

"Next week will be brave and go for it. They are obviously a great team, but the second that you shy away from it, you miss and they take advantage."

After left wing Tom Rogers became the first Welsh player to score a hat-trick against New Zealand last year, Rees-Zammit will hope for plenty of chances to show his finishing prowess on the right flank next weekend.

#2633. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."
