#2632. In Broncos-Panthers, replay was used to determine whether replay should be used

In Sunday's Broncos-Panthers game, Denver coach Sean Payton activated the replay process at a key moment, turning an incompletion into an interception.

As it turns out, the NFL also used replay to determine whether replay should be used to give the Broncos possession after Panthers tight end poppe the ball into the air in the end zone and Broncos safety Devon Key scooped it up before it touched the ground..

In a pool report, NFL V.P. of instant replay Mark Butterworth explained that replays were consulted to determine whether Payton had thrown the red challenge flag before the next snap.

"Replay can get involved to make sure that the head coach throws his challenge flag prior to the next snap," Butterworth said. "We were actually able to use angles to show that the flag was out prior to the ball being snapped, and so we permitted the challenge."

Although the rulebook doesn't expressly state that replay may be used to determine whether replay can be used, the NFL relies on the first sentence of Rule 15, Section 9: "The Replay Official or designated members of the Officiating department may provide information regarding the correct application of the playing rules."

And while Section 9 lists several specific examples as to when such consultation is permitted, the list is preceded by the phrase "including, but not limited to." Which means that there may be other situations in which the Replay Official and/or the league office may assist the on-field officials.

One of those situations is whether a coach properly activated the replay process. Which allows replay to be used to determine whether replay should be used.

#2631. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2630. Hardik Pandya-Arshdeep Singh trade: What do MI and PBKS stand to gain from the deal

Hardik Pandya-Arshdeep Singh trade: What do MI and PBKS stand to gain from the deal originally appeared on Cricket News. Add Cricket News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Hardik Pandya is set to join Punjab Kings in exchange for Arshdeep Singh, with the trade reportedly completed.

Mumbai Indians, who finished ninth in IPL 2026 with four wins from 14 matches, gain a left-arm new-ball and death-overs specialist to pair with Jasprit Bumrah.

Punjab Kings gain a fast-bowling all-rounder under Shreyas Iyer but take on Hardik's injury risk and lose their all-time leading wicket-taker.

After months of trade rumours and uncertainties, Hardik Pandya's home ahead of the 2027 IPL might be decided in the most shocking way possible. According to multiple reports, the Mumbai Indians and the Punjab Kings have agreed on one of the biggest trades in IPL history, with Hardik Pandya moving to Punjab and Arshdeep Singh going the other way ahead of IPL 2027.

An IPL source has confirmed that the trade has been completed and that Hardik is pleased to play under Shreyas Iyer, who continues as Punjab's captain. Hardik's three-year stint as Mumbai captain has been disappointing, with the franchise making the playoffs just once and losing 24 of their 39 matches.

In contrast, Arshdeep has been in excellent form in recent seasons, even helping Punjab reach the finals against RCB last year. His 111 wickets in 96 matches at an economy rate of 9.18 has also been a big part of his appeal.

Both teams have until the retention deadline on November 17, and the mini-auction takes place in Goa on December 16. Here is what each side stands to gain.

MORE: 'GG never accepted mediocrity' - Iyer reveals Gambhir's high standards with KKR

The clearest gain is bowling support for Bumrah. No Mumbai bowler took more than 15 wickets in IPL 2026, with Allah Ghazanfar leading the list with 15. Arshdeep took 14 wickets for Punjab in the same season and 21 in 2025, when he led their attack to a place in the final.

He also brings a skill set that complements Bumrah. In April, he took 3/22 against Mumbai by swinging the new ball, reversing the old one and bowling a quiet over in the middle. His record at the death is built on yorkers, and a Bumrah-Arshdeep pairing gives Mumbai two specialists for the last four overs.

The deal also resets the captaincy. Rohit Sharma is expected to regain the role, which ends a tenure that produced more defeats than wins.

MORE: Livingstone reacts after R Ashwin's co-host compares him to 'trash'

Punjab finished fifth in 2026 with seven wins from 14 matches and were among the last teams to be eliminated, so the gap to the top four was small. A fast-bowling all-rounder is the rarest profile in T20 cricket, and Hardik was the first player in that category to score 1,000 runs and take 100 wickets in T20Is.

The fit is also clean off the field. Hardik joins purely as a player, with Iyer staying in charge, and the decision to trade for him came from the captain and the coach.

The risks are real, however. Hardik has suffered multiple injury breakdowns and has not played since the last IPL, and Punjab are giving up the bowler who is the franchise's all-time leading wicket-taker.

MORE: Arshdeep Singh-Hardik Pandya swap: Fans react as R Ashwin drops major hint over MI-PBKS trade deal

On paper, Mumbai has addressed a specific issue by bringing in a proven specialist. They needed a second front-line pace option beside Bumrah, and Arshdeep is a better fit for that role than any bowler on their 2026 wicket list. A fresh captain and a fit new-ball partner give a ninth-place side a clear route back into the top four.

Punjab's logic is harder to read. Their second-half bowling was a major issue last season, and they have traded away their best bowler to fix it. That only works if Hardik stays fit and bowls meaningful overs, because an all-rounder who misses games cannot make up for the loss of a leading wicket-taker.

A side that reached the 2025 final deserves the benefit of the doubt, but the deal puts all of the risk on Hardik's body.

The trade will be judged on two numbers: how many league games Hardik plays for Punjab and how well Arshdeep and Bumrah share the death overs for Mumbai. Punjab's answer to replacing Arshdeep's overs will be revealed at the Goa auction on December 16.

The 32 things we learned from Week 9 of the 2026 NFL season:

0. Still, the number of wins by the Los Angeles Chargers, who lost Sunday to the Houston Texans and dropped to 0-8 for the first time since 2000 when – you might have read this before – Jim Harbaugh was one of their quarterbacks … though it was the Ryan Leaf era then. 3a. Also the number of sacks by New York Giants rookie LB Arvell Reese – something of a coming-out party given he had 1½ in his first seven games – in his team's 19-11 defeat of the Philadelphia Eagles.

5. More importantly, Detroit fulfilled its near-term mission of notching a W for injured LB Jack Campbell, who remains hospitalized after suffering a scary neck injury in Week 8. Campbell, who is reportedly determined to resume his career, remains at the forefront of his teammates' minds.

"A lot of what we said was win No. 6 for 46," Lions coach Dan Campbell told reporters on Sunday. "And we did that today. We got that done."

6. The number of quarterbacking changes, necessitated by various reasons, in Week 9. Sunday saw the Cleveland Browns' Shedeur Sanders, Philadelphia's Joe Milton and the Arizona Cardinals' Garnder Minshew II all make their first starts of the 2026 – none of them successful. Minshew's Cards' were swept by the Seattle Seahawks and their QB2, Drew Lock, who stood in for injured Sam Darnold and vanquished Arizona for the second time this season – though an oblique injury raises the possibility Lock could join Darnold on the sideline in Week 10. The Baltimore Ravens' Joe Fagnano lost his first career start Thursday night against the Jacksonville Jaguars.

7. The number of times New York Jets WR Adonai Mitchell was targeted in Sunday's 35-12 loss to the Kansas City Chiefs. He didn't catch a single pass.

8. The number of times Chargers QB Justin Herbert was sacked by the Texans on Sunday, the most abuse a Bolts passer has absorbed in a half-century.

9. To illustrate how much the QB carousel has spun in 2026, Jacoby Brissett, who just lost his starting job to Minshew in Arizona, is expected to replace Fagnano by becoming the Ravens' fourth different staring quarterback this season on "Monday Night Football" in Week 10. Brissett was officially obtained from the Cardinals on Friday and would face the winless Chargers if that is indeed his Baltimore debut.

10. Speaking of QB2s on Monday night, the Minnesota Vikings are turning to Carson Wentz this week against the Buffalo Bills as Kyler Murray rehabs a sore hamstring … though it's worth wondering if either he or Wentz will be the Vikes' starter on the other side of the trade deadline.

11.  New Orleans Saints WR Jordyn Tyson caught four passes for 30 yards – and also lost a fumble – in his NFL debut Sunday. A hamstring injury suffered in training camp delayed the start of his career.

12. With Tyson now in action, Los Angeles Rams QB Ty Simpson is the only first-rounder from the 2026 draft who has yet to play.

13. The number of penalties (for 119 yards) committed by the Carolina Panthers on Sunday. They lost to the Denver Broncos.

14. The number of penalties (for 153 yards) committed by the Dallas Cowboys on Sunday. They beat the Indianapolis Colts.

15. The play of Week 9? How about Broncos DB Devon Key's amazing, heads-up interception in Charlotte which completely changed the momentum of the game Denver eventually won.

16. The number of consecutive regular-season games decided by one score which the Broncos have now won – a new NFL record.

17. Sanders had perhaps the best game of his career – his 114.6 passer rating was a personal best for the Browns' second-year passer – but he came an overtime two-point conversion shy of upending the Saints in the Superdome. TBD what Sanders' 297-yard day (with two TD passes) means for incumbent QB1 Deshaun Watson, who sat out Sunday after clearing concussion protocol late in the week.

18. The number of touches (for 64 yards) Colts RB Jonathan Taylor had in Sunday's loss to Dallas, potentially his final game with Indianapolis pending what happens ahead of the trade deadline – Taylor perhaps the most prized player who remains potentially available. He had averaged nearly 23 touches over the first six games, but it's perhaps not surprising the Colts have reduced his workload lately.

19. How can a team as talented as the Cowboys nearly find a way to lose to a team as decimated as the Colts? Dallas outgained Indianapolis 508-271, averaged 2.6 yards more per play and dominated time of possession, holding the ball for nearly 11 more minutes of game time. But when you draw that many yellow flags, turn the ball over twice and even your normally elite kicker is missing field goals, you remind everyone why you might not be good enough to win even the downtrodden NFC East.

20. What a wild day for the Eagles' Braden Mann, half of his eight punts covering 60 yards – one at the end of the first half bombed 86 yards, the longest in the league in a quarter-century.

21. Yet Mann wound up with an unremarkable 48-yard gross average on the day given another punt traveled 17 yards, while his worst went for minus-2 yards. Woof.

22. Here's hoping the Chiefs don't have to dig down their QB depth chart after Patrick Mahomes experienced what he described as "scary" elbow injury in Sunday's shellacking of the Jets. Mahomes didn't miss a play but will have more testing done this week.

23. Justin Fields is Mahomes' backup nowadays … in case you were wondering.

24. In the meantime, Mahomes and Travis Kelce did break the record for most passing yards by a quarterback-tight end duo in NFL history, surpassing the mark of former AFC West rivals Philip Rivers and Antonio Gates.

25. Defensively, Chiefs DT Chris Jones had two sacks Sunday, the three-time All-Pro's first full sacks of the season. He entered Sunday with a half-sack through the season's first two months.

26. Who needs a running game when your top two receivers are doing things the storied Green Bay Packers have never accomplished in the Super Bowl era (since 1966)?

27. And while the Pack whacked the New England Patriots 45-27 in front of Pats legends Tom Brady and Rob Gronkowski, shoutout to Gronk on the weekend he entered the team's Hall of Fame.

28. Props to Las Vegas Raiders TE Brock Bowers, who now has 224 career catches – the most ever by a tight end in his first three NFL seasons. And Bowers obviously has a chance to now blow well past Trey McBride's previous mark (221).

29. Props to the Cincinnati Bengals, whose defeat of the Atlanta Falcons on Sunday in Madrid tied them with the idle Pittsburgh Steelers for the AFC North lead. More noteworthy, the Stripes' TD celebrations in front of Spanish fans in the iconic home (Bernabéu) of Real Madrid were most definitely on point.

30. Brother on brother crime overseas, Falcons DB Sydney Brown tackling his identical twin brother, Bengals RB Chase Brown, for the first time in the NFL.

31. Brother on brother crime in the heartland, Cowboys DB Caleb Downs trash talking older brother Josh Downs, a wideout for the Colts, when he dropped a pass … and nearly got literally lit up by the rookie. Adding insult to insult, Caleb Downs intercepted Indy's final pass of the day to put the four-point win on ice for Dallas.

32. Congratulations to the real vets, not the NFL variety, who will be celebrated Wednesday on Veterans Day. And a shoutout to the NFL, which has raised more than $80 million for its military nonprofit partners since 2011, when the league instituted its "Salute to Service" program, which will be in high gear from Weeks 9 through 11.

#2625. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2624. Jaylen Brown dismisses any discussions to be had between him and Jayson Tatum

Jaylen Brown dismisses any discussions to be had between him and Jayson Tatum originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Ever since Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum were split up due to a trade in the summer of 2026 that sent Brown to the Philadelphia 76ers, there has been some lingering animosity between the former running mates. Most of the talk has been coming from Brown, who has called out Tatum for a couple of supposed lies.

Due to their history on the Boston Celtics, it is a story that has left a lot of people about where they stand with each other today. It is definitely intriguing to see two players who were joint at the hip for almost a decade just outright ignore each other these days.

During their time together in Boston, Brown and Tatum were always at the top or near the top of discussions about best duos in the league. They lived up to that billing because they went to two NBA Finals during their time together, and they won one in 2024.

A lot of people spoke about how they were not compatible for a long time. However, they kept proving the doubters wrong with impressive performances as a partnership. Unfortunately, that has finally ended, and there seems to be a rift between the two since the trade that Brown seems adamant that Tatum was well aware of.

MORE: Jayson Tatum has broken his silence on Jaylen Brown and a potential feud

Brown and Tatum are scheduled to play against each other for the first time in their careers on Tuesday, November 10, 2026. It will certainly be an interesting topic to think about because Brown will be motivated to beat his former team, but it seems he has drawn a line in the sand when it comes to Tatum.

"It's nothing to talk about. He's on his side; I'm on my side. We're playing basketball, both of us are trying to win, so nothing to talk about, check the ball up," Brown said after the 76ers' impressive win against the Bulls.

It will be a fun story to follow because this could be the newest rivalry in the NBA. With their shared history, it makes a fun storyline to follow, and it will be intriguing if they do guard each other because that will show what happens when they played against each other in practice.

#2621. Winderman’s view: Heat’s Spoelstra sends wakeup call to Wiggins in win with rotation remix

Observations and other notes of interest from Sunday night's 114-108 victory over the Sacramento Kings:

– Whether taken as running low on patience or the panic of the 3-6 record carried into the night, Andrew Wiggins was shuffled out of the starting mix for the first time in his Heat career, in favor of Pelle Larsson.

– Erik Spoelstra in his pregame media session said it was go-time with his lineup and rotation amid the struggles of Wiggins and Tim Hardaway Jr.

– "Look, this is the balance: Those two are very important to our team. We want to pour life and confidence into them," the Heat coach said. "Often in a long season you have to deal with multiple realities. A lot of things can be true. They're not playing and contributing at the highest level right now. That's not always going to happen."

– Spoelstra added, "So sometimes that affects minutes. But that doesn't change how we view them and how much we want to pour into them and get them playing at a higher level. And we feel it will happen."

– So, no, not necessarily anything permanent.

– "Some things in this league can just change so fast," Spoelstra continued. "You can have a great night and then all of a sudden everything is in a flow for you."

– But, Spoelstra insisted, "The bottom line for our team right now, as a lot of guys are trying to figure it out individually, is collectively we just need a point to win. We have enough veteran players to win a basketball game."

– Also before the game, Spoelstra again pointed to Larsson as the type of connector that his team needs.

– "He just continues to progress at a rate that shouldn't be surprising for somebody with his competitiveness and his work ethic," Spoelstra said. "Right now he's leading us in terms of his ability to connect lineups. Whatever the unit he's in, he's a connector. And that's proving to be really important for us right now."

– Spoelstra added, "We need more connectors while we're gaining confidence in our game. And the other thing is he brings that competitive toughness, that spirit, on both ends of the court, that we want everybody to embody, as well."

– And, so, Wiggins was replaced in a starting lineup that otherwise for the 10th time in as many games featured Giannis Antetokounmpo, Klay Thompson, Bam Adebayo and Davion Mitchell.

–– Also, for the 10th time in as many games, the team's three two-way players (Tre Donaldson, Keshad Johnson and Vlad Goldin) again inactive.

– Donaldson and Goldin both were in action Sunday for the Heat's G League affiliate, the Sioux Falls Skyforce.

– Goldin closed the victory over the Grand Rapids Gold with 19 points, 12 rebounds, three assists and 3 blocks.

– Donaldson closed the victory with 29 points (5 of 8 on 3s), six assists and four steals

– With Domantas Sabonis sidelined by knee soreness, the Kings opened with a lineup of Darius Acuff Jr.., Zach LaVine, Dylan Cardwell, De'Andre Hunter and Precious Achiuwa

– As for the Heat rotation beyond the starters . . .

– With Larsson with two early fouls, Wiggins was first off the Heat bench.

– Followed together by Ryan Conwell and Bobby Portis together.

#2620. Sunday Night Football: Despite three Caleb Williams INTs, Bears lead 17-6 at halftime

Bears quarterback Caleb Williams started out hot on Sunday night, but three second-quarter interceptions have allowed the Buccaneers to stay in it. But Tampa Bay hasn't been able to do much with all the extra opportunities.

With a 14-3 lead in the second quarter, the Bears looked like they were about to go up 21-3, as they'd faced little resistance from Tampa Bay's defense.

But on second-and-21, Williams was looking for Rome Odunze in the end zone when Tykee Smith stepped in front of the pass to pick it off. The Bucs gave it right back when Baker Mayfield was stuffed on a QB sneak on fourth-and-1 from their own 35.

Williams gave it away again on his next pass, when he heaved a downfield throw while getting hit. That pass was picked off by Zyon McCollum.

But again, the Bucs got nothing out of it, as they had to punt.

Williams' third interception came on what looked like a throwaway attempt. But McCollum was able to pick off another with a dive, and the play was reversed upon review.

Chase McLaughlin hit a 21-yard field goal as time expired in the second half, as head coach Todd Bowles decided not to go for a touchdown with the clock stopped at :03.

That meant the Buccaneers managed just three points off of three second-quarter takeaways.

A week after not completing a single pass in a victory, Baker Mayfield is 11-of-19 passing for 139 yards with an interception. The Bucs are just 1-of-6 on third down.

Williams is 14-of-23 for 193 yards with two touchdowns and three picks. It's his first multi-turnover game since Week 3 of his rookie season in 2024. Williams had never thrown three picks as a pro before this game.

Luther Burden has three catches for 81 yards with a touchdown. Rome Odunze has four receptions for 60 yards with a TD.

Tampa Bay will receive the second-half kickoff with a chance to double up.

#2619. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2617. Drew Lock and the Seahawks earn an easy win over the Cardinals

Seahawks quarterback Drew Lock did today what he’s done three times this season: Played mistake-free football and led Seattle to a win.

Lock, playing once again for an injured Sam Darnold, was steady and efficient as the Seahawks controlled the game and beat the Cardinals 17-3.

Is Lock a better quarterback than Darnold? Probably not, but Lock might be better for what the Seahawks need right now, which is a quarterback who can play mistake-free football and let the defense win the game. Darnold has thrown nine interceptions this season, while Lock has thrown none.

Today Lock completed 19 of 28 passes for 175 yards and one touchdown. Not spectacular numbers, but the key numbers were zeroes: Zero interceptions, zero fumbles, zero sacks. Darnold has far more negative plays than Lock does this season, and that’s one of the reasons the Seahawks are 3-0 in the three games that have been primarily quarterbacked by Lock, and 2-4 in the six games that have primarily been quarterbacked by Darnold.

At 5-4, the Seahawks aren’t the team they hoped to be heading into this season, but they’re very much in contention. And they might just have a better chance of making the playoffs and getting an opportunity to defend their title if Lock keeps playing.

#2615. Heat change lineup, Giannis steps forward in 114-108 victory in Sacramento

This wasn't going to be a night for a statement victory. Of course, at this stage, the Miami Heat are in no position to be choosy.

So against an opponent that entered 1-8 and was lacking sidelined three-time All-Star and current NBA rebounding leader Domantas Sabonis, Erik Spoelstra's team simply took care of business Sunday night at Golden 1 Center.

While the 114-108 victory over the Sacramento Kings hardly was overwhelming or emphatic in light of the competition, the Heat remain very much in early-season survival mode at the moment, improving to 4-6.

And survived they did, behind 29 points and 14 rebounds from Giannis Antetokounmpo and 23 point from Klay Thompson, built off of 7-of-13 3-point accuracy.

The Heat also got 18 points and eight rebounds from Bam Adebayo and 17 points and 11 assists from Davion Mitchell, who also put together several solid late defensive sequences.

Those efforts proved enough to help offset 32 points from Sacramento rookie sensation Darius Acuff Jr., last June's No. 5 overall pick out of Arkansas.

From the start, when Spoelstra removed Andrew Wiggins from the starting lineup in place of Pelle Larsson, it was clear what mattered most was the season's fourth victory, even one that came with a 44-36 rebounding deficit and 8-of-18 shooting from the line, that element overcome with 18-of-43 3-point shooting.

Five Degrees of Heat from Sunday night's game:

1. Game flow: The Heat led 34-30 at the end of the opening period. The Heat's seven first-quarter 3-pointers tied their high for a period this season.

The Heat then moved to a 68-59 halftime lead, which matched their largest lead to that stage, aided by 12 Kings first-half turnovers.

A 7-0 run to open the second half then staked the Heat to a 75-59 lead, only to see the Kings counter with a 17-5 run, the third quarter ending with the Heat up 91-81.

From there, a 9-0 run to open the fourth quarter helped the Kings eventually tie it 95-95 with 6:12 to play.

That's when Larsson stepped up with a go-ahead 3-pointer and Michell followed with key plays on both ends to help put it away.

3. Giannis' night: Forced to take his offense more often to the perimeter. Antetokounmpo still found a way to get things done, on a night he shot 1 of 7 from the line, fouled intentionally at the close.

With his 15th point, Antetokounmpo passed Larry Bird (21,791) for 40th place on the NBA's all-time scoring list. Antetokounmpo's ninth basket was the 8,000th of his career.

Along the way, Antetokounmpo recorded his 9,000th career rebound.

It also was the seventh consecutive game for Antetokounmpo with at least 12 rebounds, tying the seventh-longest streak with at least 12 in the Heat's 29 seasons. The record is 11 consecutive such games by Omer Yurtseven in 2021-22, followed by nine in a row by Hassan Whiteside in 2014-15.

3. Changing times: For the first time in his team's 10 games, Spoelstra shook up his team's starting lineup, with Larsson given the start in place of slumping Wiggins.

It was a dramatic move for more than just being the Heat's first lineup change of the season.

It was, in fact, the first time Wiggins has played as a reserve since the 2023-24 season with the Golden State Warriors, when he played 12 of his 71 games off the bench. That season was the only season he has played off the bench in his 13 seasons.

Wiggins again was uneven, closing with six points on 2-of-6 shooting in 31 mintues.

4. Larsson's chance: Larsson, by contrast, had been a staple as a starter at times over his three seasons, including 54 of his 70 appearances last season, while also starting eight times as a rookie in 2024-25.

The change, though, lasted all of 2:11, with Larsson forced to the bench with his second foul with 9:49 left in the opening period.

Otherwise, the other four starters remained Antetokounmpo, Adebayo, Thompson and Mitchell.

Larsson was up to five fouls 67 seconds into the fourth quarter, with fouls among the reasons extended starter's minutes had long been a question with the guard taken in the 2024 second round.

Larsson fouled out with 1:51 to play, closing with 11 points on 4-of-7 shooting in 22 minutes.

5. Onward: The Heat's five-game western swing, one that has had them on the road since Nov. 1, concludes Tuesday night against the Utah Jazz.

From there, it will be a five-game homestand, a challenging run that opens with a Friday-Saturday back-to-back against the Cleveland Cavaliers and Detroit Pistons and also includes games against the Houston Rockets and Oklahoma City Thunder.

#2614. DeMarvion Overshown had a bad moment and didn’t even play

Culture was a buzzword around Brian Schottenheimer. Over the summer and through training camp, many people noted the culture that the head coach was building for and through the Dallas Cowboys.

That, obviously, has felt a bit hollow as losses have piled up this season, but on Sunday, during a win, it was on full display in the worst possible way.

In case you have no idea what is happening here, allow me to explain:

The Cowboys were holding on to a 20-17 lead against Indianapolis

It looked like the Colts were about to score

Quinnen Williams forced a fumble from Jonathan Taylor of all people

Shemar James recovered it and took it to the Dallas 30-yard line

The video in question is the Cowboys defense celebrating. Why would they not? They just took the football away at a critical moment. Their hard work was worthy of jubilation.

But someone else enters the frame. You can see someone, as the official described to be in sweats while assessing the penalty, run into the celebration. That was DeMarvion Overshown and as he was not a part of the team for the game (he was inactive) he was assessed with an unsportsmanlike conduct penalty.

This was the case because the celebration was not happening on the sideline where Cowboys players and staffers happened to be. You can see in the video that the action is quite literally in the middle of the field. Somehow, some way, Overshown ran out that far into the field and backed the team up 15 yards as a result.

Quite simply, this cannot happen. Overshown has been around for a while and is regarded to be among the team's leaders. His inability to stay healthy is an issue, but he has always been seen as someone who at the very least is great from a leadership standpoint. This certainly was not that.

Consider that the Cowboys did wind up getting those 15 yards back right away, but they punted on the ensuing possession and the Colts went on to tie the game up before eventually taking the lead that Dallas took back once and for all. But maybe the lack of penalty yards could have helped Dallas put the game away sooner.

The point isn't to live in hypotheticals. The point is that Overshown directly impacted his team's ability to win and he did so while not even a part of the game.

Brian Schottenheimer has to take care of that kind of thing.

#2612. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2611. Celtics could be without key player vs. Bucks

MILWAUKEE — The Celtics could be without Mitchell Robinson on Sunday night when they face the Milwaukee Bucks. Robinson was a late addition to the injury report, listed as questionable with an illness earlier Sunday afternooon.

Robinson is coming off the best stretch of his season; he tallied a season-high 10 rebounds in 20 minutes against the Bucks on Wednesday, and followed that up with a 7-rebound, 6-point performance against the Charlotte Hornets on Friday.

If Robinson sits, it would likely mean more opportunity for Luka Garza, who is averaging 7.3 points and 3.7 rebounds in 13.2 minutes off the bench this season. Garza is shooting a career-best 54.2% from the three-point line.

Garza has seen increased opportunities with the double-big lineup, typically playing alongside Robinson. Garza said he hasn't played much double-big in the NBA, but he has experience playing the 4 from college and with the Bosnian national team.

"It just gives me another avenue to get on the floor, especially with the bigs that we have and the depth that we have," Garza said at Celtics shootaround on Sunday morning. "So for me, it's something I'm going to commit to and buy into trying to be the most effective at that position."

The Celtics' most popular starting lineup this season has been Derrick White, Jordan Walsh, Paul George, Jayson Tatum, and Neemias Queta. That group started Friday night in a 119-80 thrashing of the Hornets.

Neemias Queta, who came off the bench in the first five games of the season, has become a regular starter for Boston. Queta is averaging 9.1 points and 5.9 rebounds per game, while shooting 56.6% from the field.

The last time the Celtics and Bucks met was Wednesday, and the Celtics won 109-102 as Jayson Tatum got a rest game. Paul George led the way with 25 points, while Derrick White added 24 points. The Bucks rallied back from a 22-point deficit to take a fourth-quarter lead behind 25 points from Tyler Herro and 18 points from Jaime Jaquez Jr.

The Celtics enter the game with a 7-2 record; the Buckers are 1-7.

#2609. Warriors lose Will Richard indefinitely to injury

Warriors lose Will Richard indefinitely to injury appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Golden State Warriors have found their groove, as they have won seven consecutive games after losing their first three contests to begin the 2026-27 season. However, the Warriors will now have to contend with the loss of a valued rotation player in sophomore guard Will Richard.

According to Brett Siegel, ClutchPoints NBA insider, Richard is out indefinitely with a UCL tear in his left thumb. The Warriors guard sustained this injury during their 126-119 victory over the Orlando Magic in overtime.

Richard still played 12 minutes during that game and put up four points on 1-of-1 shooting from the field and 2-of-2 from the foul line. He's played in all 10 games thus far for the Warriors, averaging 5.1 points, 1.8 rebounds, and 1.0 assist in his 17.2 minutes per contest.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

The Warriors have embraced a next man up mentality all season long, and Richard's injury isn't about to make circumstances any different for Golden State. At the very least, a few more minutes per ballgame should be in store for the likes of De'Anthony Melton, Brandon Williams, Gary Payton II, and even Gui Santos.

Last season, Sacramento Kings forward Keegan Murray suffered a torn UCL in his left thumb as well. This put him on the shelf for the first 15 games of the 2025-26 season, and he missed nearly a month's worth of games.

Richard could be out for a similar period of time as well. One would think that the earliest he could make his return is in early December, but there is nothing concrete yet in that regard.

Whatever the case may be in terms of Richard's injury recovery and timeline, the Warriors will look to keep grinding away wins the way they've done to start the season. Stephen Curry has been on a tear, Yaxel Lendeborg has been better than expected, and their offense is humming — starting the season within the top five in points scored per 100 possessions.
